Gilles Deleuze's Difference and Repetition is at the center of his
philosophical works, not only chronologically but also Inethodo­
logically and in tenns of interpretation. This does not mean it is his
Inost ilnportant book. This depends on the problems and questions
driving any given reading. Political studies are Inore likely to focus
on the later collaborations with Felix Guattari.I Interpretations
more interested in art will be drawn first to Proust and Signs or
to the book on Francis Bacon, The Logic of Sensation.2 More pure
philosophical enquiry does not have to start with Difference and
Repetition, since The Logic of Sense is the better starting point for
the study of Deleuze's philosophies of language and of the event}
Close reflection on the history of philosophy or on Deleuze's
ontology need not elnphasize the difference book, since works
such as Nietzsche and Philosophy and The Fold: Leibniz and the
Baroque offer more cOlnprehensive accounts of Deleuze's version of
those authors and, for instance, his concepts of world or creation.
Nonetheless, as a reading goes deeper into any of these areas, exten­
sive reference to Difference and Repetition will prove necessary. It
is the keystone to Deleuze's philosophy, but as we shall see, even
the notion of such a secure foundation is challenged in the book.
I want to argue for the value and necessity of Deleuze's major book
around two questions. First, why is Difference and Repetition a great
work of philosophy, able to stand alongside Descartes' Discourse on
33


34
TAMES WILLIAMS
Method, Kant's Critique of Pure Reason, and Heidegger's Being and
Time? One answer to this first puzzle leads to lny second question.
These great works introduce novel and ground-breaking methods
into philosophical practice. This justifies not only their influ­
ence but also their status as revolutionary in content and effect.
What, therefore, is the new lnethod developed in Difference and
Repetition?
An answer to the question of lnethod has now begun to be sug­
gested among Deleuze scholars worldwide. The revolutionary
method behind Deleuze's philosophy is to be called "transcenden­
tal elnpiricisln." My ainl here will therefore be to draw together
the lnany strands of his book around a critical asseSSlnent of the
two clailns implied by this answer: transcendental empiricisln is
Deleuze's lnethodological innovation; it is a great lnethod worthy of
cOlnparison to the Cartesian method of doubt, to Kantian critique,
and to Heideggerian hermeneutics.
My guide through the first clailn will be the most ilnportant
piece of interpretation on Deleuze's work and on Difference and
Repetition, Anne Sauvagnargues' Deleuze: L'empirisme transcend­
anta1.4 The title explains my interest, but lnore significantly her
book is the lnost deep and broad interpretation of Deleuze's oeu­
vre currently available.5 It is a tour de force in Deleuze's tradition,
a lnanner of philosophizing inherited from his rnasters Gueroult,
Wahl, and Hyppolite, whOln he lnet very early on, in his last years
of lycee, and then worked with at the Sorbonne.6 This tradition is
the French close reading seeking to draw a work together around its
most potent conceptual and lnethodological innovations, yet always
following the principle of the greatest possible faithfulness to an
author's texts, to their place in the history of philosophy, and to the
logical consistency of an author's ideas.? It is an ilnmanent read­
ing following a thinker's concepts, argulnents, and references while
lninirnizing external presuppositions.
Yet Deleuze's influence goes well beyond this French acadelnic
practice with its renlarkable scholarly and intellectual values. The
French academy has strong parochial boundaries too, not only in
styles of critical method and analysis, but also in its sense of the
place of philosophy as a historical undertaking situated above other
disciplines, even as they infonn it. Such rigorous commitments
to place and internal form have the great advantage of building on


Difference and Repetition
3 5
classical wisdOln and practice. They in1pose disciplines forn1ed and
tested over tilne. They also, though, run the risk of falling behind
the tilnes into irrelevance or exercises designed to lnaintain a privi­
leged yet elnbattled position against a changing world. One of the
n1erits of Deleuze's work is its lnastery of disciplines but as expanded
through an exploration beyond disciplinary lin1its and through
deliberately risky experilnentation, for instance in Deleuze's love
of adventure in English and Alnerican literature, or in his taste for
those living life on the edge, such as Artaud or Nietzsche.s
This hybrid fonn explains the style of Difference and Repetition.
On the one hand, it is his French long doctoral thesis, his doctorat
d'etat, the cuhnination of lnany years of strict acadelnic training.
As such it involves extended scholarly readings of lnany lnajor his­
torical and contemporary philosophical figures around an original
thesis about difference and repetition. On the other hand, it is a
lnajor work of philosophical invention and as such it also intro­
duces a novel style and accOlnpanying n10des of thought and ideas
into philosophy.
Both aspects contribute to the fearsOlne difficulty of the book,
which, like lnany revolutionary works, lnust not be dislnissed with
hasty accusations of obscurity or n1ystification. The ideas set out
in Difference and Repetition lnake heavy dernands on the reader
because they are radically new. They therefore enforce a break
with common expectations, language, and values. This break is an
intrinsic part of the book's argulnents. For eXaInple, Deleuze sug­
gests a revolutionary transformation of all of tiIne around the idea
of a caesura or split in tiIne at the introduction of the new: "[The
image of a unique and fonnidable event adequate to the whole of
tilne] exists itself in torn form, in two unequal portions; and yet
therefore it draws together the asselnbly of tin1e" (DR 89, transla­
tion modified). An event breaks tilne in two unequal parts: the past
before the event and the future ahead of it. Yet we decode the past
and the future through this event, which is therefore adequate to
the whole of tilne. The split in tiIne is therefore also its assembly.9
It is a duty for interpreters and teachers to alleviate the delnand
of Deleuze's work, but this should not be confused with the thought
that the ideas have easier fonns than the ones he sets out. Each siln­
plifying reading, like this one here, loses son1ething of the richness
and cOlnplexity of Deleuze's text. So though sections of this essay


36
TAME S WILLIAMS
will follow Anne Sauvagnargues and other interpreters in tracing
the definition of the apparently paradoxical lnethod of transcenden­
tal elnpiricisln, further sections will consider critical points against
this elnerging orthodoxy.
The conjunct of "transcendental" and "empiricisln" is historically
incongruous and analytically paradoxical because it cOlnbines two
historical n10velnents apparently at odds with each other. 10 It links
Hun1e's philosophical elnpiricisln and its scientific and philosoph­
ical heirs with the later Kantian critique. It draws out the contra­
diction of a philosophy seeking to bring no subjective and doglnatic
presuppositions to enquiry with a philosophy also attelnpting to
avoid dogmatisln but through the deduction of the necessary condi­
tions for any appearance in intuition.II
For radical elnpiricisln, to hold to the existence of such condi­
tions prior to experilnent is doglnatic. Setting down one of the Inain
tenets of this elnpiricism, HUlne therefore states that all ideas Inust
follow frOln ilnpressions, frOln experience.I2 However, for transcen ..
dental critique, to fail to take account of the a priori conditions for
such iInpressions is to Inisunderstand the nature of our faculties
of thought and sensibility. Every appearance in intuition requires
a synthesis of time which therefore allows us to deduce universal
categories for any appearance.
FrOln his earliest book on HUlne, Deleuze's interest in these
debates has been practical rather than abstractly theoretical. We
elnploy faculties in all our everyday activities, for instance, in our
connected feelings around a send button and our ilnpulses towards
pushing it, as our Inuscles tense in expectation and dread. They are
also used Inore intellectually and introspectively, when seeking to
understand our thoughts about the action of pressing, the signifi­
cance of doing so, and the rightness, wrongness, beauty, and ugli­
ness of our actions. Yet these faculties are the locus for ongoing
and thorny questions about the priority of universal conditions or of
elnpirical facts, captured by discussions around Deleuze's transcen­
dental empiricisln.
Are there universal conditions for appearances in thought and
sensation? Are we subject to a universal legislation of our facul­
ties? Or is each faculty discovered through tentative, varied, and
probabilistic experiment? Is the encountered world subject to condi­
tions holding for any possible experience - the SaIne for all? Or is


Difference and Repetition
37
it radically pluralistic and always open to disproval by new facts,
against all theoretical underpinning? Are the natural sciences the

dental elnpiricisln is as an atten1pt to avoid the oppositions between
these options. It seeks to be transcendental and elnpirical but n1ust
therefore redefine both tenns away frorn their contradiction of each
another.
My critical relnarks on transcendental elnpiricisn1 will therefore
span two thoughts. Is transcendental empiricisln a viable candidate
for a method Ineant to Inark a great philosophy? Even if it is, what
are the restrictions ilnposed on Deleuze's philosophy in giving it

the features of Difference and Repetition that are either downplayed

DIFFERENCE IN ITSELF AND REPETITION FOR ITSELF
I just can't do it
Cowmd . . .
Difference and Repetition is distilled frorn Inany Inajor and minor
Inovelnents in the history of philosophy. The variety and nlllnber of
thinkers covered in the book are demonstrated by its eclectic index
of nalnes and subjects. Each entry is listed with a short comlnent
by Deleuze explaining its place in the new versions of ideas of diffe­
rence and of repetition he argues for. The overall thesis is that we
n1ust think of difference "in itself," as pure difference defined not
in relation to identity, in accordance to the negation of an identity
(not-X), or according to a difference between two identities (X differ­
ent frorn Y). Instead, difference is to be an ideal or virtual potential
for the transfonnation of identities (the differing between X and Y).
This pure difference does not have fixed identity. It is an ongoing
variation of relations, rather than any given object, substance, or
quality.


38
TAMES WILLIAMS
For exanlple, we could think of this virtual potential in hUInan
tenns as a reserve of shifting relations of pliable nloods and passions
we eInbody at different degrees of intensity. For instance, when we
Inock another's incapacities, we force potentials of rage, humili­
ation, revenge, and sadness on to theln. These are then expressed in
a singular fonn. The potentials are pure differentials of relations of
passions because they can be expressed differently in Inany individ­
uals. The differentials transfonn those individuals or reveal theln
to be processes of individuation (how could I know it would hurt
you so nluchn. According to Deleuze's definitions of difference and
repetition, and the accOlnpanying definitions of the virtual and the
actual, all passions relnain as pure virtual potential until they are
expressed in actual processes of becOlning at different degrees of
intensity.
This potential is actualized in series of repetitions and deter­
Inined by theln. Thus repetition is defined as a differential vari­
ation across series. So repetition too is defined against identity and
representation as repetition "for itself." This is not the repetition
of another saIne thing (not the repetition of X, X, X . . .  J but the vari­
ation along differences (X, X', X" . . .  J. We could, for eXaInple, think
of repetition as a variation along a series of shades of a color. For
Deleuze there is repetition when there is difference in the shades
resisting definition according to a fixed identity.
A puzzling thought to hold, but an ilnportant one for Deleuze,
is that there can never be a repetition of the saIne thing. This is
because there is always a novel series of differences each time a
"saIne" thing is repeated. Sameness is then illusion because diffe­
rence is the condition for repetition. We never encounter the same
color. These statelnents could not come £rOIn radical eInpiricism,
given the categorical assertion pertaining to a matter of fact. It is a
transcendental claim about necessary conditions. It is also an asser­
tion at odds with even the possibility of the experimental falsifi­
cation of the statelnent about difference in any repetition. There is
hence a deep challenge to the comlnitlnent to truth as deternlined
exclusively and probabilistic ally by the eInpirical sciences.I}
For Deleuze the real always presupposes difference in itself, and
when we define reality under the opposing condition of SaIneness
we foster an illusion. One of the aims of Difference and Repetition
is to criticize clailns which support the illusion: "It is because


DiffeTence and Repetition
39
nothing is equal, because everything bathes in its difference, in its
disseinblance and its inequality, even with itself, that everything
returns" (DR 243). This stateinent reflects the great ilnportance of
Nietzsche's doctrine of eternal return for DiffeTence and Repetition:
only difference returns and never the SaIne.
Difference in repetition explains why the book naines difference
AND repetition. For Deleuze, there is no difference without series of
repetitions; and no series without difference. Another difficult clailn
is that these series of repetitions occur in pairs, where difference is
released across series (for instance frOln the series of pure virtual
passions to the series of actual expressions at given intensities). The
rage released in the victiln following a hUlniliating taunt runs with
a twin in the increased erotic intensity felt by the tormentor. Paired
differences explain the relation between two series as processes of
transformation. It also explains why difference appears in a series
at all. This is because no series of repetitions is unique. It is in a
dynainic relation to all other actual and virtual series.
These ideas about series, structures, and differences are devel­
oped Inost fully in The Logic of Sense. This explains the cOlnplex
interaction between the two near-conteinporary books. Where
DiffeTence and Repetition sets out Deleuze's ontology of difference
in repetition, The Logic of Sense shows their structural processes
in moral and linguistic practice. Note the importance of the con .
cept of explanation in understanding Deleuze, where to explain is
to describe consistent connections within a systenl, as a response to
apparent contradictions or lacunae in a part of the systeln. Deleuze
is not only creator of a novel inethodology but also of an explanatory
systein. The key critical question is whether the explanatory power
of the system as a multiple series of explanations and connections is
attenuated by the ilnposition of the restricted logic of transcenden­
tal conditions and terms.
DELEUZE'S SOURCES

Even my dark precursoTsz
The index of naInes at the end of DiffeTence and Repetition divides
thinkers according to their positive and negative stand with respect


40
TAME S WILLIAMS
to Deleuze's novel definitions of difference and repetition. Aristotle
is listed for ontological and logical difference, but where they are
subordinated to the identity of species and genera. Bergson is there
for repetition and n1eInory, as well as biological difference, repeti­
tion in physics, and the iInportant concept of intensity (difference
is differentiated by Deleuze according to differences of intensity,
such as the intensity of light in shades of colors). Freud earns a place
thanks to his work on repetition and the unconscious. While Hegel,
like Aristotle, is nalned for a logic and ontology of difference as
negation, opposition, and contradiction.I4 These brusque distinc­
tions between the saved and the dmnned are a perplexing facet of
Deleuze's writing. IS
This abruptness need not be seen as a flaw. It is a sign of two
ilnportant aspects of his philosophy and live practice. Deleuze
values the fraught and passionate relation of Inaster to apprentice.
He also values hUInor, above what he sees as rOlnantic and ultim­
ately nihilistic subject-centered irony (due to the way irony reflects
back on to the privileged position of the ironist, which itself proves
to be hollow).I6 Often, Deleuze's tough judgmental statements are
hUInorous lessons for apprentices, Ineant to strike back later filtered
by the unconscious (how stupid of me to have sought and found a
final judgment on Hegel . . .  ). A fascinating probieln is then how far
we should take account of humor when reading Deleuze and how
far hlllnor and other affects are designed to be seeded in our uncon­
scious for later effect by a playful master: killing our stupid serious­
ness with stealthy blows to the unconscious.
To develop a sense of these problems about interpretation, humor,
provocation, and aInbivalence in Difference and Repetition, we can
look at the place of error in the work. It Inight seem that Deleuze's
critique is ailned at the error of adopting a dogmatic ilnage of
thought, that is, of received ideas and of COlnInon sense. Deleuze,
though, writes of the "probleln" of stupidity. Ideal probielns have
a very inlportant role in his philosophy. They generate questions
which seek to respond to the probieln but instead transform it and
bring it into actual worlds around questions of Who? Why? How?
The questions can be answered, but the problem will recur in a
different form. An ideal probieln is then an irresolvable driver of
creative responses in individual situations. This is sOlnething like
the dialogical search in the Socratic Inethod of Elenchus, taking


Difference and Repetition
4I
US on an endless philosophical practice around the discovery of
stupidity but not with an arrival at secure truths. These would be
but another stupidity for Deleuze, which is why I treated even the
foundational role of Difference and Repetition for his work with
caution.
In Difference and Repetition he draws Socrates towards the soph­
ists and Nietzsche and away frOln Plato. Like Socrates he wants to
show how error is at its most dangerous when we think we have
done with it or are iInmune frOln it:
[B]ut the sophist does not distinguish himself from Socrates, and thereby
calls into question the legitimacy of such a distinction. Twilight of the
Icons. Is this not to designate the point where the identity of the model and
the resemblance of the copy are errors, where the same and the similar are
born from the function of the simulacrum? (DR I 28, translation modified)
There is a critique of judglnent in accordance with external images,
Inodels, and standards in Difference and Repetition. They are false
idols present in philosophy when it gives itself timeless ilnages and
postulates of what constitutes true and good thinking.
The third chapter of the book charts eight postulates defining
this image of thought. These can be sumlned up as postulates of
the natural good will of the thinker and rectitude of thought; of
a shared COlnlnon sense; of a faculty of recognition regulating all
others according to an object; of representation as detennining the
object; of error as the negative of thought; of designation as deter­
Inining the truth of a proposition; of solutions as resolving prob­
lelns; and of knowledge as the result of thought, as opposed to an
endless apprenticeship to thought (DR I67). Accordingly, thought is
defined as inherently good and as well directed when it proceeds as
a COlnlnon sense which recognizes its proper objects.
These objects must in principle be representable and are desig­
nated by true propositions. When exercised properly thought avoids
error and solves problelns, arriving thereby at knowledge. For
Deleuze these propositions of thought are to be criticized. They are
guided by false idols because each one serves to deny the role of
difference and of repetition in thinking and in the constitution of
the real. These images and idols will return even in a philosophy
designed to be critical of theln. So philosophy Inust think upon the
conditions of possibility of error, rather than towards its eventual

TAME S WILLIAMS
elilllination or about a pure thought free of it: "It would have been
enough for philosophy to adopt this problelll with its own llleans
and with necessary lllodesty. To consider stupidity not to be that
of others, but rather the object of a properly transcendental ques­
tion: How is stupidity (and not error) possible?" (DR 1 5 1, translation
lllodified). Error supposes an opposite: error-free truth. For Deleuze
truth and error are instead intertwined due to the ever-present pos­
sibility of stupidity. This arises frOlll the way in which individual
thought has to construct itself on an obscure and indistinct ground
of unconscious drives and processes of becOllling.
The unavoidable relation of thought to error is replicated in

Ie ability is shown through concepts such as dark precursor: "We
call the dark precursor disparate, that difference in itself, at the
second degree, putting heterogeneous or disparate series thelllselves
into relation" (DR 1 20, translation lllodified). All things are proc­
esses of individuation, individual becOllling, which are thelTIselves
series of repetitions cOlllnlunicating through dark precursors, that
is, through elelllents crossing frOlll one series to another though rec­
ognized in neither: precursors of change the systelll cannot foresee
or represent.
Deleuze owes lllany of his ideas on unconscious processes not
to Freudian psychoanalysis, but to Nietzsche's will to power. In
the index to Diffel'ence and Repetition Nietzsche receives the high
praise of a citation for the ontology of difference and repetition,
whereas Plato is listed for the ontology and logic of difference. It
is illlportant to note Deleuze's special use of logic, here, as prac­
tical art of the systelllatization and selection of different processes
according to difference and repetition, rather than silllply fonllal


Difference and Repetition
43
rules of implication. Duns Scotus is included due to the import­
ance of his work on ilnlnanence for Deleuze. Schelling provides the
opportunity to cover the relation between difference and power, but
also the ideas of ground, relnoval of ground, founding, and founda­
tion in Difference and Repetition (as we have just seen in relation
to error and stupidity). Kierkegaard is accorded a place for repetition
and difference in positive senses related to freedom, though this is
Initigated by the necessity of Kierkegaard's leap of faith. Heidegger,
a thinker in the background of many parts of the book, is cited for
his ideas of ontological difference close to Deleuze's.1S Levi-Strauss
and the distinction between static and dynmnic repetition testify
to Deleuze's continued interest in transforming rather than deny­
ing structuralisln, where static repetition in a system in balance
does not allow for the movelnent afforded by repetition in dynamic
systems.
Deleuze's contelnporaries also play key roles, notably Lacan
around the death drive and Klossowski for his interpretation of
Nietzsche's eternal return. Michel Foucault, Deleuze's friend,
is present because of his critique of difference, reselnblance, and
identity through a positive use of difference and repetition in the
idea of the simulacruln (a thing that is always becOlning what it
is not and whose identity at any given point cannot be referred to
an original).19 Derrida allows Deleuze to connect repetition and the
unconscious to language and to the work of art.20 Whereas Milner,
Leclaire, Ricoeur, and Miller all delnonstrate Deleuze's work on
repetition in psychoanalysis, an interest that becomes more oppos­
itional in the later works with Guattari. Althusser (and Balibar,
Establet, Macherey, and Ranciere) extend Deleuze's quite short ref­
erence to Marx's 18th Brulnaire.
This extensive list of philosophical references is testimony to
Deleuze's astonishingly productive engagelnent with philosophy,
past and present.2I It is a distortion, though, because to refer only
to philosophers is to Iniss four all-important sources for Deleuze:
literature, biology, physics, and mathematics. The last three are
perhaps the least well known, partly due to the uptake of French
thought prilnarily in literary and critical theory disciplines outside
France. Many English-language interpreters of Deleuze are based
in literature or politics departments, rather than in the sciences or
philosoph y.


44
TAME S WILLIAMS
However, the iinportance of the sciences for the understanding and
influence of Deleuze will grow once more interpreters register the
function played by differential calculus, dynainic systeIns, and the
biology of individuals in Deleuze's account of the detennination of
difference and genesis. In Inatheinatics, this is developed in relation
to singularities in the work of Wronski, Abel, Carnot, and Bordas­
Dernoulin. However, it is arguable that questions of structure and
probleins in nUlnber theory and Ineta-Inatheinatics are equally influ­
ential through the works of Plato, Fevrier, Ghyka, Heyting, Russell,
and, above all, Lautlnan. In biology the Inajor figure is Siinondon,
for his work on individuation, set critically alongside other works by
Lalande, Osborn, Dalcq, and Geoffroy Saint-Hilaire.22
The index and text of Difference and Repetition also testify to the
iinportance of novelists and poets and of the literary iinagination for
Deleuze. It is tempting to cite Blanchot as the Inajor figure here, due
to his influence on Deleuze's understanding of literary theory and his
ideas about death. However, other writers are equally iinportant, not­
ably Borges, GOlnbrowicz, Artaud, Proust, Robbe-Grillet, Tournier,
Butor, Sollers, and Joyce, as well as literary philosophers such as
peguy. Literature is not included in the book silnply as a source of
examples or as a topic of study. It is thought itself, as the imaginary,
that Deleuze values and turns to in literature; for instance, when
Borges allows hiin to explain how all worlds subsist beyond the selec­
tion of a particular one according to a dice throw, itself driven by a
death drive (DR II5-16) or when Gombrowicz allows him to deter­
Inine the relation between ideas of cosmos and chaos.23 According to
Deleuze's metaphysics, it is an illusion to hold the common-sense
idea that we can either have the real or fiction, as if they are clearly
delineated and to be valued differently. For him, the virtual is real
and it is accessed best through expressive signs encountered in art
and literature. This virtual and the expressive "fictions" cominuni­
cating with it are Inore real than any set of possible scenes traced on
a well-identified and well-represented actual world.
It would be easy to conclude from the Inixture of authors, topics,
and disciplines covered in Difference and Repetition that the book
cannot take in all of theIn, perhaps any, with a high degree of depth
and seriousness. The book would then be dilettante: an essay in
spurious and self-indulgent cOlnmentary. That this is not the case is
due to the fit of style, critical and creative Inodes of thought, and set


Difference and Repetition
45
of ideas achieved by Deleuze. These can be sun1Ined up through the
concepts of variations of speed, Inultiplication of lnodes of thought,
and consistency within novelty of ideas. Rhythn1 is very ilnport­
ant for Deleuze. He lneans a variation of different speeds such that
each one enhances and expresses the others while undermining
any clailns to self-sufficiency or supreinacy. Thought is about great
speed in slowness and great reach in intense concentration or con­
telnplation. His book includes intricate tangles of long and slow pas­
sages of critical exposition with lnuch shorter, faster, and condensed
definitions and insights.
To follow the speed it is often ilnportant to observe its effects over
the critical and expository mOlnents, for instance, where Deleuze's
definition of difference allows for a critical reflection on the concept
of being in Aristotle (DR 30-35). Or when the whole of Chapter 3
of the book, on the ilnage of thought, unfolds and explicates how
error and illusions are a necessary side of any lnode of thought, yet
one we should seek to avoid through the critique of representation:
"[The eight postulates of the image of thought] crush thought under
the iinage of the SaIne and of the Similar in representation. This
iinage is the deepest betrayal of what thought signifies, which is to
ally the two powers of difference and repetition, of philosophical
commencelnent and recomlnencement" (DR r67, translation modi­
fied). In order to avoid this crushing way of thinking, Deleuze drops
lines of escape within each critical reading.
To work with these powers of escape and flight it is necessary to
multiply modes of thought and create novel concepts, in order to
free thought frOln the dOlninance of any one of thein. This explains
the alternation of poetic expression, conceptual clarification, philo­
sophical interpretation, the glossing and transformation of scientific
and lnathelnatical ideas, and the borrowing frOln all of the arts. This
accounts for why dramatization is an essential idea for Difference
and Repetition. Yet even after taking account of this rhythin of styles
and Inodes the question relnains: how do all these different speeds
and topics cohere? To use one of the ideas from the book, how do they
create a cosmos? Given Deleuze's cOlnlnitment to difference and
repetition against forms of established yet illusory order, this must
be a COSlnos teetering perpetually on the edge of chaos, yet a cos­
mos nonetheless. The answer is that whichever part of the COSlnos we
select Inust dramatize or replay all the others in its own way.


46
TAME S WILLIAMS
Deleuze's book and his style do not confonn to the idea that
there should be one proper style or that a book should set out the
steps of an argulnent without repetition or redundancy. On the
contrary, his work repays interpretations cOlnbining aleatory
selections with jUlnps and connections across sections, topics, and
ideas. Does it mean it lacks the seriousness, tenninological econ­
Olny, ontological parsilnony, and logical consistency expected of
philosophy? No. It involves a rejection of all of these, if they are
taken as settled and natural, because they fail their own tests once
exclusions, conceahnents, and Olnissions are revealed. One of the
greatest strengths of Deleuze's apparent eclecticism and prepared­
ness to throw subjects and ideas together is to dramatize the errors
and illusions ilnplied by clailns to their independence, purity, or
natural hierarchies.
HISTORICAL SELECTIONS AND THE PROBLEM OF
INTERPRETATION
What are you afmid of;
It is never "WhaU" it is " Why!", " Who!" and "Howā"
A further point to lnake when considering Difference and Repetition
as the keystone of a new Inethod of transcendental empiricism is
about selection among its historical influences and antecedents,
given the range and style of Deleuze's work. The book was first pub­
lished in French in I968 by the pre-eminent acadelnic press Presses
Universitaires de France in a collection edited by Jean Hyppolite. It
is the saIne collection in which Deleuze published his first book,
on HUlne, in I953. Difference and Repetition is a turning point in
Deleuze's published works, since it is the first not written as an
essay on another philosopher, with the exception of the books on
novelists Proust and Sacher-Masoch (though arguably they are also
read as philosophers of, respectively, sensation and subjection under
the guises of Eros and the sensual apprenticeship to signs). All the
books prior to Difference and Repetition were single-subject works,
on Hume (I953), Nietzsche (I962), Kant (I963), Proust (I964), Bergson
(I966), and Sacher·,Masoch (I967). Each of the philosophers studied in
Deleuze's earlier works appears again in Difference and Repetition,
but each time in a lnore restricted and precise role.


Difference and Repetition
47
It is important, though, to note Deleuze's early lessons and essays,
alongside his published books. A gifted teacher, Deleuze was a Inas­
ter of dense yet suggestive and far-sighted thought when speaking
and when writing. His recorded lessons and essays show the devel­
opment of his ideas and the extraordinary range of interests leading
to Difference and Repetition. Ainong these, the Inost ilnportant for
the understanding of the later In aster-work Inight be the essay "The
Method of DraInatization.// It is a difficult yet rewarding paper, deIn­
onstrating Deleuze's thinking about the original nature of his phil­
osophy as method but also as conceptual and practical innovation.
This line of thought continued after Difference and Repetition and
attained a further level of conceptual clarity in the book co-written
with Guattari, What is Philosophy? It is tempting therefore to inter­
pret the earlier book through the more clear distinctions of the later,
for instance around art, philosophy, and science, or through the con­
cepts of plane of iininanence, geo-philosophy, and conceptual per­
sonae. This is not an approach I favor, because the later book adopts
negation and definitions according to conceptual identity for polem­
ical reasons around conflicts within philosophicai inovements and
with the sciences. These characteristics Inake it a more accessible
but philosophically less innovative book.
The profound influence of Deleuze's encounters with Hlune,
Nietzsche, Kant, and Bergson mean Difference and Repetition
enters into a critical and creative dialogue with them. There are
three more to add to the list determined by Deleuze's earlier works:
Plato, Leibniz, and Spinoza. The latter was given a book in par­
allel to Difference and Repetition, Spinoza and the Problem of
Expression (1969). Leibniz rernained unfinished business until The
Fold: Leibniz and the Baroque (1988). Plato is discussed at length
in the appendices to The Logic of Sense but, like Marx, Deleuze's
definitive encounter with him is lacking. There is a plausible case
to be rnade for the many significant references to Plato in Difference
and Repetition to constitute Deleuze's final view on his "reversal of
Platonism.//24 It is instructive to remark upon Deleuze's references
to Plato, alongside Nietzsche, in the closing passages of two of the
main chapters of his book and at the heart of two others. Though
transcendental ernpiricisin places Kant at the heart of a filial dispute
with Deleuze, another interpretation could argue for the irnport­
ance of his creation of a fearsome hybrid of Nietzsche and Plato.


48
TAMES WILLIAMS
The setting within the history of philosophy is problelnatic,
therefore, since each interpretation of Difference and Repetition
and each account of his Inethod ilnplies a selection within his fore­
runners. More significantly, each of these readings is also a selec­
tion within possible versions detennined by Deleuze's Hume, or
Deleuze's Bergson, or his Nietzsche, or Plato. However, as soon as
Deleuze's n1ethod is defined as transcendental elnpiricisln, Kant
takes on the central role. This consequence is not without its dif­
ficulties, since as Sauvagnargues delnonstrates, although Deleuze
owes his dedication to transcendental critique to Kant, this is at the
cost of a critical reading. In giving one of her Inany definitions of the
Inethod of transcendental empiricisln, Sauvagnargues stresses both
the debt to and departure frOln Kant: "Transcendental elnpiricisln
is defined thus: to recast transcendental critique while expurgating
its confonnisln; to dissolve Kantian idealisln in a renewed eITlpiri­
cisn1."25 According to this definition, the point of the new Inethod
is to preserve critique based on the transcendental conditions of the
given, while avoiding Kant's fonnal presuppositions.
If we accept Sauvagnargues' account, Kant relnains an idealist
through these presuppositions because they commit hiln to the ideal
fonn of any possible experience, which in turn sets lilnits (categor­
ies) for the given.26 Instead, Deleuze's elnpiricisln is to be radically
experilnental in seeking to be struck by the given by going beyond
the lilnits set by an orthodoxy.27 The given is therefore not SOlne­
thing appearing within a general pure form of representation and in
the identity of the appearance, but rather an event occurring when
such fonns break down: "As experience, as actualized perfonnance,
according to its proper Inode, art realizes that which a philosophy fas­
cinated by identity and unity has such trouble achieving: a critique
of representation, condition for a transcendental elnpiricisln."28
This language of revolutionary purification is therefore not mere
rhetoric. Transcendental elnpiricisln is a reaction against a per­
ceived philosophical confonnisITl which is literally a cornlnitment
to fonnal presuppositions but also, socially and politically, a COln­
Initlnent to established fonns set by COlnlnon sense and "cliche."29
Expurgation is to be attained through an experiInental lnethod where
experiment is taken in the strong sense of adventure and creativity
outside the boundaries of established knowledge and against a true
and "proper" ilnage of thought.3° Here, the experiInental is extended
beyond its association with the elnpirical sciences and into artistic


Difference and Repetition
49
and aesthetic creativity: "Transcendental elnpiricisln is therefore
presented as a true transcendental aesthetic, silnultaneously a sci­
ence of the sensible and a philosophy of art, or, as Deleuze will say
in I98I: a logic - of sensation."31
This extension is guided by other thinkers frOln Deleuze's early
books: "Hlune and Nietzsche, Proust, Bergson and Spinoza are the
successive interlocutors of such a transfonnation of Kantian cri­
tique."p The selection of Kant as the n1ain protagonist in the devel-
0plnent of Deleuze's thought and Inethod is not therefore done
through the exclusion of other thinkers, but rather occurs through
an ordering of theln in tenns of priority and function. According to
Sauvagnargues' cOlnplex and authoritative definition of transcen­
dental elnpiricisln, Kant becOlnes the central figure in Deleuze's
philosophy: both saint and sinner. Other thinkers then occupy roles
in relation to this struggle with the Kantian legacy.
This in turn shows some of the risks in interpreting Difference
and Repetition through its historical relations in philosophy but also
in relation to the rest of Deleuze's oeuvre. The dialogue entertained
with the tradition has Inany voices and the voice selected as dOlnin­
ant then conditions not only the place of all the others, but also our
understanding of the Inain tenns of the book. There is a different
Difference and Repetition, when Nietzsche, Bergson, Plato, or Hume
is taken as the Inain influence or opponent. There is also therefore the
potential for different accounts of Deleuze's Inethod. I will go on to
show smne of these possible readings while following Sauvagnargues'
analysis Inore closely in relation to the subject and to time.
If we take Plato as the Inain inheritance and opposition for
Deleuze, then the notions of condition and the concept of transcen­
dental take a lesser role in his philosophy. This does not Inean these
notions disappear. Instead, Deleuze transfonns Platonic idealism
through a reversal where Plato's Ideas switch into pure becOlning
and intensities. This Ineans the Idea no longer fulfills the role of
timeless anchor and test for poor copies and false pretenders, such
as the Idea of virtue against which virtuous citizens are found want­
ing. Instead, the Idea generates difference and dissimilarity, render­
ing true copies obsolete and legitimate pretenders illegal:
Among Plato's most strange pages, manifesting the anti-Platonism at the
heart of Platonism, there are those suggesting that difference, the dissimi­
lar, the unequal, in short, becOlning could not just be faults affecting the


5 0
TAMES WILLIAMS
copy, as a cost of its secondary quality, a counterpart to its resemblance,
but would themselves be lllodels, terrible models of the pseudo where the
power of the false is developed. (DR 128, translation modified)
In this passage, Deleuze is discussing six of the lnost important
thelnes of Difference and Repetition. First, difference is to be seen
as prilnary and resistant to representation and to identification.
Difference is dissimilar in itself and hence ilnpossible to represent.
Difference is always unequal; it cannot be equated to its identity.
Second, difference is change. It is always in a process of becom­
ing. Third, difference is generative. It forces things to becOlne dif­
ferent, thereby lnaking theln different to what they are. The being
of a thing - past or present or future thereby becOlnes a falsehood.
Fourth, then, everything is a lnodel, not a copy of an original but
rather a silnulacrum. So - fifth and sixth thelnes - such silnulacra
exist in ever-changing series, not regulated according to the repeti­
tion of the sarne, but rather by the creation of difference through the
repetition of becorning.
Deleuze allies this reversal of Platonism not to anything found
in Kant, but rather to Nietzsche's model of eternal return. Plato
operates according to a circle based on the truth and identity of
the Idea (over time all new false pretenders to virtue are found
out by the recurrence of the true Idea). This circle is replaced by
Nietzsche's eternal return, according to which only difference
returns and never the same. The Kantian focus on the given and
on its conditions then becOlnes a moment in a wider set of meta­
physical processes, such as eternal return. The process is very
hard to interpret in post-Kantian transcendental terms because
the return of difference cannot be deduced but is instead posited
speculatively. The return is the only process that relnains the
same: lilt is not the SaIne that returns. It is not the Similar that
returns. The SaIne is the return of that which returns, that is of
the Different. The Silnilar is the return of that which returns, that
is of the Dissilnilar" (DR 384). In this passage, as elsewhere in
the book, the use of capitals indicates the idea rather than any
particular manifestation. Taken on this Nietzschean and Platonic
basis, Deleuze's method becomes speculative. It is still elnpir­
ical in Sauvagnargues' sense. Now, though, it is so right down to


DiffeTence and Repetition
5 1
Deleuze's n1ethod itself, which instead of being characterized once
and for all as transcendental idealisln, becomes a succession of
creative speculative systelns.
No doubt this has the disadvantage of losing the self-critical and
anti-dogn1atic rigor of radical critique. It has a double gain, though,
of releasing the concept of experilnent frOln its post-Kantian condi­
tions and of allowing interpretations of Deleuze and of his work
with Guattari as a series of Inetaphysical experilnents, rather than
a single line on transcendental elnpiricism, from its birth pangs in
Deleuze's historical studies to its refined apogee in his last essay,
"hnlnanence: A Life . . .  " There is still place for Kantian critique,
for instance where Deleuze introduces Kant's idea of a transcen­
dental illusion for types of thought after Plato, where representa­
tion takes over frOln the copy and the Inodel: "Representation is the
place of transcendental illusion. This illusion has Inany fonns, four
interpenetrated fonns, corresponding particularly to thought, to the
sensible, to the Idea and to being" (DR 265, translation modified).
Against the ubiquitous application of transcendental empiricislTl
to Deleuze's work, the transition from Platonic copy to Aristotelian
representation shows each critical method to have a well-defined
role: "A slippage occurred frOln the Platonic world to the world of
representation (which is why, there too, we could present Plato at
the origin, at the crossroads of a decision)." Every method, every
systeln, and hence every critical Inove is the result not of a tran­
scendental deduction regarding legitilnate conditions, but rather of
a dice throw at a crossing. They are selections rather than deduc··
tions. For Deleuze, a transcendental illusion, for instance regarding
thought, involves the ilnposition of illusory figures on a process.
These figures deny the essence of the process while clailning to
elnbody it. In continuing to interpret Deleuze in the transcendental
line we release the full potential of this critique and renew the line
of elnpiricisln drawn frOln HUlne. The probleln relnains, though,
of whether this allows the full force of Deleuze's other methods to
COlne through, notably, though not exclusively, his praglnatisln and
his creative and speculative legacies.

The Deleuzian reversal
Platonism
Of the lnany books that Deleuze devoted to the history of
philosophy, none focuses on ancient philosophy. And yet, one could
argue that few thinkers of the twentieth century have engaged
with ancient philosophy, and drawn frOln it, as much and as sys­
tematically as Deleuze. Naturally, one could think of Heidegger as
another twentieth-century philosopher whose thought was shaped
through his confrontation with Greek philosophy. But where
Heidegger eventually mobilizes the Presocratics against Plato and
Aristotle, and seeks to extract froIn theln possibilities of thought
beyond the "closure" of metaphysics, Deleuze doesn't envisage
the history of philosophy in the same terms, and simply refuses
to identify what he calls "the lnetaphysics of representation," or
"PlatonisIn," with metaphysics as such. That being said, it would
be difficult to ignore that his entire philosophy - up until Logic
of Sense at least
unfolds under the Nietzschean injunction to
reverse, overturn, and overCOlne Platonisln, stressing all the while
the need to construct another "image of thought." Most striking,
perhaps, is the extent to which his effort to produce another iInage
of thought requires a new thought of the image, one that he finds
already at work in Greek philosophy itself. My goal, here, is three­
fold: to return to the source of Platonism by following the thread
of the image, and show the extent to which philosophy is, from the
start, and irreducibly, a matter of ethics, aesthetics, and politics;
to reveal the extent to which Deleuze saw resources to overturn
Platonisln in ancient, and even Platonic, philosophy itself; to indi­
cate the lnanner in which, drawing on those resources, Deleuze
develops an anti-Platonism based on the concepts of "difference"
and "repetition."

How, exactly, does Plato Inanage to avoid anarchy and "exorcise

What are the constitutive features of the Idea? How does it world
By contrast with the iInage, which is only an iInage, that is, second­

the only differences that can be recognized, in every sense of the

HOW TO OVERTURN PLATONISM?
Having established the origin and fundaIllental meaning of
Platonism, we need to ask about its possible reversal: what can it
possibly Illean, and how can it be brought about? How can one dis­
tinguish between appearances without subordinating thelll frOlll
the start to the self-sameness of Ideas, and introducing the prob­
lematic of imitation and participation? How can one select with­

of view within the immanent plane of appearances? How, in other
words, can one think without Ideas, and overturn the doxic irnage
of thought? If, as I have tried to show, the Platonic Idea is a polit­
ical tool, a lTlOral concept, and an aesthetic ideal, the question is
one of knowing what ilnage of thought, and what concepts, can be
developed as a genuine alternative. Those questions take us to the
very heart of the Deleuzian project and intirnate the extraordinar­
ily difficult task that awaits those who wish to create an image of
thought, and a philosophy, devoid of idealislll and transcendence. It
cannot be a question, here, of providing a detailed account of such a
project. Nor can it be a question of reviewing the various attempts
that, according to Deleuze hilTlself, have developed in the mar­
gins of Platonism, threatening it all along, opening up possibilities
of thought and life unknown to it. Whilst dorninant, and deeply
entrenched in Western thought, Platonism is a porous substance,
frOlll which thought escapes in all sorts of ways.
In a way, the overturning of Platonislll begins to unfold in the
Platonic text itself, and in the Sophist in particular. For there, we
arrive at the paradoxical and eminently open-ended situation where
the art of the Sophist is brought to a point of almost absolute prox­
iIllity with that of the genuine philosopher. The conclusion that we
are invited to draw is that, ultimately, there is no absolute criterion
for the distinction between images and simulacra. Deleuze doesn't
fail to mention the irony, pushed to its very liIllits, that is, to the
point of humor, which is at work in the Platonic dialogue: Plato is


The Deleuzian reversal of Platonislll
"the first to indicate this direction for the overthrow of Platonisn1//
(LS 295). By confronting "sophism as its enelllY, but also as its lin1it
and its double" (Eee 136L Platonislll reveals a force it cannot con­
tainP It is precisely frOlll that standpoint, internal to Platonism,
that Deleuze will attelllpt to overturn it. Deleuze's strategy, then,
is not to construct a systelll of thought outside Platonism. Rather,
it is to show that its reversal takes place at the very mOlllent at
which it is set up, and to see the entire systelll frOlll the point of
view of the simulacnllll itself. Some have gone as far as to describe
the Deleuzian project "as a rejuvenated and even a completed
Platonislll.//I3 Ultimately, I would like to suggest that Deleuze's own
formidable philosophical activity and creation of concepts in the

be attributed to such a strategy and to the need to invent new pos­
sibilities of thought and new fonlls of life. As a preliminary stage,
however, I would like to indicate how Deleuze identifies resources
to overturn Platonislll within the post-Platonic age of the Greeks,
and lllOSt notably in the naturalisllls of Lucretius and Stoicism.
Lucretius' naturalism
In his essay, "The Silllulacrum and Ancient Philosophy/' Deleuze
presents the naturalism of the author of De Rerum Natura as a
speculative as well as practical enterprise that is specifically anti­
Platonic. It consists of an investigation into the nature of things,
and the laws of nature, the ultimate goal of which is to identify the
conditions under which the hurnan can achieve the highest degree
of fulfilhllent, which Lucretius defines as pleasure:
The speculative object and the practical object of philosophy as Naturalism,
science and pleasure, coincide on this point: it is always a matter of denoun­
cing the illusion, the false infinite, the infinity of religion and all of the . . .
myths in which it is expressed. To the question "what is the use of philoso­
phy" the answer must be: who else would have an interest in holding forth
the image of a free man, and in denouncing all of the forces which need
myth and troubled souls in order to establish their power? . . .  One of the
most profound constants of Naturalism is to denounce everything that is
sadness, everything that is the cause of sadness, and everything that needs
sadness to exercise its power. From Lucretius to Nietzsche, the same end is
pursued and attained. (LS 278-79, translation modified)

On one level, Lucretius' point of departure is reinarkably close to

blurs the distinction that Plato had introduced between copies and

By contrast with the Platonic account, Lucretius' explanation

and transcendent structure, governed by the Idea's relation to its
Inany instances, we have an imlnanent structure, governed by a
cOlnplex play of depth and surface. In place of an eidetic core, appre­

apprehended through the senses. In addition, the relation between

do not see or perceive matter at the atOlnic level, we always perceive
it as it is, in the way in which it is cOlTlbined and cOlnposed. Near

THE NEW PRINCIPLES OF SELECTION: "DIFFERENCE"
AND "REPETITION"
Deleuze's own systelnatic atteInpt to overturn Platonisln draws

The Deleuzian reversal of Platonisn1
73
Judgn1ent. Diffel'ence and Repetition goes as far as to define itself

differences freed fron1 the grip of Ideas and the logic of identity. The

of Illultiplicities?24
Deleuze defines the simulacrum as "the systelll where different
[ie difjel'ent] relates to different through difference itself" (DR 277).
To the formula, "only that which resembles differs/, he opposes
the fonllula, "only differences can resemble each other" (LS 261).
Each corresponds to a different reading of the world, and a differ­
ent perspective. The first, connected with the thoughts of Plato
and Aristotle, thinks difference frOlll the standpoint of a previ­
ous siIllilarity or identity. The second, on the other hand, suggests
that we think of siIllilarities, and even identity, "as the product of
a deep disparity" (LS 261). By saying that identity is a product of
difference, Deleuze not only reverses the tenns of Platonism. He
also, and IllOSt significantly, replaces the metaphysics of represen­
tation with a Illetaphysics of production, which Lucretius had
already intilllated: difference, he claiIlls, is essentially productive,
and the only engine or principle of production. The world does not
unfold through iIllitation and reproduction, but through a rigor­
ous dynarnic of production. If there is an origin of production, it is
not identity, but dispal'ity: it is as a result of disparities between
elelllents and series, and at every level (physical, biological, psy­
chological, aesthetic, social and political, etc.L that the world is


74
MIGUEL DE BEISTEGUI
shaped and events take place. It is only because of differences and
differentials - of potential, energy, pressure, level, teinperature,
tension, in short, differences of intensity2) - that new phenOlnena
einerge. The first fonnula defines the world as a world of copies or
representations, and posits it as iinage. The second fonnula, on the
other hand, "defines the world of silnulacra" and "posits the world
itself as phantasIn."26 According to this reading of the world, phe­
nOlnena are not the n1anifestation of a hidden essence, which they

product of a con1n1unication between heterogeneous and dispar­
ate series, which results in a teinporary solution. In such a read­
ing of the world, there is no longer any roon1 for icons or iinages:
the only reality is that of silnulacra, that is, phenOlnena generated
through difference. The silnuiacruin is no longer a degraded copy. It
is no longer a Inatter of distinguishing between essence and appear­
ance, or Inodel and copy. Rather, it is a n1atter of distinguishing
between copies and sin1ulacra, but with a view to affinning the
rights of the latter, and subverting the distinction constitutive of
representation: "Crowned anarchies are substituted for the hier­
archies of representationj non1adic distributions for the sedentary
distributions of representation" (DR 278). The systein of silnulacra
does not force one to choose and judge between appearances, but to
affinn the world of appearances and celebrate it as "a condensation
of coexistences and a silnultaneity of events" (LS 262).
In place of the systein of representation, dOlninated by the para­
digin of iinitation, reseinblance, and participation, and the sys­
tenlatic exclusion of sin1ulacra, Deleuze construes a systein of
difference, insisting all along that such a systein is the only real­
ity, and that the world of representation is itself an illusion. Were
it not for the connection between difference and repetition, how­
ever, the reversal of Piatonisin would rernain incOlnplete. Why?
Because representation itself presupposes a concept of repetition.
Whether in the Platonic theory of iInitation, the Neoplatonic the­
ory of participation, or the Aristotelian theory of truth, a certain
type of repetition is at stake. It is the repetition of the saIne in the
other, the identical in difference, or the Inodel in the copy, but in
a way that signals a loss, however Ininin1al. In other words, it is a
Inodel of repetition as degradation. The Deleuzian concept of repe­
tition is entirely different, and aiined at wresting production from


The Deleuzian reversal of Platonisln
75
representation. Re-petition, he clailns, is opposed to re-presentation.
If the two concepts share a prefix, its meaning is radically differ­
ent in both (DR 57). In the first instance, difference is said only in
relation to the identical, whereas in the other it's being itself
the
being of the silnulacrum - which is said in relation to difference.
That which is repeated, or returns, in the systern of the simulac­
Hun, and that which Inakes (a) difference, is not the self-identity
and self-presence of the paradigIn, but difference itself. All that
returns, and returns always, and always differently, is difference.
Were it not for this return, the world would repeat itself identically,
or perhaps slightly differently, but always as the world that unfolds
frOln an identical origin. There would be no possibility of anything
new emerging in the world, no possibility of creation or evolution.
In that sense, repetition is opposed to re-production. The world is
produced as a result of a principle of difference that never ceases
to return. The only thing that reproduces itself, and always differ­
ently, is difference.
In following Nietzsche's effort to overturn Platonism, Deleuze
also follows Nietzsche's most extreme and deinanding thought,
nanlely, that of the eternal return. Yet, as with the definition of
Platonisln, Deleuze interprets Nietzsche's thought of the eternal
return in a specific way. If Nietzsche's thought of the eternal return
is able to reverse representation and destroy the icons (as well as
the model), it is to the extent that he does not presuppose the Same
and the Siinilar, but shows them to be produced by the system of
the silnulacruIn, and thus, at the Saine tiIne, reveals the illusion of
the SaIne as the origin and foundation of everything that is, as the
Being beneath beings. But the only Being is that of difference, and
its productive repetitions:
Returning is being, but only the being of becoming. The eternal return does
not bring back "the same", but returning constitutes the only Same of that
which becomes. Returning is the becoming-identical of becoming itself.
Returning is thus the only identity, but identity as a secondary power, that
is, the identity of difference, the identical which is said of the different, or
which gravitates around the different. Such an identity, produced by diffe­
rence, is determined as "repetition". (DR 41)
It is a mistake, therefore, to clailn that everything - every single
thing, every being - returns. Insofar as what returns is difference,


76
MIGUEL DE BEISTEGUI
the power of the silnulacruIn, the chaos of a world without begin­
ning or end, repetition is itself a principle of selection, albeit a
paradoxical one: "What is selected are all the procedures opposed
to selection" (LS 265). What is excluded is selection in the sense
of Platonisln - selection based on silnilarity and identity. "Not
only does the eternal return not Inake everything return, it causes
those who fail the test to perish . . .  The Identical does not return.
The SaIne and the Silnilar, the Analogous and the Opposed, do
not return" (DR 299). Everything that doesn't withstand the test

what is selected, is precisely everything that Platonisln excludes,
namely, "that which pretends to correct divergence, to recenter the
circles or order the chaos, and to provide a Inodel or make a copy"
(LS 265). Far from being merely a theoretical representation of the
world, then, repetition "carries out a practical selection among dif­
ferences according to their capacity to produce," and this means,
ultilnately, their capacity "to pass the test of the eternal return"
(DR 4I). Difference returns insofar as it can producei production
is itself a result of a process of differentiation. The new principle
of selection is one that affinns the reality of becoming, or chaos,
over the fixed order and hierarchy of representation. And if it is
"practical," it is not just in the ontological, but also in the eth­
ical sense of the tenn. As Nietzsche hilnself emphasized, repeti­
tion is a test for us, and a principle to which our entire existence
should be subjected as the rnost delnanding, extrerne, and beau­
tiful thought. For it is the thought through which we affirm the
reality of becoming and difference over the illusion of being and
identity, the desire for a world of creation over the world as imita­
tion, the love of the abyss over the longing for secure foundations,
the celebration of new values over the reproduction of old values.
Repetition is selective in the ontological and ethical sense. But
the point is that, for Deleuze, as for Spinoza or Nietzsche, there is
no longer any difference between the ontological and the ethical:
the ethical is nothing outside our ability to realize or Inaxilnize
our ontological potential, nothing, that is, outside our capacity to
extend ourselves to the limit of our power (puissance), to reach
that extrelne lilnit or point at which our energy is able to trans­
form itself. As such, repetition signals the possibility of a life that
is no longer governed by the ordinary and the average, but by the


The Deleuzian reversal of Platonisn1
77
extrelne, the lin1inal, the singular. Those categories aren't lnoral,
but, first and foren10st, ontological, physical, lnathelnatical, as
well as ethical. If they can be understood as values, it is not insofar
as they belong to a faculty of judglnent, but insofar as they corres­
pond to a type of existence, and signal a lneasure, a desire to lneas­
ure and describe intensities, or energies. They signify a life beyond
good and evil, an existence that is no longer defined by its ability
to judge. To live, to exist, now becolnes a lnatter of experimenting,
creating, in short, becOlning.
By focusing on Deleuze's account of Platonisln, and his effort
to overturn it, we were able to extract the origin of, or the funda­
mental rnotivation behind, the lnetaphysics of representation, and
reveal another image of thought, which is at once genealogical and
constructive. Platonism is the nalne of a solution to a problem that
is at once lnoral, aesthetic, political, and lnetaphysical. The notion
of ilnage, especially as distinguished frOln that of the silnulacruln,
crystallizes all those aspects, initially unified. The Deleuzian over­
turning of Platonisrll doesn't relnain content with reversing the
terms of the Platonic distinctions, nor even, as the tradition has
done, with dislnantling the distinction between appearance and
essence. Rather, it extracts from Platonisln that which the Platonic
text sought to neutralize and set aside, but which keeps returning,
disrupting that text, undermining the efforts of representation. It
doesn't offer a different solution to an old problenl - how to select
genuine images and exclude simulacra - but reverses the tenns of
the probleln itself, and asks: how to select siInulacra and reveal the
extent to which they escape the play of the original and its cop­
ies? How to think images outside the fralnework of representation?
How to produce an ilnage of thought that is based on a different
thought of the ilnage? How to think and live without transcend­
ence? In the end, we can wonder whether those questions, initially
triggered by Deleuze's confrontation with the history of Platonisln,
continued to orient and shape his thought, despite the fact that the
notion of silnulacIllln lnore or less disappeared from his work after
the publication of Difference and Repetition and Logic of Sense.27
Even though Deleuze's later thought no longer focuses on the dis­
tinction between copies and simulacra, and the need to select the
latter in place of the fonner, it is still concerned with the possi­
bility of distinguishing between ilnages, of drawing a typology of


78
MIGUEL D E  BEISTEGUI
ilnages, to which specific ilnages of thought correspond. Whether in
connection with Francis Bacon or the history of cinelna, it is now a
Inatter of distinguishing between Inere cliches and genuine ilnages,
between organic images and fragmentary or crystal-iInages. It is a
Inatter of revealing the gap that separates the orthodox conception
and practice of art and philosophy as representation, that is, as the
effort to produce an organic and hannonious image via the con­
vergence and accord of all faculties, and the hetero- or para-doxical
ilnage of thought and art, which is set in Illotion, and quite literally
provoked, through the experience of an iIllage that tears the world
of farniliar iInages apart, brings about a conflict of the faculties that
results not in harmony and beauty (as symbols of the moral good),
but in an unsettling pathos that draws thought out of its dogmatic
sleep, laziness, stupor, and stupidity, and opens the way to what, in
his later thought, Deleuze calls the body without organs, or the life
of pure ilnlnanence.

Deleuze and ..a.. .- _  .... ....
How are things determined to be what they are? Kant's answer to
this question defines transcendental idealisln, and forms the core
of Deleuze's lifelong fascination with Kant.r He criticizes Kant for
reducing detennination to the external application of concepts to
the given. Yet what preoccupies Deleuze is a deeper account of deter­
mination in Kant: the determination of the being of the self ("I am")
by its own thinking activity ("I think"). Because this deterrnination
happens in time, the being that is determined is different frOln the
being that is detenninable. The latter, by definition, is not thought
and cannot be thought, and yet it is precisely what is to be thought.
Here is what, above all, fascinates Deleuze about Kant: the very act
in which the I thinks its own being requires that being to squirm
out of thought's reach. Through its thinking activity the I generates
difference from its being, indicating that the being of thought is
both what must be thought and what cannot be thought.
The difference of thought from its being recurs through­
out Deleuze's writings on Kant. An early version of it appears in
Empiricism and Subjectivity (ES I I I); in an essay of the following
year, Deleuze speaks of Kant in tenns of "the difference internal
to the Being which thinks itself" (DI 17, translation Inodified).2 It
is a rich presence in Difference and Repetition (DR 58, 85-86, 276),
in the essay "On Four Poetic Formulas That Might Summarize the
Kantian Philosophy" (Eee 27-35, d. Kep vii-xiii), and in his 1978
lectures) Finally, this difference is evoked in terms of the plane of
imlnanence:
Perhaps this is the supreme act of philosophy: not so much to think THE
plane of immanence as to show that it is there, unthought in every plane,
and to think it in this way as the outside and inside of thought, as the
82


Deleuze and Kant
not-external outside and the not-internal inside - that which cannot be
thought and yet must be thought, which was thought once, as Christ was
incarnated once, in order to show, that one time, the possibility of the
impossible. Thus Spinoza is the Christ of philosophers, and the greatest
philosophers are hardly more than apostles who distance themselves from
or draw near to this mystery. (WP 59-60)
Deleuze appears to be an apostle arnong apostles, attempting to
draw ever nearer to the lnystery of thought that thinks its own
being. This activity both lnakes possible and relies on the difference
of being and thought. Thought thereby generates its own differing
frOln itself, its own "unthought" or "outside" that is nevertheless
internal to it, "as if thought was worked over from the inside by
sOlnething that it cannot thinlz."4 This is significant for Deleuze's
understanding of philosophy itself, the non-philosophical core of
which is the origin of thought that necessarily escapes thought.
This may COlne as a surprise to those who take Deleuze's relation
to Kant to be largely critical, partly on the basis of Deleuze's own
relnarks.5 Certainly Deleuze is disparaging about Kant's elnpiricism
and his idealisln, and he is eager to break the bounds within which
Kant restricts "the transcendental" and "ideas." Incontestably,
Spinoza, Nietzsche, and Bergson do lnore than Kant to advance
a philosophy of difference. Yet a strong body of critical literature
presents Deleuze as a post-Kantian, and for good reason: Deleuze's
work extends Kant's critical project and Copernican revolution.6
With respect to the discovery of this specific problenl
that of how
thought is to think its own being - Kant is second only to Spinoza
in Deleuze's estilnation. We cannot reduce the Deleuze-Kant rela­
tion to one of rivalry or criticism, but should instead see Kant as
Deleuze's co-apostle, trying to reveal the Salne lnystery.
In this chapter I will show how Deleuze upholds Kant's recogni­
tion of the difference of being and thought as an internally deter­
lnining and ontologically productive difference.? It is because of this
discovery that he criticizes Kant's tendency to conceal this diffe­
rence and to lnake detennination an external, merely epistemic pro­
cedure. Kant discovers the differing of thought frOln its own being,
but covers over this internal difference with the identity of the sub­
ject, leading hiln to affinn only the external separation of concep­
tual thought from the being of the given. Thus a pure, productive,
internal difference is suppressed in favor of a restrictive external


84
