separation reliant on a self-identical subject. This separation, a
defining Inove of transcendental idealisIn, is for Deleuze a betrayal
of transcendental philosophy, which on Kant's Inodel does not delve
deeply enough into the differential conditions it briefly identifies.
This tension between revealing and concealing difference is the
subject of the first two sections.8 In the third section we see how
this tension is indicative of both the Kantian plane of iininanence
and of Deleuze's conception of what philosophy is.
EXTERNAL DIFFERENCE AND DETERMINATION
Kant's Critique of Pure Reason ailns, mnong other things, to
destabilize the rationalist assuInption that thought is necessarily
adequate to being. For the rationalist, to be is to be conceived in
a con1plete concept by God's infinite intellect, and to be thus con­
ceived is to be brought into being. Rationalisin thereby provides
a unified account of the genesis of beings and of their detennin­
ation to be what they specifically are. Fundmnental to transcenden­
tal idealism, by contrast, is the denial of the intelligibility of the
genesis and cOlnplete detennination of beings (d. KCP 13-14). The
finite thought of a hUlnan Inind is not only inadequate to being; it
cannot even be certain that there is an infinite intellect that would
generate, detennine, and know being adequately. The hUlnan Inind
has an idea of such an intellect, and ideas about how being is gener­
ated and c0111pletely detennined. These "transcendental ideas," as
Kant calls theIn, can be thought but not known. Such ideas regulate
the procedure by which the hUlnan Inind overcomes its Inetaphys­
ical ignorance: the procedure of applying concepts to intuitions. For
the hUlnan Inind is not like the idea of an infinite intellect, which
would produce its objects in thinking theIn; the hun1an Inind "can
only think," and it Inust apply its stock of concepts to given things
that affect it from the outside.9 Pure a priori concepts - Kant's cat­
egories - neither generate beings nor internally detenl1ine theln to
be what they are. The categories are applied externally to the given,
determining it as an "object in general" that can be represented and
known. Detennination is a Inatter of applying to intuited Inaterial
the concepts which make it an object of possible experience. The
genesis and internal detennination of that material are inaccessible
to us, and Inust "reinain undetennined."IO


Deleuze and Kant
85
Deleuze criticizes transcendental idealisln on the grounds that
in separating thought and being, it blocks every attelnpt to inves­
tigate the genesis of being. Deleuze is critical of Kant's "elnpiri­
cisln" in considering structures of thought only as they are applied
externally to received givens: neither the concept nor the idea has
any internal connection to being. Kant's transcendental idealism
is not transcendental enough for Deleuze, because it cannot show
how the structures of thought are really and internally connected
to the given, nor how thinking can apply itself to anything other
than its own representations. Thinking, trapped amongst its con­
cepts, is fundamentally at odds with the being whose Ineans of
production it cannot know. The Transcendental Aesthetic, "the
science of the sensible/' becOlnes founded on "what can be rep­
resented in the sensible/, and not on the "being of the sensible"
(DR 56-57).
This probleln was identified in Kant's own day by Solomon
Mailnon, a critic of Kant and an important source for Deleuze. In
Mailnon's 1790 Essay on Transcendental Philosophy, the claiIns of
the Critique of Pure Reason are subjected simultaneously to ration­
alist and eInpiricist scrutiny.II MaiInon argues that Kant has estab­
lished neither the fact of experience (quid facti) nor our right to
apply concepts to it (quid juris). The fundmnental probieln is the
separation of sensibility frOln understanding: "How can the under­
standing subInit sOlnething (the given object) to its power (to its
rules) that is not in its power? In the Kantian system, nmnely where
sensibility and understanding are two totally different sources of
our cognition, this question is insoluble."D Mailnon believes that
to overcome this probleIn, transcendental idealisIn Inust investi­
gate not the conditions of possible experience, but the conditions
of real experience: the genesis of qualitative sensations. Drawing
on Spinoza and Leibniz, Mailnon argues that the finite understand­
ing is a mode of the infinite intellect and operates in the saIne way,
producing its objects in intuiting theln. Real experience is thus
the product of a genesis that takes place within the understand­
ing, based on rules for generating qualities called "differentials."
FrOln these pure differences flow intensive qualities called "ideas of
understanding," which are finally "fixed" as objects of sensibility
through ilnagination and representation. There is no probieln about
applying concepts to the content of sensibility since the latter is


86
BETH LOR D
produced by the finite understanding and is not fundmnentally dif­
ferent from the content of thought.
Mailnon reinstates the adequacy of thought to being in order to
assert that being is the product of thought, and pushes transcenden­
tal philosophy further by investigating the genesis of the real from
the idea. It might be contested that this is rationalism in the service
of absolute idealisln, but Mailnon is cOlnlnitted to the transcen­
dental nature of experience, and so is Deleuze. Maimon's Essay is
therefore an important source for Difference and Repetition, which
takes on this same project while avoiding its idealist conclusion.I3
Deleuze says that it is Maimon "who proposes a fundamental refor ..
Inulation of the Critique and an overcoming of the Kantian duality
between concept and intuition" (DR 173). For Kant, the concept is
applied to the intuition frOln the outside, such that the transcenden­
tal lnOlnent is one of conditioning, and the requirement to account
for the genesis of the given is renounced. The determination of things
through concepts is, sirnilarly, a process external to those things
that relies on Inere empirical differences. These differences enable
us to deterrnine things relative to one another or "negatively," but
they do not account for how they are positively detennined in the
first place. Through their external differences things differ Inerely
in degree, because they all resemble each other at some level of gen­
erality. Kant's categories are the tenns of highest generality that
render objects of experience the same in their objectivity in general,
but different in the degree of their spatial extent, telnporal duration,
qualitative intensity, causal efficacy, and so on. The determination
of the given takes place through applying the terms of highest gen­
erality to it frOln the outside.
It is in this sense that the Inistake of empiricisln is "to leave
external what is separated," while that of doglnatism is to fill it
(DR 170). Doglnatism fills the gap between a determinable object
and its conceptual detennination either by arguing for the a pri­
ori cOlnplete determination of the fonner by the latter (Leibnizian
rationalism) or by showing that they necessarily collapse into a new
indetenninate unity (Hegelian idealism). But empiricism leaves
the determinable object and its conceptual determination separ­
ate, such that the detenninable-determinant relation is external to
the thing to be detennined. A more profound transcendental phil­
osophy must consider how detennination, and the crucial relation


Deleuze and Kant
between detenninable and detenninant, operates internally to the
thing, such that the detennination of the thing is no broader than
the thing itself. Externally detennining conditions will only give
us "objects in general" that differ in degree, and the Inass of pos­
sible experience. Kant's approach is "too elnpirical," investigating
merely the superficial relation of concepts to representations, and
never delving into the "depth" of what Inakes those representations
what they are.
Deleuze's "transcendental elnpiricisln" is worked out in contrast
to this.I4 It is concerned with what is immanent to the elnpirical,
the transcendental conditions that generate the real (where "real"
is understood in the Kantian sense of qualitative or intensive,
that which can only be sensed and not known a priori). In seeking
what lies within and gives rise to real experience, Deleuze wants
to recover a Inore detenninate version of Kant's noumenon: one
that does not have a transcendent relation to phenomena. He seeks
"the nOUlnenon closest to the phen01nenon" which is internal to it
and produces it immanently (DR 222). Whereas Kant's noumenon
is the indeterminate thought of an intelligible ground, Deleuze's
is the being of the sensible which is both determinable and deter­
Inining, and which can only be sensed or encountered, not repre­
sented. The object of encounter which "forces us to think . . .  is not
a sensible being but the being of the sensible. It is not the given
but that by which the given is given" (DR 139-40). The sensible is
Kant's "empirically real" world of possible experience, whereas the
encounter is the real of sensation - "just that element which cannot
be anticipated."Is The encounter is forced into the sensible reahn
by intensity, a real (but not elnpirically real) transcendental condi­
tion (but not of possible experience). Intensity is insensible in tenns
of representation, and unthinkable in tenns of concepts. But the
fact that it cannot be grasped by the elnpirical exercise of the facul­
ties of "common sense" does not render it unintelligible; rather, the
intensive condition of the given is internal to thought and must be
thought. This is not conceptual thought applied to an already-given
object; it is the thought that is forced on us in the encounter, in the
giving of the given (DR 192-94).
Deleuze makes the Kantian "real of sensation" the product of
a Mailnonian genesis imlnanent to thought. For Deleuze as for
Mailnon, the real transcendental conditions of the given are ideas,


88
B ETH LORD
within which intensities flow and surge. Kant also locates the condi­
tions of the given in ideas: our thoughts of what produces and COln­
pletely detennines being are the transcendental ideas of the infinite
intellect and the SUIn total of reality. These ideas are conditions that
cannot be experienced, but that force then1selves upon our thought.
But while Kant's ideas can legitin1ately be thought as the conditions
of our thinking of the genesis of being, they cannot be assulned to
be the real conditions of that genesis. Furthermore, transcendental
ideas are legitilnate only when they regulate conceptual thought.
For Kant, atteInpting to investigate the genetic conditions of being
brings us to transcendental ideas whose explanatory power is lim­
ited to the reahn of concepts of possible experience. Once again we
are closed off frOln the conditions of the real of sensation.
Kant's transcendental ideas are iInportant but frustrating for
Deleuze.16 They are "problelns" concerning the genesis of being,
expressed in the paralogislns, antinomies, and ideal of pure rea­
son. But these problenls are "solved" only in relation to represented
appearances and concepts of understanding. Problelns that can­
not be solved in relation to these external conditions are "false
problelns" that generate transcendent objects as their illusory
solutions (DR 168-70, 161-62).17 The antinomies, for instance, are
insoluble if we aSSUIne their ideas relate to the real being of a COln­
plete universe, first cause, and so on. They are resolved only when
their ideas are restricted to regulating our thinking about possible
experience. Deleuze thinks this makes the truth of the Kantian
idea dependent on the possibility of finding a solution in its exterέ
nal relation to representation, and not on its internal power (DR
16I-62). The constitutive power of the idea to detennine and gen­
erate being is distrusted and suppressed by Kant, leaving it only a
regulative power to deternline thinking. It is not that transcenden­
tal idealisIn is unwilling to investigate the conditions of being in
the idea, Deleuze thinks; rather, it is unable to do so, for it cannot
trust the power of ideas except insofar as they are considered not to
be constitutive of being.
Deleuze's criticisIn that Kant's transcendental idealisIn is "too
empirical" has four targets: Kant's failure to account for the genesis
of beings, his "external" account of detennination, his reliance on a
conception of difference in degree, and his denial that the idea has
any real constitutive power. Deleuze's transcendental eInpiricisIn


Deleuze and Kant
is proposed as a corrective to these lilnitations: it looks into the
depth, interior, or being of experience and does not remain on its
surface. Deleuze wants to find there a concept of difference in itself,
where differences in kind affinnatively detennine and individuate
things, and Ideas are the internally detennining conditions that
produce intensive real objects that differ in kind. Difference nUlst
be internal to detennination, which takes place between detennin­
able and detenninant elelnents in the idea. The relation will then
not be one of externally conditioning the object of experience, but of
internally generating and detennining the real object, yet without
relying on an infinite intellect.
INTERNAL DIFFERENCE AND DETERMINATION
Deleuze's critique of Kant risks the objection that it misses the
point. Kant's intention - indeed his innovation
is precisely to
instate the "external" detennination of judgInent in the place of the
internal ontological detennination of ration ali SIn, which relies on
doglnatic clailns about the infinite intellect. What makes Deleuze
a significant reader of Kant is his insistence that Kant does the­
Inatize internal detennination, in a way that avoids dogmatisln and
approaches transcendental eInpiricisIn. In chapter I of Difference
and Repetition, Deleuze says:
Rather than being concerned with what happens before and after Kant
(which amounts to the same thing), we should be concerned with a precise
moment within Kantianism, a furtive and explosive moment which is not
even continued by Kant, much less by post-Kantianism . . .  For when Kant
puts rational theology into question, in the same strol{e he introduces a
kind of disequilibrium, a fissure or crack in the pure Self of the "I think,"
an alienation in principle, insunnountable in principle. (DR 58)
Kant "puts rational theology into question" by Inaking God a regu­
lative idea in the Transcendental Dialectic. God no longer has the
power to think the SUIn total of reality, nor to generate or deter­
mine being internally in thought. Instead, the subject has the
power to think the idea of God, and to detennine objects externally
through the categories. It has this power thanks to its spontaneity,
the "active" part of the subject that applies concepts to the given
objects taken up by the subject's receptivity. Thus in asserting the


9 0
B E T H  LORD
spontaneity of the subject, Kant puts rational theology into ques­
tion, and gives the power of internal detennination to the subject.
The "lnOlnent" in question is one that lnakes the spontaneous self
an other to itself in section 25 of Kant's B-edition Transcendental
Deduction.Is Here, the difference between the given and the con­
cept that grounds the external detennination of possible experience
reveals a lnore profound difference between being and thinking that
grounds the internal deternlination of the real.
The Transcendental Deduction is where Kant justifies the appli­
cation of the categories to the given. He does this by way of an ana­
lysis of the cOlnplex lnoves through which experience, and both the
subject and object of experience, are fonned. The sections under
consideration here concern how the lnanifold of intuition
includ­
ing intuition of our own existence - is synthesized, and thereby
detennined, by our spontaneous understanding. In section 24 Kant
shows that inner sense on its own contains only the fonn of intu­

lninate intuition. The understanding, through the spontaneous act
that relates the lnanifold to the "I think," combines the lnanifold
and determines it. In this way, indetenninate intuitions are pro­
duced as detenninate representations that can be thought. Even
time (the pure form of inner sense) cannot be represented, deter­
mined, or thought unless its manifold is combined, in the drawing
of a line for instance. The self, insofar as it is intuited in inner sense,
is equally indetenl1inate unless its lnanifold is combined by the
understanding. The indeterminate self and the self that detennines
it are the same subject, and yet they are distinct. But there is no dif­
ficulty here, Kant says: since I am an appearance in space and tilne,
I lnust be "given" to my own passive receptivity, and synthesized by
lny own active spontaneity, just like any other object.I9
The cOlnplexity of this position is drawn out in section 25. In
the activity of cOlnbining the nlanifold, "I anl conscious of lnyself,
not as I appear to lnyself, nor as I am in lnyself, but only that I mn.
This representation is a thought, not an intuition."20 That is, the "I
think," which is responsible for deteIlTIining intuitions, involves
the indeterminate thought of my existence. My existence is not
ilnlnediately detennined by the "I think," for it can be determined
only through a cOlnbination of the lTlanifold of inner sense. The
consciousness of lny existence that arises with the "I think" gives


Deleuze and Kant
9 1
nle no knowledge of lny existence. "Accordingly I have no know­
ledge of lnyself as I anl but lnerely as I appear to Inyself."2I
Deleuze rightly points out that this is Kant's response to
Descartes' cogito, which states that detennination ("I think") dir­
ectly inlplies an undetennined existence ("I anl") detenninable by
it ("I dIn a thing that thinks").
The entire Kantian critique amounts to objecting against Descartes that
it is impossible for determination to bear directly upon the undetermined.
The determination ("I think") obviously implies something undetermined
("I am"), but nothing so far tells us how it is that this undetermined is
determinable by the "I think" . . .  Kant therefore adds a third logical value:
the determinable, or rather the form in which the undetermined is deter­
minable (by the determination). This third value suffices to make logic a
transcendental instance. It amounts to the discovery of Difference - no
longer in the form of an empirical difference between two determinations,
but in the form of a transcendental Difference between the Determination
as such and what it determines; no longer in the form of an external diffe­
rence which separates, but in the form of an internal Difference which
establishes an a priori relation between thought and being. (DR 85-86)
In Kant's rejection of the ilnlnediacy of "I think therefore l aIn,"
Deleuze finds that Kant adopts transcendental empiricisln - just
for an instant
and discovers the pure difference internal to the
relation of determination. This difference establishes an original
relation between thought and being that is deeper than the relation
between concept and given.
To understand Deleuze's point, we need to look at Kant's import­
ant footnote to section 2 5 .  Kant reiterates that while my existence
is already given with the "I think" that detennines it, lny existence
cannot be ilnlnediately detennined. For the lnode in which I dIn to
determine lny existence - that is, the lnanifold to be cOlnbined by
the "I think" - is not ilnlnediately given. It lnust be intuited in tilne,
which lnakes my indetenninate existence detenninable by the "I
think." But the "I think" thereby detennines sOlnething different
frOln itself: it detennines its existence as it is given in time. Thus "I
cannot detennine 111y existence as that of a self-active being; all that
I can do is to represent to lnyself the spontaneity of my thought,
that is, of the detennination."22
The "I think," in the act of detennining its existence, prevents
the detennination of its own activity. And so the detennination of


92
B ETH LORD
Iny existence is a n10veIllent of self-differing. Detennination is not
here a n1atter of subsulning SOlne given being in general under an
external concept that would detennine it as Iny being. It is a In at­
ter of producing Iny being by internally differentiating it from Iny
thinking. This is the InOlnent in which Kant approaches a theory of
iInInanent differential genesis: the "I think" generates itself frOln
its own differential relation to itself. The difference n1eans there is
no possibility of adequately thinking Illy spontaneity; it can only be
represented insofar as receptivity experiences the spontaneity of my
thought being exercised upon it, as if fron1 outside it. The self can­
not enact or be its own thinking activity. For this reason, Deleuze
characterizes this InOlnent with Rimbaud's phrase, "I is an other"
(DR 86; Kep vii; Eee 29). Tilne is the condition of detenninability
of the self, and the hinge on which I generate Iny difference frOln
myself. I cannot experience or know the spontaneity of my thought;
I can only experience its effects in time.
Kant's "fractured I" grounds determination in an original diffe­
rence of undetermined being and detenllinant thinking. The frac­
tured I is undetermined being, the determinability of that being,
and the activity of detennining it. In this sense, it has the saIne
problelnatic unity as the "Idea" in Deleuze's sense:
Ideas . . .  present three moments: undetermined with regard to their object,
determinable with regard to objects of experience, and bearing the ideal of
an infinite determination with regard to concepts of the understanding. It
is apparent that Ideas here repeat the three aspects of the [fractured] Cogito:
the I am as an indeterminate existence, time as the form under which this
existence is determinable, and the 1 think as a determination. Ideas are
exactly the thoughts of the Cogito, the differentials of thought. (DR r69)
This passage, frOln the opening to chapter 4 of Difference and
Repetition, suggests that Deleuze's Idea has its roots not only in
Kant's transcendental ideas but also in the fractured I and Mairllon's
ideas of understanding. The spontaneous activity of Kant's frac­
tured I relnains undetermined as it detennines its being, thereby
producing itself as that which can only be represented as an other.
Correspondingly, Deleuze's Idea, in its activity of detennining itself
with regard to objects of experience, relnains undetennined with
regard to its object. The object of the Idea is the virtual problem,
and its self-detennination is its integration into actual solutions.


Deleuze and Kant
93
As the Idea detennines itself through actual solutions in the realn1
of the sensible, its virtual object, the probleln, "lnust be represented
without being able to be directly detennined" (DR I69). The prob­
len1 becOlnes detennined by analogy with the objects of experience
it relates to. So the Idea is its differing frOln itself. It produces itself
as the detern1inate solutions (singularities and events) that lnake its
problelnatic being fundatnentally undetenninable, and thinkable
only in tenns of representation. As a result, problelns can be repre­
sented in tenns of experience and concepts, though the problelnatic
being of the Idea is fundatnentally undetermined and unrepresent­
able. Just as Kant's passive self "receives the activity of [its] own
thought as an other" (Eee 30), Deleuze's actualized Idea receives
the activity of the problen1 as an other. This other has a powerful
capacity to shock: it is the "being of the sensible" that can only be
sensed; the unthinkable that lnust be thought (DR I99-200).
The Idea, like the "fractured I", indicates the priority of tilne in
the detennination and genesis of real experience. "Ideas are exactly
the thoughts of the [fractured] Cogito" because they enact the activ­
ity of thinking determining its own being as the unfolding of tilne.
Tilne is the form of the determinability of being by thought. For
Kant, it is the fonn internal to the I that continually produces the
I as differing frOln itself. For Deleuze, tilne is internal to the Idea:
its unfolding is the activity of the Idea detennining itself and pre­
serving its own indetenninacy. Time is understood here as the pure
difference that establishes the a priori relation between thinking and
being (DR 86).23 It is not that thinking detennines being in time, as
if thinking and being were concept and object standing opposed to
one another in a telnporal container. Rather, tiTHe is the difference
internal to both the fractured I and the Idea: thought detennining

indicates an original and irreducible relation that does not merely

thinking and being that can be fonnulated "thinking-tilne-being"
or "detenninant-determinable-undetern1ined." Ideas contain these
"dislnelnbered ln0111ents" as "an internal problelnatic objective
unity" (DR I70).
It is clear that this determination does not take the form of a
judglnent between a concept and an object, and that the thinking


94
BETH LORD
and being spoken of here do not refer to a subjective lnind or actual
objects. Rather, this is a deeper ontological relation that fornls the
internal structure of Deleuze's virtual, pre-individual Idea and
Kant's pre-subjective 1. The original relation of the detennination
and genesis of the real is the relation of being and thinking differen­
tiated by tilne. Deleuze thinks Kant is wrong to locate this differ­
ential relation in a subject, where the difference and its generative
power become covered over. The fracture in the I is "quickly filled
by a new fonn of identity" and obscured by the external difference
between spontaneity and receptivity (DR 87). Yet in this moment
of the Transcendental Deduction, which even the post-Kantians do
not recognize, Kant discovers pure difference. "For a brief moment
we enter into that schizophrenia in principle which characterises
the highest power of thought, and opens Being directly onto diffe­
rence, despite all the lnediations, all the reconciliations, of the con­
cept" (DR 58).
Thus the fractured, self-differing I is, for Deleuze, the essence of
Kant's Copernican revolution and "constitutes the discovery of the
transcendental" (DR 86). For here Kant discovers the genetic condi­
tion of real experience in the pure difference of being and thinking.
In this, Kant overcomes rationalism and even supersedes Mailnon.
For without this difference, experience and detennination would be
grounded in the identity of an infinite intellect for which there is
no being that cannot be thought. Actual experience would be noth­
ing lnore than a copy of a "possible reality" detennined in advance.
Both the content of experience and the space in which it plays out
would be predetennined, and the future would be foreseeable (DR
198, d. LS 105-6). Where experience is grounded in the pure diffe­
rence between being and thinking, however - either in Kant's frac­
tured I or in Deleuze's Idea
the sensible is detennined as it is
generated. Experience is produced as surprising and unforeseeable:
frOln pure difference elnerge the real encounters that "cannot be
anticipated" and that shock us into thinking.
ORIENTATION ON THE PLANE OF IMMANENCE
Kant's Copernican revolution rests on the discovery of internal
difference, but also on the transcendent subject that covers it over.
Kant both reveals and conceals that experience is transcendentally


Deleuze and Kant
95
conditioned by pure, detennining difference. This tension between
revealing and concealing is intrinsic to transcendental idealisln.
The "concealing" Inove of conceptual judgment is necessarily
grounded in the fractured 1. But the internal difference of the self
cannot renlain openly "revealed" lest the whole critical enterprise
collapse in on itselfi Kantian critique requires a self-identical sub­
ject that can reliably think about itself and its projects. The subject,
in order to be a subject, necessarily conceals the differential condi­
tions of its own genesis: no wonder, then, that Kant cannot, or will
not, inquire into the genetic conditions of the real. This interplay
of revealing and concealing alnounts to the Kantian plane of ilnma­
nence, a discussion of which will cOlnplete the Deleuzian portrait
of Kant.24
In What is Philosophy Deleuze and Guattari tell us that
the history of philosophy is comparable to the art of the portrait. It is not
a matter of "lnaking lifelike," that is, of repeating what a philosopher said
but rather of producing resemblance by separating out both the plane of
immanence he instituted and the new concepts he created. (WP 55)
The Kantian plane of ilnlnanence needs to be separated and iso­
lated from Kant's new concepts, a task which is partly achieved in
the diagram of a proposed "lnachinic portrait" of Kant (WP 56). In
this diagrmn, the "I think" (an ox head) loudly proclaims its self­
identity by repeating "Self = Self" while powering a waterwheel
that generates experience through categories. Neither the machine
nor the diagraln includes the difference of the "I think" £rOln its
being, for even if that difference could remain open, it could not
be represented Inaterially or diagrammatically. However, ilnma­
nent to the "I think" and binding the image together is a broken
line representing "the transcendental field of possible experience,"
a line which unifies the portrait and draws a boundary around it
(WP 5 7). (Significantly, the transcendental ideas extend outside the
boundary.)
Imlnanence and the transcendental are neatly conjoined for
Kant. Things that appear to us have conditions of possibility that
are transcendental
pertaining to representational and conceptual
thought - but not transcendent. This means that the principles that
serve as transcendental conditions, and our search for and use of
those principles, are "ilnlnanent" in a Kantian sense: they remain


96
B E T H  LORD
within the bounds of possible experience.25 Principles that profess
to pass beyond those bounds are transcendent, and generate tran­
scendental illusions. So for Kant, concepts and representations are
ilnnlanent as long as they apply only to objects of possible experi­
ence, and ideas are used iInnlanently as long as they only regulate
and detennine thinking. The act of externally detennining givens
through concepts is iInnlanent, but an attelnpt to account for their
genesis would not be.
This sense of ilnmanence is ahnost the opposite of Deleuze's.
Yet the "plane of ilnlnanence" is a partly Kantian idea, discussed
in What is Philosophy in Kantian tenns. Distinguishing the plane
frOln the concept, Deleuze and Guattari state "the plane of ilnlna­
nence is not a concept that is or can be thought but rather the iInage
of thought, the ilnage thought gives itself of what it nleans to think,
to Inake use of thought, to find one's bearings in thought" (WP 37).
As becomes explicit in the next paragraph, this refers to Kant's
"What is Orientation in Thinking?/' the 1786 essay in which he
stresses the iInportance of recognizing the lilnits of reason. Kant
draws an analogy between spatial and logical orientation: we ori­
ent ourselves in an unfamiliar landscape or room by relating fixed
objects to the difference we feel "within [our] own subject" between
left and right.26 We sinlilarly orient ourselves in thought by lIleans
of "a subjective principle of reason where objective principles of rea­
son are inadequate."27 When reason ventures beyond the "frontiers"
of possible experience, it too orients itself by Ineans of a subjective
feeling
in this case, its own need for a concept of an uncondi­
tioned being to ground the concept of possible experience as a whole.
Instead of left and right, reason Inakes use of a distinction between
its own theoretical and practical elnploYlnent, which detennines
whether an object is a Inatter of knowledge or rational belie£. With
the understanding that God is a Inatter of rational belief and not
knowledge
a regulative and not a constitutive idea - the thinker
has a "compass" with which to "orient hilnself on his rational wan­
derings" beyond possible experience.28
Kant's ilnage of what it Ineans to find one's bearings in thought
is spatial and geographical: it is the iInage of reason wandering in
a field, crossing the boundary line of possible experience into the
dark and unfmniliar land of the supersensible, orienting itself by
Ineans of its need for the unconditioned and the internal COlnpass


Deleuze and Kant
97
that tells it how to reflect upon it. Put otherwise, "reason gives
the orders" as to what is iInInanent and includable in conceptual
thought, and what is transcendent and excluded (Kep 14; NP 92).
This is the Kantian plane of iInInanence, "the ilnage thought gives
itself of what it Ineans to think": thinking Ineans excluding that
which cannot be thought in representational and conceptual tenns.
These excluded things can be thought
they are ideas - but they
are objects of speculation and syn1bolization. Every elelnent of the
plane is in the subject: the subject constitutes the field, the condi­
tions of what appears inside the boundary line, where the boundary
line is drawn, the need to venture beyond it, and the tools for orien­
tation. Danger, too, carnes frOln the subject: thought is no longer
threatened by error or "holes in the path" as it was for Descartes,
but by illusions that issue frOln reason's own "internal arctic zone
where the needle of every cOlnpass goes Inad" (WP 52-53). Kant's
asslunption that reason can identify and assess its own conditions,
structures, and errors is known as "ilnInanent critique": the notion
that reason can orient itself without reference to anything outside
it (Rep 3; NP 91).19
The Kantian plane is an ilnage of iInInanence as the transcen­
dental. This plane is constituted by the subject, as the condition of
possibility of all cognitive thinking as such. Kant says at the end
of his life: "transcendental philosophy precedes the assertion of
things that are thought, as their archetype, [the place] in which they
must be set."30 Kant called his plane transcendental philosophy and
believed he had thereby avoided transcendence and illusion. But by
rnaking the plane iInInanent to a subject, Kant "discovers the mod­
ern way of saving transcendence": the transcendence of "a Subject
to which the field of ilnInanence is only attributed by belonging
to a self that necessarily represents such a subject to itself" (W P
46). What the subject transcends is the unthinkable, which is either
l11ade thinkable through synthesis and judgInent, or is pushed off
the plane. In aHinning itself as the center and legislator of iInmaȯ
nence, in deciding what is to stay "inside" conceptual thought, rea­
son creates an "outside" to which it is transcendent.
Yet there is another 'outside' at work here. The "best" plane of
ilnmanence - perhaps discovered only by Spinoza - is one in which
imInanence is not iInInanent to anything, transcendence is not
restored, and the unthinkable "outside" of thought is not external to


98
BETH LORD
thought itself (WP 59-60). The SaIne idea is expressed in Deleuze's
essay "Irnlnanence: A Life . . .  ":
Without consciousness the transcendental field would be defined as a
pure plane of immanence since it escapes every transcendence of the sub­
ject as well as of the object. Absolute immanence is in itself: it is not in
something, not to something; it does not depend on an object and does
not belong to a subject. [ . . .  In Kant] immanence is deformed and ends
up being contained in the transcendent. Immanence does not relate to
a Something that is a unity superior to everything, nor to a Subject that
is an act operating the synthesis of things: it is when immanence is no
longer immanence to anything other than itself that we can talk of a
plane of immanence.31
Just as Deleuze criticizes Kant's suppression of difference, he criti­
cizes Kant for deforming ilnlnanence and containing it within
a transcendent subject. But equally, just as the suppression of
difference conceals an original discovery of internal difference
in the fractured I, the "subjection" of ilnmanence conceals the
"unthinkable" at the heart of conceptual thought. Deep within the
transcendent subject
indeed, imlnanent to it - is the ilnpossibil­
ity of thought thinking its own being. This "unthinkable" is not an
idea to be cordoned off by reason into the reahn of rational belief;
it is an "unthinkable" that originates all conceptual thought, that
lTIUst be thought if representation and concepts are to get off the
ground in the first place.
Kant's plane of ilnlnanence reveals and conceals THE plane of
ilnlnanence, the one that
is, at the same time, that which must be thought and that which cannot
be thought. It is the nonthought within thought. It is the base of all planes,
immanent to every thinkable plane that does not succeed in thinking it.
It is the most intimate within thought and yet the absolute outside
an
outside lTIore distant than any external world because it is an inside deeper
than any internal world: it is immanence . . .  the incessant to-ing and fro­
ing of the plane, infinite movement. (W P 59)
In this to-ing and fro-ing is the rhythm of revealing and concealing.
"The nonthought within thought," the very being of thought, is pre­
cisely what Kant finds at the heart of the transcendental, which can­
not represent or conceptualize this being and so "does not succeed


Deleuze and Kant
99
in thinking it." The unthinkable is revealed in the process of the
self analyzing its own conditions - "ilnn1anent critique" but Inust
necessarily be concealed in order for critique by a self to be pos­
sible at all. The condition of possibility of critique is that the being
of thought, "that which cannot be thought," n1ust be concealed in
order to be revealed, and vice versa. Kant's critical philosophy itself
rests on this paradox.
Deleuze's own conception of what philosophy is is far too COln­
plex to SUlnlnarize here. But I want to close by suggesting that it is
related to the revealing-concealing structure at the heart of Kant's
critical philosophy. The condition of possibility of philosophy is
smnething that "cannot be thought and Inust be thought," son1ething
non-philosophical that COlnes frOln outside. The non-philosophical
is revealed in the shock of the encounter, and cannot be accomlno­
dated to representation or conceptualization. Thought can only give
itself an ilnage of what it Ineans to think, an ilnage of what it is to
be affected by the unthinkable. For this reason, "the suprelne act of
philosophy" is not "to think THE plane of iInlnanence" but to show,
in an ilnage, that it is there (WP 59-60). Philosophical "showing"
reveals "that which cannot be thought and yet Inust be thought,"
but this act of showing and thinldng the unthinkable is surely also
a conceahnent of the unthinkable spur to thought.
The dimension that shapes thought most decisively is also the dimension
that escapes thought, that thought is never quite able to bend backwards
towards its own presupposition, and Inalze its own image transparent to
itself. All that thought could ever hope to do would be to intimate its own
image, to approach it, asymptotically as it wereY
Deleuze and Guattari's What is Philosophy! shows us what philoso­
phy is by playing out the ilnpossibility and the exigency of thinking
its own unthinkable sources. Kant's Critique of Pure Reason silni­
larly shows us what philosophy is in a Deleuzian sense. This is also
what philosophy is in a Kantian sense: asking into the conditions
of possibility of thinking as such. Kant and Deleuze n1utually illu­
n1inate each another's thought through their shared COlninitn1ent
to the "unthought" that generates thought. For this reason, Kant
is crucial for understanding Deleuze, and Deleuze is a significant
interpreter of Kant.

LEONARD LAWLOR
fragility of
Deleuze
Deleuze is frequently characterized as a critic of phenomenology. Yet,
Deleuze's thought cannot be understood unless we recognize its silni­
larities to phenomenology. There are in fact three silnilarities. First,
phenomenology takes up what Deleuze in Difference and Repetition
calls "the task of Inodern philosophY"j that task is to reverse Platonisln
(DR 59). Through the epoche, phenornenology reduces any transcend­
ent world or transcendent thing in itself to a phenOlnenOnj anything
transcendent COlnes to be located within experience. Second, through
the preposition "within," we see that the reversal of Platonism
mnounts to a reduction to ilnlnanence. No greater debt to phenOln­
enology appears in Deleuze's thought than in his use of the word
"immanence." lInmanence in both Deleuze and in phenomenology
refers to a transcendental (but not transcendent) ground. A third silni­
larity appears in the fact that the grounding relation in both phenom­
enology and in Deleuze's thought is paradoxical. All transcendental
philosophy results in a paradoxical relation: the ground of experience
Inust relnain within experience (the ground Inust not be separate from
the grounded) and the ground Inust be at the san1e tirne different frOln
what it grounds (the ground Inust not reselnble what it grounds). In
other words, the ground Inust relnain ilnlnanent and yet, as ilnlna­
nent, not result in a vicious circle. It must be the case that what is
being grounded is not presupposed in the ground.
This chapter extends my "The End of Phenomenology: Expressionism in Deleuze
and Merleau-Ponty," in Leonard Lawlor, Thinking thwugh FIeneh Philosophy
(Bloomington: Indiana University Press, 2003), pp. 80-94.

LEONARD LAWLOR
It is in relation to the third silnilarity - the paradoxical rela­
tion of ground and grounded - that we can see Deleuze's specific
criticisn1 develop.I Both phenOlnenology and Deleuze seek the
elilnination of all transcendence; both seek to arrive at a plane
of in1n1anence. Despite the silnilarity however, phenOlnenology
defines iInn1anence by a dative relation; it relates the plane of
ilnlnanence back to - in this "to," we have the dative relation - a
subject or consciousness that constitutes the given (WP 46).2 In
order, however, for there to be a pure plane of iInlnanence, a plane
with no transcendence whatsoever, there Inust be no dative rela­
tion. In other words, the plane of imlnanence Inust not be an ilnage
of sOlnething else. In particular, it lnust not be an ilnage of what
it is attelnpting to ground. There lnust be a difference or a het­
erogeneity - a non-reselnblance relation (LS 99) - between ground
and grounded, between condition and conditioned, or between ori­
gin and derivative. According to Deleuze, the phenOlnenological
reduction lnerely lnoves the phenOlnenologist from the natural
attitude (doxa) back to what Husserl calls Urdoxa.3 In other words,
there is a resen1blance of opinions, with no difference between
natural opinion and proto-opinions (WP 149-50; DR 137). Despite
Husserl's attelnpt to escape, phenOlnenology falls into a "vicious
circle" (LS 105).
Deleuze's criticisln of phenOlnenology reselnbles those found
at roughly the same tilne in the works of Foucault and Derrida.4
Although Deleuze is unique among his generation of French phi­
losophers because he embraces (at least at the InOlnent of the end
of the I960s) the tenn "structuralisln," his criticism of phenomen­
ology arises, like Derrida's and Foucault's, frOln reflections on struc­
turalist concepts like the floating signifier.5 Moreover, like Derrida,
Deleuze links the criticisln of phenomenology to the criticisln of
lnetaphysics, of "the old lnetaphysics." Once again, the task of lnod­
ern philosophy is to reverse Platonisln. However, unlike Derrida,
Deleuze criticizes both phenOlnenology and metaphysics together
(phenOlnenology as lnetaphysics) by lneans of the concept of the
event: to reverse Platonisln, for Deleuze, is first and forelnost to
depose the old Inetaphysical essences with events (LS 53). Yet, the
concept of event appears not only as a reaction against phenOln­
enology and Inetaphysics. It is also a reaction against (a "counter­
effectuation" of) chaos. Deleuze's thought is always in a two-front


PhenOlnenology and lnetaphysics, and chaos
105
battle, at once against already fonned ideas and concepts (cliches)i
this is the battle against "phenOlnenology and lnetaphysics." But
his thought also battles against the undifferentiated abyss of chaos
(such as the experiences of schizophrenia, alcoholisln, and lnad­
ness disclose). Hence, the title of this essay: "PhenOlnenology and
Metaphysics, and Chaos." However, it is the subtitle
"On the
fragility of the event"
that indicates the prilnary thesis of this
essay. We shall argue that the event, in Deleuze's precise sense of
the tenn, is unlilnited (he says "eternal"). The unlirnitedness of the
event will lead us into the probleln of potency and ilnpotence, power
and inability. Despite its singularity and novelty, the event does
not endi it is incessant (Deleuze negates the French verb, cesser,
at crucial points in his discourse).6 The event has a potency that
cannot be stopped ("il ne cesse pas"). As the word "cannot" already
ilnplies, what calls forth the event's unlilnited potency is the fragil­
ity, indeed, the ilnpotence of the event, the inability to lnake it stop.
What cannot be stopped is dying. What cannot be stopped, never­
theless, lnust be stoppedi what cannot be grasped must be grasped.
And that ilnperative - given in a vision tells us that all events are
like battles. As we shall see, Deleuze wages a two-front war on cli ..
ches and chaos because he is precisely a thinker of the event as a
struggle.
We shall be able to see this two-front war develop only if we
exalnine Deleuze's 1969 The Logic of Sensei in fact, the entire fol··
lowing essay takes place within the confines of this book. In The
Logic of Sense, we find Deleuze's most explicit criticisln of phenom­
enology (of Husserl's 1913 Ideas I) coupled with his strongest appro­
priation of the idea of structure. More importantly, however, in this
book, we find Deleuze's lnost developed concept of event. In fact, as
we shall see, the concept of event that Deleuze invents in The Logic
of Sense contains four inseparable features: (I) noveltYi (2) effectu­
ationi (3) counter-effectuationi and (4) unlimitedness. It is the last
feature
unlimitedness
that will lead us to the battle. As title of
Deleuze's book indicates, we shall be able to reach the concept of
event as struggle, and therefore reach Deleuze's two-front battle,
indeed, we shall be able to reach his very concept of philosophy
(laying out a plane of ilnlnanence and creating concepts), only if we
pass through "the logic of sense." This is a logic inspired by "phe­
nOlnenology and structuralisln."

PHENOMENOLO GY AND STRUCTURALISM
The logic of sense (or the requirenlents
for a true genesis)
To write a logic of sense ("sens" is the French word, "Sinn" in Gernlan;
both ternlS lnean "lneaning" as well as "direction" and both are con­
nected to words like "sensibility") Ineans to write a transcendental
philosophy (LS lOS). Above, when we spoke of the paradoxical ground­
ing relation, we sunl1narized what we lnight call Deleuze's "principle
of all principles" for transcendental philosophy ("the principle of all
principles" being a phrase coined by Husserl). Here is the principle
in Inore detail: the ground - sense or what is expressed by a propos­
ItIOn or a sentence
Inust not be posited as existing outside of the
grounded or expression; and at the Salne tilne the ground must not
reselnble the grounded (see especially LS 21 and LS 99 for the two
clauses of the principle).? The first clause of inseparability relnoves
sense frOln "the old Inetaphysics," while the second clause of reseln­
blance relnoves sense frOln anything subjective such as universals
or general concepts and frOln anything objective like things or states
of affair. This "principle of all principles" is the principle of imma­
nence. The ground cannot be a second world, a world of essences; it
cannot be separate frOln this world; it lnust be within, ilnlnanent.
Yet, as ilnnlanent, it lnust not be ilnlnanent to anything else, not
grounded on anything, not copied off anything else. For Deleuze, this
principle is not a principle of conditioning (as in Kantian transcen­
dental philosophy), but a principle of genesis. The logic of sense is
a logic of genesis (and for Deleuze as for Derrida there is no conflict
between structure and genesis).8 Indeed, what is at issue in The Logic
of Sense is what Husserl calls "the donation of sense" or constitution
(LS 7I). What is at issue is the detennination of the "transcenden­
tal field" (LS 105) or "true genesis" (LS 98). What is required for true
genesis, according to Deleuze? Sense lnust generate the other dilnen­
sions of the prilnary elelnent in discourse, that is, the proposition. In
other words, sense nlust generate (I) the state of affairs denoted by the
proposition (denotation); (2) the signified concepts and classes of the
proposition (signification); and (3) the states of the subject lnanifested
by the proposition (lnanifestation). All of these aspects of the prop­
osition are aspects of belief, of doxa. As the genetic source, however,

sense must not duplicate doxa (or opinions). Sense Inust be neutral in
regard to all the lllodes of the proposition, and yet it must be product­

Phen0111enology (or Deleuze's criticism of Russerl's
theory of constitution in Ideas I)
Deleuze is interested in phenOlllenology because, as he says, "phe­
nOlllenology [illight] be the rigorous science" of sense for which he
is seeking (LS 2I).9 To detennine whether phenOlllenology is this
"rigorous science," Deleuze focuses, as we said, on Husserl's 1913
Ideas 1.10 Husserl seeills to "discover sense" in Ideas I through the
idea of the noeilla (LS 96, also LS 32).II As the Greek word indicates
(frOln "noein," to think), the noeilla is the thought-object, which is
correlated to what Husserl calls "noesis" (thinking). At first glance,
it seelns, according to Deleuze, that the noema - the entire appar­
atus of intentionality laid out in Ideas I - satisfies the requirements
for a true genesis. The noema in Husserl looks to be "independent"
and "neutral" because Husserl distinguishes the noelna from the
physical object, from the psychological or frOlll lived experience,
frOlll mental representations, and frOln logical concepts (LS IOI). In
other words, the noema seems to differ from denotation, manifest­
ation, and signification. It does not seem to reseillble what it is sup­
posed to generate.
As the word "seems" suggests, however, Deleuze argues that
Husserl's genesis occurs only in "appearance" (LS lao); it is a
"sleight of hand" (LS 97). In fact, Deleuze's criticism of Husserl's
genesis takes place in three steps. First, Deleuze notices that, when
Husserl discusses the noelna, he uses the illlage of a core; the noema
or sense has, according to Husserl, a nucleus.I2 As Deleuze says,
"Nucleus llletaphors are disquieting; they envelope what is in ques­
tion" (LS 98, also LS 212). What Husserl has done, according to
Deleuze, is determine the nucleus of sense as a "predicate" (LS 97).
Detennining sense as a predicate (the greenness of the tree in the
proposition "the tree is green"), Husserl understands the nucleus as
a concept or a generality. If sense is a generality, then it gives itself,
readY-lnade, the fonn of signification - rather than generating it.
The nucleus of sense, being detennined as a generality, is related, for
Husserl, to "a something = X," which is an object in general. So, as

Husserl determines the sOl11ething
X as an Idea in the Kantian
sense (an approxil11ation to an ideal). The idea in the Kantian sense
lnaintains reason as the basic fon11 of genesis. More precisely, by
111aintaining reason, Husserl seems to be presupposing "an origi­
nary faculty of COl11111on sense"i the originary faculty of common
sense accounts for the identity of the object in general (the identity
is what is held in COlnlnon by all the possible objects) (LS 97, also LS
I I6, LS I I9). According to Deleuze, Husserl even seel11S to be assum­
ing a good sensei good sense (this is the Ideas in the Kantian sense as
a telos) accounts for the process of identification of all the objects in
general to infinity (the process is always seeking the identification
of all objects as if that identification is the good) (LS 97).13 Always
seeking the same cOlnmonality, always seeking the SaIne identi­
fication, the genesis once again falls into a vicious circle. Finally,
third, Husserl maintains the form of consciousness (LS 102, also
LS 122).14 According to Deleuze, Husserl divides consciousness - a
"radical separation" between actual doxic (or believing) conscious­
ness, which is productive (it posits that something exists and makes
judgrnents), and a 111erely "thinking of" consciousness, which is
neutral and non-productive (its neutrality lneans that it posits no
existence and 111akes no judgrnents).15 Actual consciousness (or the
actual cogito) is under the "jurisdiction of reason," while the con­
sciousness that 111erely thinks is not. Deleuze stresses that Husserl
determines the relation between the two kinds of consciousness as
a relation of proper and improper and he provides an i111age of this
relation. For Husserl, the neutral, that is, il11proper consciousness
is the shadow, while the proper and rational consciousness is the
thing that casts the shadow. Thus, according to Deleuze, Husserl,
through the "separation," Inakes a "disjunction" within conscious­
ness, endowing the fonn of actual consciousness with the potency
of genesis (productivity), while the neutralized consciousness has
no productive potency. However, in order to have a genuine gen­
esis, the generating sense 111USt be at once neutral and productive.
In other words, Husserl's genetic source is not neutral in relation to
the generated forms of consciousness that are actually lTlanifested

in the proposition. Once again, Husserl falls into a vicious circle
since he rnakes one actual fOrI11 of consciousness (the rational fornJ)
