Structuralism (or true genesis)
In The Logic of Sense, Deleuze clearly takes inspiration from the
entire apparatus of intentionality that we find in Husserl's Ideas 1.
As have seen, however, there are three ways, according to Deleuze,
in which Husserl (or phenOl11enology) I11akes transcendental genesis
(constitution or sense donation) false or be only apparent. First, by
giving hiI11self the forms of the object and the concept (generality)
readY-I11ade, genesis in Husserl remains a vicious circle. Second,
by maintaining the fon11 of reason and the direction of an Idea
in the Kantian sense, Husserl presupposes a COl11Inon sense and a
good sense (the Urdoxa). Thanks to the Urdoxa, genesis functions
by means of identity and teleology. What is produced is distributed
hierarchically in relation to the identity and oriented purposively.
Again, the vicious circle appears. Finally, by detennining neutral
consciousness as only a shadow, only as improper consciousness,
Husserl lnaintains the fonn of consciousness as the Inodel for what is
generated. FundaI11entally, the criticisI11 that Deleuze levels against
the apparatus that Husserl sets up in Ideas I is that genesis is a kind
of copying. The noema is not neutral in relation to the diI11ensions
of the proposition. Based in intentionality, genesis is not an event;
sense is not a singularity. As Deleuze says, "Only when the world,
teeming with . . .  impersonal and pre-individual singularities, opens
up, do we tread at last on the field of the transcendental" (LS 103,
translation Inodified).
We now enter into one of the 1110St cOl11plex parts of Deleuze's
thought, the determination of the transcendental field. In order to
designate the transcendental field, Deleuze employs several negative


I IO
LEONARD LAWLOR
tenns: nonsense; paradox; anti-generality; informal; incorporeal;
indetennination; indifference; infinitive; a-conceptual; anonyln­
ous; inllnediate; iInpersonal; impenetrable; iInpassible; non- (or pre-)
individual; counter-God; unlilnited; and unconscious. These nega­
tive tenns function as a guardrail to steer us toward the fact that
the ground in Deleuze does not resemble - no copying relation - the
grounded. And yet, their negative function is supposed to open onto
sOlnething positive (LS 136, 123). What the negative function opens
out onto is something smaller than beliefs (doxa or opinions arrived
at by consensus); generalities; forms; bodies; smaller than deter­
Ininate, differentiated, or finite terms and concepts; smaller than
what has a name; slnaller than Inediation; persons; individuals;
than what can be penetrated; than what is either active or passive;
sInaller than God (or Inan); a liInit; and consciousness (LS 63-64).16
What is a singularity? It is no larger than a point or an instant.
Husserl, in Deleuze's eyes, relnains at the level that is too large,
as it is itself constituted by the slnaller processes of singularities.
But, insofar as Husserl does not reduce to the sIn all, he also does
not reach the really large, that which does not stop, the eventum
tantum (LS lSI).
For Deleuze, the singularities that populate the transcendental
field fonn a structure. Deleuze tells us that there are "three miniInal
conditions" for a structure (LS 50). First, there must be two heteroge­
neous series; a single series does not make a structure. Here, as to be
expected since The Logic of Sense appears in 1969, Deleuze makes
use of structuralist tenninology, saying that the one series is the
"signifier" (a repeatable phonic or graphic form), while the second
is the "signified" (what the form means). Throughout The Logic of
Sense, Deleuze refers to dualities such a signifier-signified, but also
word-thing, and, as we shall see, bodies-incorporeals. The hetero­
geneity of the two series is due to a "perpetual relative displacelnent"
or "perpetual disequilibrillln"; the two series are always "out of step
with one another" ("decalage") (LS 52-54). The "decalage" ensures
that, fundarrlentally, there is no reseInblance between the two ser­
ies. Second, as in structural linguistic, the tenns in the series are
detenl1ined in relation to one another, that is, by the difference in
value between the tenns. To these relations or to the value of these
relations "very particular events correspond, that is, singularities"
(LS 50, Deleuze's emphasis).!? Each series, then, has a distribution


PhenOlnenology and lnetaphysics, and chaos
I I I
of singular points, that is, of events. As already indicated (lnention­
ing Derrida as well), Deleuze stresses that it is a lnistake to oppose
structure to event. What a structure registers is events, and what a
structure produces is more events. Structure is genesis.
The third condition concerns genesis. The two heterogeneous
series converge toward what Deleuze calls a "paradoxical agency"
("instance paradoxale": also paradoxical instance, paradoxical last
court of appeal) (LS 40, 98).18 The inspiration for what Deleuze calls
a paradoxical agency comes frOln Lacan. One example of the para­
doxical agency, then, is a phantasln, that is, a child's real or unreal
representation of the parental coitus (LS 7, 2 10). The psychoanalytic
eXalnple indicates that the paradoxical agency is a representation of
sOlnething real but separated from that reality; it is ilnaginary too.
Another exalnple, this time frOln Levi-Strauss' structural anthro­
pology, is the "floating signifier" (LS 48-49).19 A signifier (a phonic or
graphic fonn) such as the Polynesian term "m.ana" does not possess
a determinate signified (or meaning); not having a detenninate sig­
nified this signifier "floats" between the series of signifiers (it differs
from the rest of the signifiers since it lacks a detenninate signified)
and the series of signifieds (it seems to be its own signified). The
paradoxical agency belongs, then, to neither series (neither the real
nor the ilnaginary, neither the signifiers nor the signifieds), although
it is situated between or (just above) the two series. The paradoxical
agency articulates or differentiates the two series, reflects the one
into the other, lnakes theln comlnunicate, coexist, and resonate (LS
51). In short, the paradoxical agency donates sense onto the two ser­
ies. The paradoxical agency is able to endow the two series with
sense because it is equally present in the signifying series and in the
signified series; it is "two-sided, "at once word and thing, nalne and
object" (LS 40). The paradoxical agency, by its very nature, is split
apart in relation to itself, incomplete. As Deleuze says, there is noth­
ing stranger than "this two-sided thing with two unequal or uneven
'halves'" (LS 4 1).20 What makes the agency strange is the fact that it
is in default, defective, or lacking ("defaut"). The paradoxical agency
is defective, the signifier "floats" because it lacks a determinate sig­
nified; it includes non-sense (LS 68-71).21 Since it includes non-sense,
the paradoxical agency lacks ultilnate detennination and a unique
direction or one sense (LS 77). Precisely because it lacks sense, it is
able to give too lnuch sense. The combination of not enough and too


II2
LEONARD LAWLOR
rnuch explains why, for Deleuze, the paradoxical agency is defined
by a question, a question with too Inany answers because there is
no one ultilnate answer (LS 56). The cOlnbination also explains why
the paradoxical agency is problelnatic; it is a probleln with too many
solutions because there is no one ultilnate solution. Most ilnport­
antly, the paradoxical agency "does not stop circulating" ("ne cesse
de circuler") within the two series (LS 40). It never stops, it has no
end (no detenninate direction); the paradoxical agency is unlilnited,
infinite, and therefore it has, as Deleuze says, an "eternal truth" (LS
63). Because the paradoxical agency is two-sided, it is neither denota­
tion nor signification. Lacking a detenninate sense, the paradoxical
agency is a "sOlnething = X," but this X is not an Idea in the Kantian
sense (LS 66). The paradoxical agency "destroys" both good sense
and con11non sense (LS 3). Finally, because it is a structure, and an
unlimited structure, it does not have the fonn of consciousness.
The paradoxical agency does not endow sense only apparently, but
truly. Structuralisln's discovery of the paradoxical agency results in
the idea of true genesis. It produces sense as an event. Thus struc­
turalisln for Deleuze in The Logic of Sense belongs to the Inovement
of reversing Platonisln.
WHAT IS AN EVENT?
Earlier we clailned that Deleuze had taken inspiration frOln
Husserl's phenOlnenology in his logic of sense. Then we saw how
he appropriates structuralist thinking (Lacan's psychoanalysis
and Levi-Strauss' anthropology). The real inspiration for Deleuze's
conception of sense as an event, however, COlnes frOln Stoic logic
(which he sees operating in Lewis Carroll's writings). As he says,
"the Stoics undertake the first great reversal of Platonisln, the radi­
cal reversal" (LS 7). The genius of Stoic philosophy, according to
Deleuze, lies in the new "cleavage" it Inakes in the causal relation.
Unlike Aristotle and Kant, who distinguish types of causality, the
Stoics "dissociate" the causal relation; they rnake a "border" where
there never was one before: between cause and effect. On the one
hand - this is one of the Inany dualities in The Logic of Sense we
have already noticed - there are bodies and Inixtures of bodies; the
Inixtures are the causes. The interaction between bodies is called
"lnixture" because the interaction is accidental. It is, however, the


Phenon1enology and n1etaphysics, and chaos
II3
accident that turns what happens into an event. But the accident
itself is not the event. On the other hand, there are the effects that
the causes bring about. Being dissociated frOln the causal bodies,
the effect is not bodies (LS 19). The effects of the corporeal causes
are non-bodies, what the Stoics call "the incorporeals" (LS 4). The
incorporeals are "ideal [or irreal] events" (LS 52). The Stoics not only
Inake a cleavage between cause and effect, they also n1ake a cleavage
between accident and event. Accidents are corporeal, while events
are incorporeal or ideal.22
Deleuze uses the word "event" in two ways. That Deleuze, in
The Logic of Sense, uses the saIne word to refer to two phenOlnena
indicates how closely connected (inseparably connected) the two
are. Indeed, Deleuze distinguishes between the two uses only by
Ineans of lower case and upper case: event versus Event. Event (with
an upper-case "e") is not depth; it is opposed to the abyss of chaos;
chaos is the unforeseen Inixtures of bodies, the nunbling of bod­
ies in the depths (LS 164, I06-7, 156-57). The Event is the surface
(the word "surface" appears countless tilnes in The Logic of Sense).
Insofar as it is the surface, the Event is an incorporeal. Or, Inore
precisely, it is the limit between bodies and incorporeals. Deleuze
always qualifies the Event (with an upper-case "e") with the phrase
"one and the saIne": "un seul et Inelne Evenement" (LS I I, 64, II4).
Even though the Event of the surface being laid out happens as one
and the same, it ralnifies into many dualities, Inore than the ones
that we have seen, between bodies and incorporeals, between signi­
fier and signified; there is always Inore than one surface. This clain1
about multiple surfaces explains why Deleuze says that what is at
issue is neither Inonisln nor dualism (LS 24). The ralnification of
dualities Ineans that one and the SaIne Event contains potentially
a Inultiplicity of events (now with a lower-case "e"). Written with a
lower-case "e," events are not the surface (not the Event), but "sur­
face effects" of the Inixtures of bodies. Yet, as we have seen, the
Stoics Inake a cleavage between cause and effect. What makes the
surface effects differ in nature frOln the causes is that events (still
written with a lower-case "e") in Deleuze are always events of lan­
guage (or of expression) (LS 12). The linguisticality of events is what
makes the events ideal; it is what gives them a "miniInUIn of being,"
a kind of "extra-being" (LS 22, 180, 7). The miniInUIn of being, how­
ever, is not separate being. The events do not reach the height of


I I4
LEONARD LAWLOR
Inetaphysical essences and Platonic ideas; they are not generalities
or universals. Events (with a lower-case "e") do not exist, but rather
insist. Not being facts (or bodies) and not being generalities, events
(lower-case "e"), according to Deleuze, are verbs (LS 3, 21, 214-15).
For exalnple, the event expressed in the proposition "the tree is
green" is not found in the predicate "green," but in the infinitive
"to green" (LS 214). Since the event is expressed in an infinitive, the
verb has the potency or power to divide itself into other tenses and
nUlnbers. This power - it is this power that defines a "pure event"
for Deleuze (LS 136)
makes the event unlimited. Unlilnited, the
event (still written with a lower-case "e") "follows the border" or
"skirts along the surface" (LS 10).23 Then, the event (lower-case "e")
is virtually identical to "the one and the same Event." The event is
always said twice (LS 34).
We anticipated these two senses of the event in Deleuze - Event
as surface and event as surface effect - when we spoke of the para­
doxical agency. In fact, there is no difference in Deleuze between
paradoxical agency and event. The event is paradoxical, it is two­
sided; it is always both incorporeal and corporeal, ideal and factual,
surface and surface effect at once (LS 8). Despite the doubleness,
what, first and foremost, defines the event, just as for the paradox­
ical agency, is singularity. This is the list that Deleuze produces of
what counts as a singularity: "turning points or points of inflection;
bottlenecks, knots, foyers, and centers; points of fusion, condensa­
tion and boiling; points of tears and joy, sickness and health, hope
and anxiety, 'sensitive' points" (LS 52). On the basis of the list, we
see that what Inakes sOlnething be a singularity lies in its being
caused, effectuated, or realized by Inixtures of bodies.24 Bodies Inix
and there is contagion, which causes illness; bodies mix and there is
heartbreak, which causes tears. That an event results from an "effec­
tuation" Ineans that an event is always at first an effect, always at
first a fact or an accident. For Deleuze, there can be no event, no
singularity that does not begin as an accident. What happens, what
has happened, is that, when bodies mix (again contagion or poison),
the rnixture has an effect. Then, it is possible that something of the
effect is selected (LS 151). What is selected is what there is "in prin­
ciple" or "by right" ("en droit") in the event (LS 22, 17). For instance,
and we shall return to this exarnple below, what is selected from
the accident of a wound is the idea of a scar that at once disjoins and


PhenOlnenology and n1.etaphysics, and chaos
1 15
joins. These two features - referring to a "no longer" whole and refer­
ring to a "not yet" whole - are lnade consistent in the selection.25 In
other words, the selection transfonns the factual accident into an
ideal event, becOlning neutral (indifferent) in relation to the ways
it is effectuated through bodies. Yet, because the selection of what
is "by right" is within the effect (inseparable frOln the effectL the
event relnains at once corporeal and incorporeal. Most importantly,
for Deleuze, this doubleness affects the telnporal status of the event.
The event is a singularity because it is effectuated in the present
instant. In this regard, the event is really singular, unlike anything
else, unlike any other event; it is a novelty. Yet, when the "by right"
features of the event are selected, when they are expressed, the lnin­
ilnum of being that those features acquire turn the event into a
"counter-effectuation." The event is "against" effectuation because
the features selected have, as we lnentioned earlier, "eternal truth/,
or the temporal status of being "eternal." They are "eternal" not in
the sense of an eternal present that never changes or of a circle of
tilne that constantly returns to the present. Events (lower-case "e")
are "eternal" in the sense of being non-present, that is, they refer,
like a verb, to an unlimited past and future (LS 61).26
Once more, it is necessary to stress that the event is a singularity,
a novelty. However, even though it is not separable £rOln the cause
(it is once again not a Platonic idea) - it has only extra··being - the
effect is expressed in language. For Deleuze, linguistic expression
means minilnally that the effect takes on a form. The minimUIn of
being Ineans a IniniInUIn of language, nothing Inore than the "stut­
tering" of the infinitive (LS 24). Nevertheless, the Ininilnal formal­
ization differentiates the event £rOIn the effect. When fonnalized,
the event becomes repeatable. It is a caused, factual, by chance
accident, and, at the saIne time, sOlnething that can be repeated.
The repeatability is the power or potentiality of the event. With
this power it "skirts along the surface," and becomes allnost iden··
tical to the Event (with an upper-case "e"). As ahnost identical to
one and the SaIne Event, the event becOlnes larger than any of its
corporeal effectations. Then, the fonn of the event "supervenes"
("survient") on bodies and their Inixtures (LS 24). The relationship
of supervenience Ineans that the event, now ideal, an ideal fonn,
takes on the characteristic of being an a priori condition for its
own factual or empirical effectuation. It appears to be originless or


1 16
LEONARD LAWLOR
self-originating, having no beginning, opening out onto an unliIn­
ited past. The event is, nevertheless, itself a singularity and thus
caused. The event is a first that is a second and a second that is
a first. Being caused and yet prior to its own cause, the event is a
repetition without an origin. Like the paradoxical agency, the event
is based on a lack, on nothing, on no sense. The lack explains why
Deleuze defines the event in this way: "the event is the identity of
fonn and emptiness" (LS 136). It explains his use of all the nega­
tive tenns that we have seen, but especially this one: "the infor­
Inal" (LS 107). It is fonnal (Ininilnally formal), although it lacks an
ultilnate fonn. The eInptiness or void refers to the surface having
no lilnit in the past, having no stopping point in that direction. But,
the event is always bi-directional (it has no good sense or cornInon
sense). It is a question for which the answer was not given ahead of
tilne and for which no one answer will ever fully respond. Not only
does the event supervene on the Inixtures of bodies (functioning
as their prior condition in the past), but also the event "soars over"
("survoler") the mixtures (functioning as what exceeds them into
the future) (LS ISS). In this second direction, into the future, the
event's power is excessive. It never stops being able to be repeated
beyond any liInit or over any stopping point; it is incessant and
endless (LS 40, 5 1 , 62, 63, ISO, 167). Instead of one determinate end,
it possesses an unlilnited number of ends.
CONCLUSION: PHENOMENOLO GY AND
METAPHYSICS, AND CHAOS
We are able to summarize the concept of event that we just devel­
oped in the following way. The event is a singularity; it is a novelty.
What Inakes the event new is that it is caused, accidentally or by
chance, by a mixture of bodies. The event is always effectuated.
The source of effectuation is chaos, depth, or the abyss. However,
it is possible that sOlnething of the effect is selected. The selection
of what is "by right" or "in principle" in the effect is not effectu­
ation (it is no longer what is by chance or accidentally), but counter­
effectuation. Counter-effectuation makes what was in the depth
rise to the surface. Counter-effectuation produces the surface and
the surface effects: the one and the saIne Event and ideal events.
Although above the depth, the Event and the ideal events are still


PhenOlnenology and Inetaphysics, and chaos
r r7
lower than the height of Platonic ideas and generalities. We have
seen that Deleuze's concept of ideal events reselnbles the psycho­
analytic phantasln and the structuralist floating signifier. But
Deleuze, in fact, Inodels the concept on literature. The one and the
Salne Event is a story ("une histoire") cOlnposed of turning points,
boiling points, points of crisis (LS 50). The question posed by the
paradoxical agency is the question of the tale and the novella (LS 63):
what happened, what is going to happen?27 These questions cannot
be answered with causes. Even more, we Inust recognize that the
event ilnplies that there are no ultilnate answers to these questions.
These questions remain answerless because the Event or paradox­
ical agency is "eternal" or, more precisely, unlilnited. The paradox­
ical agency is a repeatable fonn that lacks a detenninate origin.
Lacking a detenninate origin, it becomes repeatable incessantlYj it
has a potency that is endless. To be as concise as possible, we can
say that the Deleuzian concept of event contains these four insep­
arable features: (r) noveltYj (2) effectuationj (3) counter-effectuationj
and (4) unlilnitedness.28 Shnilarly, here is the list of the examples of
the Deleuzian event that we have seen so far: (r) the psychoanalytic
phantasmj (2) the structuralist floating signifierj (3) the infinitive
(the verb)j and now (4) the tale and novella.
Besides these four examples, there is one Inore. Indeed, it is the
Inost important eXalnple. We anticipated it above when we spoke of
the wound and the scar. Deleuze says, "the battle is not one exalnple
of an event alnong others . . .  [it is] the Event in its essence" (LS roo).
The battle is the Event in its essence because it fits the definition of
the event that we have seen so far. Due to the chance Inixtures of
bodies on the battlefield, each battle is novel, singular, and differ­
ent from all the others. The Inixtures of bodies cause or effectuate
the battle. But also, the battle fits the definition because the battle
"soars over" ("survole") its own battlefield (LS roo). That the battle
soars over the battlefield Ineans that it can be the subject of a coun­
ter-effectuation. The counter-effectuation (the selection of by-right
features) makes the battle be neutral in relation to all its effectua­
tions in the present, indifferent in regard to the victor and the van­
quished, the brave and the cowardly. Because counter-effectuation
takes place in language, Deleuze mentions famous novels about war
by Stendhal, Hugo, Tolstoy, and Stephen Crane. The counter-effectu­
ation in the novel lnakes the battle "eternal." It is no longer silnply


n8
LEONARD LAWLOR
the present of effectuation; instead, the battle is "always to COlne and
already passed" (LS 100). That is, as an ideal sense, battle appears to
lack an origin; but also, as an ideal sense, it exceeds all possible ful­
filhnents. Deleuze says that the non-present teInporal status makes
the event "all the Inore terrible" (LS 100). It is all the Inore terrible
because the linguistization of the battle (as in a novel), the grasping
of the battle (as in a philosophical concept), is the unlimiting of life,
or, Inore precisely, the unlimiting of life in its struggle with death.
The exaInple of the battle (but really the battle shows what the event
truly is) therefore provides us one more feature of the event. This
feature is really why it is the Event in its essence. It shows that every
event is like a plague, war, or death (LS 151).
For Deleuze, the battle's effectuation and counter-effectuation -­
indeed, effectuation and counter-effectuation in general
are like
the ambiguity of death. Here, in regard to the atnbiguity of death,
Deleuze follows Blanchot. 29 Blanchot had shown that death is not
only personal, me dying, Iny life being too weak when the moment
comes; it is not only a, so to speak, "big death." But also, death is
impersonal, without a relation to me, with me being too weak for
life which as it exceeds lilnits is like a series of "little deaths." In
other words, there is the portion of the event that is accomplished
and realized (personal death: me, as a soldier, with a proper name, I
aln dying); then there is the portion that cannot realize its accom­
plishInent (iInpersonal death: other soldiers whose nalnes I do not
know, they are dying and never stop dying). The lack of accomplish­
Inent Ineans that impersonal death is incessant: "they never finish
up with dying" ("on n'en finit pas de Inourir") (LS 152). Behind the
emptiness of the question, behind the answerlessness of what hap­
pened and what is going to happen, there is always dying. It is this
endless death that has risen up from the depths of the battle's chaos
to the surface. The surface is fragile (LS 82, 94, 120, 167). It is fragile
because the soldier is Inortally wounded, personally "in his own
flesh" (LS 101, see also LS 156). He has risked his life in the abyss of
the battle. In this moment of grace between life and death, however,
the battle in its unlilnitedness hovers above the battlefield enough
for the soldier to "see" ipo What does the soldier see? It is the vision
of so many singularities dying (not just soldiers with unknown
nalnes dying, but also animals perishing and countrysides and cities
being destroyed). It is the vision of life in its endless struggle with


PhenOlnenology and n1etaphysics, and chaos
I I9
death: chaos, or, as Deleuze sometilnes calls it, "Bichat's zone."3I It
is this vision that cannot be "grasped" (LS I56). It is this vision of
death never ceasing, never ending, never accomplishing itself, never
Inaking itself be over once and for all, this vision cannot be thought.
The inability to stop ("il ne cesse pas, ne cesse jalnais") is the impo­
tence of the event. And yet, from this powerlessness COlnes power.
The unthinkable Inust be thought, the ungraspable Inust be grasped.
Responding to the vision, the Inortally wounded soldier, "in a single
act of violence" includes all violence and all inortal events in one
single Event, in a plane of immanence that denounces and deposes
all violence and all death (LS I52-53). The Inortally wounded soldier
must write the story of the battle
in order to liberate it "always
for other times" and "to make us go farther than we would have
believed possible" (LS I6I).32 As Deleuze (and Guattari) would say in
A Thousand Plateaus, the vision of the battle is grasped (sOlne part
of it) in order to Inake us become.
Perhaps the mortally wounded soldier's vision of the battle's sur­
face (the vision of this plane of ilnmanence) is like a phenomeno­
logical intuition. We do not know.33 What we know, however, is
that Deleuze connects his criticism of phenomenology to another
criticisln. Like Derrida at the same InOlnent, Deleuze associates
phenomenology with metaphysics. Deleuze says, "Metaphysics
and transcendental philosophy," that is, phenOlnenology, "have
reached an agreement" (LS I06). Metaphysics and phenomenology
set up an alternative (LS I06). On the one hand, Inetaphysics has
God as a sovereign Being, completely and infinitely, analytically,
deterrnined by its concepts. Yet, still on the first hand, transcen­
dental philosophy (that is, phenomenology) has the finite fonn of
the Person which synthesizes representation. We see what is on
the first hand: it is God and Inan. What is on the other hand, in
distinction from God and man, is "an undifferentiated ground, a
groundlessness, fonnless non-being, an abyss without differences
and without properties" (LS I06). It is this alternative - either the
analytic form of the sovereign being plus the synthetic form of the
person or chaos - that Deleuze is criticizing, and continues to criti­
cize throughout his entire career. Only if we recognize Deleuze's
rejection of this alternative do we understand the philosophy, and
the conception of philosophy, that emerges frOln his criticisins of
both phenOlnenology and "the old Inetaphysics." Philosophy, in


120
LEONARD LAWLOR
Deleuze, is a two-front fight, against already constituted fonns (cli­
ches) and against the chaos of no fonn at all. What elnerges frorn
this two-front fight is the conception of philosophy presented in
What Is Philosophy as concept creation and the laying out of a plane
of in11nanence. But only with this two-front battle in Inind do we
see that the purpose of concept creation lies in the fight against
cliches, while the purpose of the laying out of a plane of imma­
nence lies in the fight against chaos. What is still at issue in What
Is Philosophy is surface effects and the surface. The purpose or end
of philosophy, for Deleuze, really Ineans that what is required in
philosophy is to "grasp" the event in its singularity, a singularity
that verges on the formless chaos on the one hand and on the forms
of the readY-lnade on the other. Philosophy's specific power is to
create concepts, to "grasp" the event, but this power is based on a
vision of ilnlnanence that cannot be grasped.34 As Deleuze says in
What Is Philosophy (with Guattari), "We will say that THE plane
of immanence is, at the sarne time, that which must be thought
and that which cannot be thought. It is the nonthought within
thought" (WP 59). hnitating Deleuze's capitalization of "THE plane
of immanence," we are tempted to write this final sentence: "THE
plane of iInmanence, it can NOT be thought."

Here we are focusing only on what Deleuze calls "static genesis/'
which he opposes to "dynamic genesis." In The Logic of Sense, Deleuze
says, "The expressed makes possible the expression. But in this case,
we find ourselves confronted with a final task: to retrace the history
which liberates sounds and makes them independent of bodies. It is
no longer a question of a static genesis which would lead from the pre­
supposed event to its effectuation in states of affairs and to its expres­
sion in propositions. It is a question of dynamic genesis which leads
directly from states of affairs to events, from mixtures to pure lines,
from depth to the production of surfaces, which must not implicate at
all the other genesis" (LS I86, Deleuze's emphasis). The final third of
The Logic of Sense concerns dynamic genesis (Series Twenty-Seven to
Thirty-Four). For more on genesis, see DR I83.

I I
The criticism that Deleuze presents here in The Logic of Sense should
be compared with the one Derrida presents at basically the same time.
In an eady essay, "'Genesis and Structure' and Phenomenology" (col­
lected in Writing and Diffemnce), Derrida, like Deleuze, recognizes the
innovation that the Hussedian idea of noema represents. Yet, Derrida,
again like Deleuze, thinks that Hussed retreats from this innovation
insofar as Hussed conceives of history as teleological (the Idea in the
Kantian sense). In Voice and Phenomenon, Derrida might appear at
first to be at odds with Deleuze since in this book Derrida criticizes
the Husserlian idea of expression. Yet, what Derrida criticizes is the
restriction that the Husserlian concept of expression seems to impose
on sense. In other words, like Deleuze, Derrida conceives sense as an
infinite (unlimited becoming), not to be reined in by a telos of "the
relation to an object" (the Idea in the Kantian sense again). Derrida
sees the unlimited nature of sense in what Hussed calls indication
(Anzeichen), rather than in expression. Neither Derrida nor Deleuze
is satisfied respectively with Hussed's difference between and con­
ception of indication and expression. The lack of satisfaction implies
a community of conception between Derrida and Deleuze. Notice in
this formula of a "relation to the object," we see the dative. Derrida's
criticism of "the relation to the object" implies that he does accept
the dative relation. See Jacques Derrida, Voice and Phenomenon, trans.
Leonard Lawlor (Evanston: Northwestern University Press, 20I I), p.
84. He cannot therefore be easily classified among the so-called "phi­
losophers of transcendence." The other (of any sort) is internal and not
a transcendence that puts a break (not a stopping point) on becoming.
Both Derrida and Deleuze are thinkers of infinite, continuous vari­
ation (multiplicity or dissemination). See Jacques Derrida, "'Genesis
and Structure' and Phenomenology," in Writing and Difference, trans.
Alan Bass (University of Chicago Press, I978), pp. I62-63; also Derrida,
Voice and Phenomenon, Introduction and chapter 7.

But the investigation would be guided by this comment from Foucault's
Hermeneutics of the Subject: "Meditating death (medit(ni, meletan),
in the sense that the Greeks and Latins understand this . . .  is placing
oneself, in thought, in the situation of someone who is in the process
of dying, or who is about to die, who is living his last days. The medita­
tion is not therefore a game the subject plays on his own thought, with
the object or possible objects of his thought. It is not something like
eidetic variation, as we would say in phenomenology. A completely dif­
ferent kind of game is involved: not a game the subject plays with his
own thought or thoughts, but a game that thought performs on the sub­
ject himself. It is becoming, through thought, the person who is dying
or whose death is imminent." Michel Foucault, The He1'meneutics
of the Subject: Lectums at the College de France, 1981-1982, trans.
Graham Burchell (New York: Palgrave Macmillan, 2005), pp. 359-60.
In Husserl, an eidetic variation results in an eidetic intuition. What
Foucault is implying here is that the phenomenological eidetic intu­
ition does not transform the subject doing the variation and having the
intuition. In contrast, the volitional intuition, like meditation in this
sense, transforms the subject.
34 In What is Philosophyz, Deleuze (and Guattari) compare the plane of
immanence to an intuition (WP 40).

INTRODUCTION
In the years between I966 and I969, Deleuze was close to the work

a Lacanian and a member of the Freudian school, participated fully
in the propagation of structuralism in his form of psychoanalysis.
As for Deleuze, his desire to leave the history of philosophy made
him very receptive to the ongoing tUlnult in the hUlnanities. For
hhn, the figure of the schizophrenic be carne a question in both its
clinical form and its literary fonn. But neither Deleuze nor Guattari
could be satisfied with a silnple adherence to the dOlninant theories
of the thne. Just before their encounter in 1969, the position they
were both expressing was already a lively critique of structuralisln.

which had just launched the Cahiers pour l'Analyse. Guattari

Within the lllatrix of this lecture, we find another concept, derived

the Deleuzo-Guattarian system - the concept of the "war lIlachine."
Guattari takes up, in his own manner, the Deleuzian orientation

What interests Guattari is the subject, and he sees it as split, torn,

If Guattari found material for his arglunents in the two books

He argued instead for a refusal of the alternative between structure

According to Deleuze, however, structuralism relnains a prisoner

The Stoics discovered sense along with the question of the event:

THE DELEUZE AND GUATTARI ASSEMBLAGE
The "Deleuze-Guattari assel11blage" that begins in 1969 will rad­

THE CRITIQUE OF CLAUDE LEVI-STRAUSS
The relationship with Levi-Strauss is far less polelnical than the

practice of psychoanalysis. Nevertheless, Deleuze and Guattari

Deleuze and Guattari thus reject the idea of cold societies, priIni­
tive societies without history, grounded in a simple reproduction

dOlninated by archetypes and their repetition, is particularly weak

sible for this conception, it is he who divided civilizations into hot
societies, operating on the model of thermodynainics, and cold soci­
eties, with the mechanical functioning of a watchmaker, sheltered,
through repetition, from any aleatory eleinent that rnight bring
about change.
Deleuze and Guattari also work to overturn Levi-Strauss' clas­
sic deinonstration that the universality of the exchange of WOlnen
would derive from the desire to avoid the closure of society into
itself. "Far frOln being the extension of a system at first closed, the
opening is priinary, founded in the heterogeneity of the elelnents
that compose the prestations and that compensate for the disequi­
librium by displacing it" (AO 150). Deleuze and Guattari challenge
the universal law of the prohibition of incest discovered by Levi­
Strauss, which was taken to be an inviolable law of every society in
every latitude. They argue that the very idea of such prohibition is

societies.27
They base this claim on research by Meyer Fortes on territorial
logics that are more basic than the exchange of WOlnen. "The prob­
lein is not that of the circulation of WOlnen . . . A WOlnan circulates
by herself. She is not at one's disposal, but the legal rights of the
offspring are detennined in favor of a specific person."28 The earth,
with its territorial segmentarities, is then priinary in relation to
Inarriage exchanges and parental structures. Systeins of alliances

FRAN<;OIS D O SSE
and kinship rules are thereby relegated to a second plane cOlnpared
to territorial encodings of the socius. Contrary to Levi-Strauss'
argulnent, Deleuze and Guattari state: "A kinship systeln is not a
structure but a practice, a praxis, a method, and even a strategy"
(AO 147).
The very idea of a potential closure of a kinship system is derived

of the sphere of exchange and circulation at the expense of produc­
tion and social reproduction. However, for them, once again, the
rnachinic is opposed to the structural because it is the hard link:
"The soft structure would never function, and would never cause a
circulation, without the hard Inachinic elelnent that presides over

All these activities run through lived experience in order to seg­
ment it spatially or socially, combining different types of segmen­
tarity - linear, binary, or circular. However, it is the nonstructural
Africanists such as Fortes, Evans-Pritchard, and others who have
succeeded in showing how the political systeln of primitive soci­
eties without a state has rnanaged to incorporate territorial systerns
and segmentarities, building and appropriating kinship relations

ity of primitive societies to the hard seglnentarity of modern soci­
eties. To support their thesis, they evoke the reform of Cleisthenes
the Athenian in ancient Greece, who built a political space of citi­
zenship by overcoding the lineal segments in order to produce a
hOlnogeneous space. But these two types of segmentarity are inter­
twined with each other: as Kafka showed, one can find suppleness
at the heart of the most rigid bureaucratic systems; by contrast, one

Deleuze and structuralisln
143
character of politics: "Everything is political, but every politics is
silnultaneously both a macropolitics and a Inicropolitics" (ATP 213).
The Inicropolitics that results from these observations, along with

cept with the publication of A Thousand Plateaus, where it replaces

In Anti- Oedipus, Deleuze and Guattari oppose to structural
anthropology an entire historical and political anthropology,
which is deployed in accordance with the bipolarity between the
logic of flows of decoding and the logic of processes of recoding. If
structuralisln is not a good approach for studying priInitive soci­
eties, functionalism succeeds no better when it inquires into the

their function. Ethnologists, however, are credited with being far
in advance of the psychoanalysts, who remained ensconced in
the question, "What does it mean?" To these false questions, the
schizoanalysis substitutes a focus on uses: "How it works is the
sole question" (AO ISO).
This is the question posed by Deleuze and Guattari, and it leads
them to differentiate between three types of societies: Savage,
Barbarian, and Civilized, according to degrees of deterritorializa­
tion of flows. They thus construct a genuine political anthropology
in which the driving role is attributed to the process of decoding,
which is at once both progressive and discontinuous, the weakness
of codes and the liberation of flows. At each step, the central state
structures, whether despotic or feudal, are shown to be incapable
of resisting the forces of decoding that subordinate the institu­
tional state fonns in order to enslave theln. Little by little, flows of
nUlnerous types impose their laws: "Flows of property that is sold,

italist Inachine is that it will be able to connect all these decoded

of hUlnanity is in no way presented as a new teleology, since it is
anilnated by the contingency of events that Inake social becomings

"It is certain that, even and especially in their Inanifestations of
extrelne force, neither capitalism nor revolution nor schizophrenia


144
FRAN<;OIS D O SSE
follows the paths of the signifier" (AO 244). What defines civiliza­
tions is their degree of codification or de codification of flows. What

go wrong with its policy of "the great confineinent," as analyzed

According to Deleuze and Guattari, there would therefore be

priinacy of productive Inachinery, having regained a dynarnic and

synchronic interpretations that value invariants and permanence,

diglTlatic is of little ilnportance, since it relnains infinite in its
possibilities.
Deleuze opposes to Saussurean semiotics another source of
inspiration, one that allows hiln to pursue his desire to construct
hannonics between signs, in continuity with what he had already
undertaken with Proust. When he talks about cinema, he has in lnind
a classification of signs. Announcing to his students on Novelnber
2, 1982 that he is going to continue his previous year's course on
cinema, and ruminate on it, he adds, "like a cow," he starts frOln his
intuition that he is on to sOlnething important, which he intends
to pursue through a systematic exploration of signs: "I'ln not say­
ing that it will change the world if I get to this classification, but
it will change me, and it would give me great pleasure."38 Deleuze
ambition is not to establish, in fihn, an equivalent of Mendeleyev's
table. Rather than Saussure, it is the inventor of semiotics, Charles
Sanders Peirce, who will play the role of toolkit, and who will

give a cOl11pletely different direction to Deleuze's research: a prag-

Indeed, his trilogy basically starts with the movel11ent-il11age, and

but above all, he rejects Peirce's idea of closing the system with

The I11ajority of specialists in fill11 theory gave a very critical, if

was able to accept the principle that lay at the base of Deleuze's cri­

books on their teachers. It is this type of experience that made
Jacques Aun10nt rethink his initial negative impressions
priI11ar­

of the philosophical tel11ple, concerned above all to 111aintain a mon­

VITALISM CONTR A STRUCTURE
The culn1ination of Deleuze and Guattari's joint work appeared in
the fonn of a highly classical lnetaphysical question that the two
friends seen1 to have rejected earlier by asserting the prilnacy of
"and" over "is." In I 9 9 I, however, What is Philosophy! was pub­
lished, to widespread surprise.
Philosophy shares the dOlnain of creativity with art, but here
again, their objects differ. Art is the dOlnain of affects and percepts,
which are distinguished frOln affections and perceptions by their
ability to be conserved, to go beyond the Inoments when they are
experienced. The function of art is to Inake possible this conserva­
tion and translnission beyond the finitude of existence and experi­
ence. If the philosopher creates concepts, the artist creates percepts
and affects, by various means.
The objective is to liberate vital forces wherever they are ilnpris­
oned, to rediscover their virtuality by an operation of destratifica­
tion. Insofar as there is no detenninism to be discovered on the
plane of ilnmanence, every InOlnent and every place can be a rich
source of experimentation. Whence the generalized constructivisln
suggestive of a creative "bricolage" that could push all expressions
of life into other arrangements and Ineasure whether the results are
interesting.
The fundalnental gesture consistently chalnpioned by Deleuze
and Guattari is the setting-in-lnotion of the natural, animal, and
hUlnan world. Through a close observation of the way things hap­
pen, they attelnpt to grasp Being in its becOlning. This ilnplies a
philosophical style that is always on the lookout for new assem­
blages and new concepts.

PREFACE
It is perhaps a testimony to their influence and notoriety that
Deleuze and Guattari can be located at a number of stations along
the continuUln between parody and homage. I want to briefly con­
sider three seemingly distant stations, each with its own humorous
elements, but deployed to different ends. These prefatory consider­
ations will open onto questions and demonstrations of how to char­
acterize Deleuze and Guattari's collaborations, that is, how they,
and others, posed and answered such questions, and what these tell
us about their joint work. The emphasis in this contribution is less
on the content of the books they wrote together than on characteri­
zations of their process and how these characterizations have been
deployed and redeployed to different ends.
The late French cartoonist and illustrator Gerard Lauzier (1930-
2008) was known, among other things, for his parodies of intellectu­
als and the Left, including Deleuze and Guattari. Lauzier collapsed
Deleuze and Guattari into a third figure of "Gilles Guatareuze"
(one "t" only), described tentatively by Fran<;ois Dosse in tenns of
the rather neutral "coalescence."I Dosse provides smne dialogue
frOln a single panel
the final one - of Lauzier's illustrations of
1978, and quotes Fran<;ois Fourquet, smnewhat tangentially, in order
to explain how an already fragile Guattari was deeply wounded by
such cruel comlnentary:
Mad drama. 5 policemen wounded at Saint-Tropez. Gilles Guatareuze
called the police to have his mistress interned. The least surprising wasn't
seeing the famous theoretician of antipsychiatry running after the police
chief of Saint-Tropez pleading, "You aren't going to put her at La Borde or
1 5 1


1 5 2
GARY GENOSKO
with the nice people, right! They could screw up and let her escape! They
could screw up and let her escape! No, no a serious place, right? A padded
cell and everything."2
Dosse has not actually seen these illustrations. But the cartoon
panel continues in this way: "Otherwise the next tiine she Illay tear
Ille to bits." Over the course of a few lurid color pages, the fear expe­
rienced by Guatareuze of Solange, his hysterical Illistress, whose
desire is rendered explicit as she bites and scratches hilll after he
tries to convince her that she is an iIllposter who has replaced his
true lover (the nmlle "Solange" parodies both the shepherdess-saint
and the region where La Borde is located). Guatareuze is pompous,
fat, grey-haired, and bespectacledj his arrogance is palpablej he is
Illanipulative, complaining that his desire has becOllle a gulag, that
he has been reduced to a slave. His face reddens in exasperation
at his hitherto oblivious lover before she finally attacks. The final
panel is a news report, frOlll which the above quotation is taken}
The comic strip works a nlullber of acute angles of highly vicious
parody, mocking Guatareuze as "the Lenin of the libido" and refer­
ring to trees as having "tellurian erections/' but concludes with
Guatareuze's betrayal of his own principles (even if, strictly speak<
ing, those of anti-psychiatry were decidedly not Guattari's own
since he considered it too easily recuperated by refonnist conces­
sions and its maintenance of familialisIn).
The label of anti-psychiatrist remains attached to Guattari, at
least in the popular iIllagination of French cartoonists. Lauzier is
not alone in this. Consider Pierre-Fran<;ois Beauchard - pseudonym
of David B, author of Epileptic - a graphic novel drawn in black and
white. Centered around namesake Pierre-Fran<;ois - and told frOlll
his point of view - his younger sister Florence, and older brother
Jean-Christophe, this novel recounts the fainily's struggle with
J.-C.'s seizures and the "endless round of doctors" that defined their
lives together frOlll childhood through adulthood. Faddish Illacro­
biotic treatments, hopes and letdowns, guilt and escape lllerge in
Pierre-Fran<;ois' entry into the world of drawing comics, a dirnen­
sion populated with toteill animals and Inagical beings where he
finds SOlne respite. Inspired by May 68 as an epic scene of violence
seen through the pages of Paris-Match, young Pierre-Fran<;ois at
nine years of age defines his drawing style in densely detailed bat­
tles, indebted to the psychedelic mysticisill of Illiddle-brow French


Deleuze and Guattari
153
journal Planete. At the saIne tinle, his parents struggle with another
hope - the leading lights of the anti-psychiatry lnovelnent: "The
anti-psychiatry movelnent begins, propounded in France by people
like Gilles Deleuze, Felix Guattari, Roger Gentis." Sitting in bed
Pierre-Fra11<;ois' lnother says: "These are the kind of people Jean­
Christophe should be seeing." And to this his father replies: "You
think?"4 Nothing comes of it. Much of Epileptic explores the struc­
tures of lnisrecognition in the family, as well as the stereotypes of
the series of alternative COlnlnunities and healers they come into
contact with in search of a treatment for Jean-Christophe. Including
Deleuze in the anti-psychiatry movelnent is based on a misunder­
standing, perhaps an overly hopeful interpretation of his support
for Gennan anti-psychiatrists.5 The irony is lost that Guattari's
criticisms of the lnoverrlent include that its best experilnents were
short-lived and largely a literary phenomenon.6 By the same token,
the Alternatives to Psychiatry Network in which he was active
from the lnid 1970S to the early 1990S Inade few substantive inroads
outside of Italy,? even though it exposed horrendous conditions in
psychiatric facilities that still existed in southern Europe.
There is another "Guatareuze" in circulation, but this time it
is neither in France nor a parody. Rather, it is an homage, and a
heroic one at that. As Nick Thoburn observes the Bolognese pir­
ate radio station "Radio Alice . . .  used the cOlnposite 'Guattareuze'
(double It') to characterize their practice."s In a section concerning
the characteristics of a "Ininor Inusic" in the Collectif A/traverso
textual collage Radio Alice, radio libre,9 the formulation is attrib·
uted: "as Guattareuze would say." HOlnage to Guattari, whose little
essay "Millions and Millions of Potential Alices" sought to transfer
some of the energy (with a strong coefficient of deterritorialization,
flush with politics, and collectively asseInbled enunciations) that
the free radio station catalyzed; and also to the creative deviation
of lninoritarian becOlning that Guattari developed with Deleuze,
but stretching beyond literature. While "Bifo" did not coin the word
"Guattareuze/, he recalls that it dates from the A/traverso Inaga­
zine of Septelnber 1977. Its origin remains eInbedded in a collective
enunciation.
Moving, then, frOln the caustic through the valorizing to the
awkward asseInblage. Guattari's journal entries for the period of
October 1972 are a rich source of reflections on the ilnplications of


154
GARY GENOSKO
the process of writing Anti- Oedipus. Here we find Guattari's out­
sider status - "a COlnlnon psychoanalyst and an author" - on full
display to the extent that his Inanner of working puts hiln at odds
with the "systematic acadelnic" approach Inastered by Deleuze in
the field of philosophy. Guattari asserts: "I don't really recognize
nlyself in the A.O. [Anti- Oedipus]. I need to stop running behind the
image of Gilles and the polishedness, the perfection that he brought
to the Inost unlikely book. Dare to be an asshole. It's so hard being
strapped onto Gilles! Be stupid in my own away."IO Guattari iso­
lates a residue of the process of writing with Deleuze: strapped onto
Deleuze in a way that fornls a contraption within a stereotypical
hierarchy of the extra-academic (muddy and banal) and acaderrlic
(pristine and polished) relationship. The contraption was a conse­
quence of the success of Anti- Oedipus; it is a contraption rather
than a desiring-lnachine because it is a tool-like projection ilnposed
upon Guattari by the fallout of the book; it gets between hiln and
his own practices, for instance, leading to a false distribution of
Inastery, to a second-rate identity, thus interrupting by deactivat­
ing desire, also distorting his relationship with Deleuze. Guattari
strapped onto Deleuze gives us a rattletrap. As Guattari once said in
a joint interview: "we couldn't just hook a Freudian engine up to the
Marxist-Leninist train" (DI 217). Not any old asselnblage suffices to
free desiring-production.
I NTRODUCTION
It may be true that "Genosko has done the Inost to restore Guattari
to his rightful place as equal collaborator" in his work with Deleuze,
as Julian Bourg so generously put it,II but it is a task that requires
constant attention and reinforcement. As Bifo suggests, what is
called "DG thought" is a selniocompound of Guattarian molecular
perturbations and Deleuzean ontology of events.I2 However, there
is, he insists, a Deleuze without Guattari and a Guattari without
Deleuze. But when they worked together, they set in Inotion a "rhi­
zOlnatic Inachine" to whose functioning Guattari's contributions
cannot be underestilnated and, therefore, to treat this Inachine as a
whole detennined by Deleuze alone (and with Guattari strapped on)
is not only to distort it but to suppress the collective asselnblage of
its functioning. Any denial of the two who are several who are also


Deleuze and Guattari
155
a crowd, the adlnission that begins A Thousand Plateaus (ATP 3),
diminishes the assen1blage's creativity and arrests the transversal
movelnents of its cOlnponents of passage. The insertion of a hier­
archy into the rhizOlnatic machine in the fonn of an authorial con­
traption like the acadelnic/lnilitant re-identifies who is doing Inost
of the writing. Deleuze has observed that he and Felix did not write
a book in which one played the Inadlnan and the other the analyst;
on the contrary, "we did write a book [Anti- Oedipus] in which you
no longer know who is speaking" (DI 219). The goal was to avoid
traditional dualities and not to have recourse to stereotypical hier­
archies; any effort to secret a totality into a book of flows runs it
aground on one bank.
It is useful to refer to specific acadelnic straps-ons. The use of
parentheses is cOlnlnonplace. Take, for instance, Patricia Clough's
quoting of What is Philosophy, a book apparently by "Deleuze (and
Guattari)."13 Such is a simple qualification of Guattari's authority.
And of course one winces at Slavoj Zizek's highly cliched atternpts
to separate the "Deleuze proper" from the "guattarized" books.14
Clearly, I have not done enough over titne to discourage these prac­
tices. But some solace can be taken that acknowledgelnents of the
probleln are more and Inore often published, like Nicolas Bourriaud:
"Guattari Inay still seeln significantly under-estilnated to us, and he
is often reduced to the role of Deleuze's foil, yet it does today seem
easier to acknowledge his specific contribution to the co-authored
writings."Is Easier, but no less treacherous.
Deleuze is perhaps one of the Inost insightful decoders of Guattari
as an author and as a collaborator. Considering Guattari as a group
schizo-subject consisting of an anti-Self, Deleuze figures his friend
as a "catatonic stone" (when Felix is without his glasses) who also
"lights up and seethes with Inultiple lives the InOlnent he looks,
acts, laughs, thinks or attacks" (DI 193). Pierre and Felix (Pierre­
Felix Guattari): Inilitant and psychoanalyst. It is useful to look at
the figures to which Deleuze has recourse when describing how he
worked with Guattari, for they are relnarkably consonant with his
characterization of Felix. Deleuze responds to Kuniichi Uno to this
effect: he is the unlnoving hill, with some internal Inovements,
talking little; and Felix is the rolling sea, "sparkling with light."
Deleuze then finds a sporting figure, which is rare in his writing
(the exception is tennis), that suits theln: "Together, Felix and I


I56
GARY GENO SKO
would have Inade a good SUlno wrestler" (TRM 237). While enig­
Inatic, the principles Deleuze evokes with this figure are based on
size and strength - the wrestler's body type
and lightning-quick
reflexes and power exercised within a confined space - the circu­
lar ring. Traditionally, the SUlno wrestler belongs to a stable and
lives collectively - hence, he is a group subject. Deleuze continues
with a nUlnber of contrasts: Felix tinkers with diagraIns; while he
prefers articulated concepts; their written letters to one another,
cOlllposed for Anti- Oedipus, were "disorderly" and the manner in
which each handled thein put their rhythills of work at odds with
one another; Felix had "brainstonns" and Gilles was a "lightning
rod." What Deleuze eillphasizes is the "accumulation of bifurca­
tions" in a rhizOlnatic proliferation. To which he adds, during the
process of writing A Thousand Plateaus, "resonances" established
between disciplines in which they worked; and Inutually fructu­
ous "guesses" about where they would go next. Deleuze even writes
about being "under Felix's spell" and in this state perceiving new
worlds. Deleuze's careful and iIllaginative figurations of his rela­
tionship with Guattari are consistently laudatory and insightful, yet
also seein to be resisting pressure - not frOlll the cliche of Guattari's
erasure and the rise of the first author, full credit systein but frOln
the normative force of a pairing, sliding irrevocably into the couple,
which stills process and stalls proliferation. The couple has a bad
naine in Deleuze and Guattari's thought. Every time the couple is
excoriated, there is whispering: MOllllny-Daddy and their assigned
roles in the farllily dral1la. The couple crushes 111Ultiplicity: no exit.
To be coupled by others (doubled) is a difficult fate, but to find one­
self acting like a couple (i.e. finishing each other's sentences) is even
worse. Coupling can be like a drug, Guattari thought, that people
take in order to protect theinselves frOln greater dangers; coupledom
can be hell, and it can also be productive.16
Deleuze's brief reillarks in "For Felix" are reserved for his friend's
creations, noting at the outset the "discovery and joy" of their col­
laborations, but the wish "not to talk about the books [they] wrote
together" after Felix's passing (TRM 382). Relief: not to yet again
explain their collaboration, but to focus on the singular creations
of Felix. Not to profess their mutual love, or find witness-readers to
it. But identify and probleinatize a symptOlnatic response to joint
publications read without love: referring to Anti- Oedipus, Deleuze


Deleuze and Guattari
157
once cOlnlnented that "I've wondered whether one general reason
for SOlne the hostility toward the book is silnply the fact that there
are two writers, because people want you to disagree about things,
and take different positions. So they try to disentangle inseparable
elenlents and identify who did what" (N 7). A partnership with prob­
lems is good for the business of criticisln. Bickering satisfies the
critics. Bile pollutes love.
C OUPLING ISSUES
In Anti- Oedipus machines couple according to the productive,
connective synthesis: and . . .  and then . . .  Constant coupling is the
productive force of this synthesis that connects flows and partial
objects, "continually producing production" (AO 7). When this rule
is bent and broken a contraption results, the process stalls; but this
is not an elenlent of anti-production. It is the influencing nlachine of
couplehood that persecutes partnerships (but without Victor Tausk's
reduction of Inachines to genitalia). A coupled contraption doesn't
silnply break down; rather, a couple oozes self-destruction. Intilnacy
is toxic. According to Guattari in The Anti- Oedipus Papers:
The persistence of the oedipalized couple depends on the vertigo of recip­
rocal destruction, "low heat" self-destruction through the other one's fire.
With couples, the desire for abolition makes its own laws and compromises.
The conjugal couple is the elementary social structure, in a capitalist
regime, for the exhaustion of the death drive. Its function is simultaneously
to autonomize its existence and to delilnit its rule. It's the minimmll over­
coding unit in a system based on decoded flow which, without it, would
be too disorienting. It's how people find their status, the principle of their
identity and their immanent legitimacy. The fraud of the person rests on
that of the couple and that of the couple rests on the oedipal triad.17
There is a continuUln between machinic coupling and a lTlar­
ried heterononnative couple. Deleuze and Guattari create figures
of their collaboration that deflect all efforts to push them toward
the conjugal pole, whether it is by Ineans of the critical need for
knowing who wrote what (disentangle), or the creation of cartoon
Inonsters (entangle). They resist this overcoding which infects the
representation of all partnerships under capitalisln. When Deleuze
and Guattari retort they do so in the nmne of Inachinic couplings
and asselnblagesj for Deleuze, the priority is given to nuptials rather


158
GARY GENOSKO
than couples, exchanges, binaries (D 2). Many critics find this strat­
egy disorienting and resort to interpolating themselves as the third
party who completes the couple by expressing the truth of the rela­
tion, which is generally at the expense of Guattari and, secondar­
ily, both authors. The question of how to distribute the weight of
authorial input and authority correctly still bewitches readers of
Deleuze and Guattari.
AC CORDING TO THE AMPERSAND
Any deviation frOln the intennezzo in favor of an average, or one
or the other of authors, is a betrayal of the logic of the and. In this
sense, then, Dosse's effort around how to explain the workings of
the "duo" is not designed to sweep both away, "reciprocally impli­
cated and transformed in a transversal cross-cutting."I8 Sweeping
away the question of "who wrote what" (still, Dosse wants to attrib­
ute specific concepts to Guattari like the ritornello because he was
a pianist) and restoring Guattari's "competence" through his "palp­
able marks" are laudable goals. But "both and" are never really
swept up and away in this approach. The conjunction of the "duo"
relnains too exclusive, and departures are forfeited. According to
the logic of the ampersand, then, the emphasis is placed on the and
between Deleuze and Guattari. By the time we reach A Thousand
Plateaus, the language of synthesis has been displaced by a linguis­
tics in which a redundancy of alnpersands "and . . .  and . . .  and [et]"
gets the upper hand on "is" ["est"] to introduce continuous variation
into language (D 98-99). 'And' - an "atypical expression" - works as
a tensor: "it causes language to tend toward the lilnit of its elelnents
. . .  the tensor effects a kind of transitivization of the phrase . . .  An
expression as silnple as AND . . .  can play the role of tensor for all
language. In this sense AND is less a conjunction than the atypical
expression of all of the possible conjunctions it places in continuous
variation" (D 99). Deleuze takes such care in creating figures that
describe his modus operandi vis-a-vis Guattari because in theln he
is uprooting restrictive configurations of the relation between the
two authors, elnphasizing variability and redistribution, and col­
lective enunciations. Deleuze defines the conjunction AND: "nei­
ther a union, nor a juxtaposition, but the birth of a stalnlnering, the
outline of a broken line which always sets off at right angles, a sort


Deleuze and Guattari
159
of active and creative line of flight" (D 9-10). Stumbling over "and
. . .  and . . .  and" is a way of creation for Deleuze.
For Par net, AND is a Inultiplicity and, as such, it is never alone:
"And, And, And
stmnInering. And even if there are only two
terms, there is an AND between the two, which is neither the one
nor the other, nor the one which becOlnes the other, but which con­
stitutes the Inultiplicity" (D 34-35). The line - a "narrow stream" -
between sweeps both away undoing dualisms from the inside (as
they become about "successive choices" (D 19).19 All the ANDs of
a collaboration "would appear as so Inany distorted ilnages in run­

ASIGNIFYING CREATIVITY
Another strap-on: ''I'ln strapped to this journal. Grunt. Heave. [Oct.
6, 1972]."20 At this point in 1972, Anti- Oedipus had been out for
about eight Inonths; Guattari was supposed to be contributing to
"volume 2" of Capitalism and Schizophrenia, keeping his journal
had become a burden, and he was experiencing anxiety at read­
ing the proofs for Psychanalyse et transversalite. He had becorne
an author, publishing two books in one year, his first and second.
Guattari engaged in a protracted self-analytic assessment of his
strategy of writing during this period, and whenever the occasion
arose thereafter. He expressed his worries about not producing and
being accountable for Gilles; that he was just "fucking around"; yet
that was how he worked because the energy was in the mess: Inay­
hem, bad spelling, poor expression, arguments to hide behind; Inul­
tiple beginnings. "I'm a sort of inveterate autodidact, a do-it-yourself
guy, a sort of Jules Verne
Tourney to the Centre of the Earth. In
my own way I don't stop . . .  But you can't tell. It's the never ending
work of reverie. Lots of ambitious plans. Everything in my head,
nothing in the pocket."2I Guattari's explanations are full of deni­
als - "I'ln not a philosopher" - and colorful characterizations and
alignInents. Around 1982, while in Brazil, Guattari explained how
he worked in contrast to Deleuze: "I try out ploys, like people who
try to rob banks - I venture into maneuvers of expression in a cer­
tain context, in a certain situation. Later, I abandon it all and go and
do sOlnething else."22 Guattari's reticence about producing Ineaning
effects is worked though in his desire to diagram. The plan without


I60
GARY GENOSKO
the heist; the outline without the text. If I stole Felix, and I hope
he did the Salne for Ine," Deleuze once said, invoking constructive
thievery in the conceptual reahn (D I7). Scrunched-up papers in a
dustbin. Restlessness at his writing desk. These deterritorialized,
Inachinic signs conjoin directly with other signs and objects and
affects, but without the crutches of representation and signification
and the anchor of an individuated subject. Guattari's diagrains oper­
ate whether or not anybody, including Deleuze, can Inake sense of
thein. They generate ideas and objects: little Inachines of invention
that relate to one another by triggering interactions.
Taking a cue frOln Deleuze's description of how his book Dialogues
with Claire Parnet "nlade a new point which Inade possible a new
line-between" (D ix), in addition to which it fell between Anti­
Oedipus and A Thousand Plateaus (not to Inention Kafka in I975),
which were themselves between hiinself and Guattari, Guattari is a
Inonlentary point of subjectification, as an author with his nanle on
a book contract, caught up in the seiniotic and Inaterial flows that
spirit him away in a plurality of articulations. He is also between
Anti- Oedipus and A Thousand Plateaus by way of Psychanalyse
et transversalite, intersected by his journal entries and letters to
Deleuze. Muddled lines fonning rhizomes between the points and
carrying thein away. Stammering, stuinbling, drawing diagrams,
unable to get down to writing the next text.
ETHOLO GICAL BECOMINGS
Ethology is the scientific study of processes of animal behavior,
favoring field observation, and a subdiscipline of evolutionary biol­
ogy. The ideas of its Austrian founders Nikolaas Tinbergen and
Konrad Lorenz were influential in postwar anthropology and spread
via Levi-Strauss in France. In The Machinic Unconscious, Guattari
approaches ethology in order to criticize its mechanistic behavior­
ist assuInptions and hierarchical arborescent logics by means of the
insertion of refrains (rhythms of teinporization and territorializa­
tion and facility traits like silhouettes) that precipitate innovations
(rhizOlnic Inutations) in and between the selniospheres of animals,
and aniinals and plants. Guattari further rethinks territory in
nonhuman anilnals by describing the open asseinblages of COln­
ponents, some of which becOlne non-passively expressive and give


Deleuze and Guattari
161
consistency to social relations between the san1e and different spe­
cies. Instead of explaining territory by Ineans of innate Inechanisn1s
and the need to satisfy the big drives (hunger, sex, flight, aggression),
Guattari turns to expressivity as surplus value of code (beyond the
SUIn of genetic encoding, ecological adaptation, and social COlnn1U­
nication) as a source of innovation, that is, as art, which "perhaps
begins with the anilnal, at least with the anilnal that carves out
a territory and constructs a house . . .  The territory-house systeln
transforn1s a nUInber of organic functions
sexuality, procreation,
aggression, feeding. But this transfonnation does not explain the
appearance of the territory and house; rather, it is the other way
around" (WP 183).
To the extent that Guattari's The Machinic Unconscious Inay
be read as a workbook for A Thousand Plateaus, the eInphasis on
ethology plays a vital role as a "very privileged Inolar domain for
delnonstrating how the Inost varied cOlnponents . . .  can crystallize
in asseInblages that respect neither the distinction between orders
nor the hierarchy of fonns. What holds all the c0111ponents together
are transversals, and the transversal itself is only a cOlnponent that
has taken upon itself the specialized vector of deterritorialization"
(ATP 336).
Falnously, the aparallel evolution of "the wasp AND the orchid"
(D 7) has becOlne for smne a description of Deleuze and Guattari's
work together.23 Guattari writes:
It is known that the wasp, effectuating a simulated sex act with a morpho­
logical and olfactory lure constituted by the rostellum of the orchid, after­
wards releases and attaches the pollen that it transports onto other plants
. . .  The ensemble of the transcoding systems authorizing these round-trip
tickets between the vegetable kingdom and the animal kingdom appears
completely closed to any individual experimentation, training, or innov­
ation . . .  Nothing would be gained by reducing a symbiosis like that of the
wasp and the orchid to a simple "attachment" between two heterogeneous
worlds. This encounter produces what I called elsewhere a "surplus-value
of code" . . .  the new symbiotic assemblage actually functions like a mutant
wasp-orchid species evolving on its own account and redistributing the
genetic and semiotic components selected from both original species
according to its own standards . . .  thus a new evolutionary line of flight is
launched on the bio-ecological rhizome which is in other respects immedi­
ately masked by the genetic encodings which delimit it.24


162
GARY GENO SKO
Borrowing fron1 studies of the wasp and the orchid, baboon-troop COln­
Inunication, and tool usage by various birds, Guattari interpolates
freedOln, in the fonn of sen1iotic expansion, into closed behavioral
sequencing. I1nprovisation and individual initiative helps to deterri­
torialize a given asselnblage (i.e. courtship rituals) and establish new
transversal co-relations between the Inost and least detenninistic
components. Guattari wanted to expose ethology's "lnisunderstand­
ing" of linear causality (higher to lower) and its binarislns (inhib­
itors-innate releasing Inechanislns) through the non-transcendent
entanglelnents of cOlnponents of assemblage. Guattari's goal was to
complexify ethological logic rather than sirnply reverse its priorities
or substitute one hierarchy for another. He accOlnplishes this task in

and by injecting psychoanalytic, selniotic, and political concepts as

The relationship between the wasp AND orchid does not adequately
describe the work of Deleuze and Guattari. The selectively is far
too exclusive. The elnphasis is on the "and" hence on the new lines
thrown out into the rhizOlne beyond the conjunction; on what "each

becOlnings. Aparallel evolution, Deleuze (D 18) explains, happens
between animals, between ideas, not between persons.
FLYING PAPERS
In a review of Guattari's The Anti- Oedipus Papers, philosopher
Charles T. Wolfe relnarked:
[W]hat exactly is in this book? Most notably, the "matrix" for one of

Oedipus with Deleuze. These writings include annotated notes and

ciSlns of bad psychoanalytic clinical habits. Guattari often responds

terizations of how Guattari approached concepts punctuate expla­

entries provide insight into his self-analysis and how he thought

CONCLUSION
The Inany exalnples and details discussed in this chapter nlay be
connected together by a nUlnber of ANDs in order to create the
kind of rhizOlne with a particular logic discussed throughout, in
addition to the limitations on connectivity in the defense of prac­
tical, juridical reason. The lines of connective alliances include the
authorial collaborations of Deleuze and Guattari, which are them­
selves Inodified and expandable by other ANDs. The logic of AND

remake. Yet the war machine has both positive and negative sides:
affinnation and reaction.34 The fornler is often integrated into the
collaborative books themselves as a statelnent of how Deleuze and
Guattari understood their collaborations (subjects do not enunci­
ate; collective asselnblages enunciate); at other tilnes their expla­
nations are reactive in the sense that they answer the questions of
those looking for secrets, for evidence, for the statelnent that will
Inake an interview, for the bare author-subject. There are SOlne very
ugly mOlnents in the universe of reception that clog the collective
asselnblages, but also some relnarkable creations as well (as we have
seen, figures of the sunlO, ocean waves, running water, thievery).
Why is it ilnportant to continue considering the ways in which the
relationship between Deleuze and Guattari have been characterized

unresolvable tension between the nalne, signature, and identity, and

narne as pseudonYln for philosophical conceptual characters is no


Deleuze and Guattari

INTRODUCTION
Deleuze's engagen1ent with ethics - both his specific Inonographs
on Spinoza's thought and the n10re extensive engagelnent with the
ethical ilnplications of affinnative nOlnadic ontology throughout
his work - constitutes the core of his philosophy. This claim needs

ethics of freedOln and affinnation offers a robust reply to the doxa­
driven belief that any attelnpt at challenging or decentering the

The ethics of affinnation, with its einphasis on lnoving across the

ROSI BRAID OTTI
What is positive in the ethics of affirmation is the belief that

An ethics of affinnation involves the transformation of negative

put it. The practice of transfonning negative into positive passions
is the process of reintroducing time, movement, and transformation
into a stifling enclosure saturated with unprocessed pain. It is a ges­

the injustice. The displacement of the hurt is achieved through a
sort of de-personalization of the event, which is the ultimate ethical
challenge.
Moreover, the ethics of affirmation is about suspending the quest
for claims and compensation, resisting the logic of retribution of
rights and taking instead a different road. In order to understand


NOlnadic ethics
this Inove it is important to de-psychologize the discussion of

or the notion of potentia as the affinnative aspect of power. It is joy­

the tenns and Inodes of its expression. This Ineans concretely that

unable to sustain it. Affirmation is therefore not naIve optimism
or Candide-like unrealisln. It is about endurance and transfonn­
ation. Endurance is self-affinnation. It is also an ethical principle
of affirmation of the positivity of the intensive subject
its joy­
ful affirmation as potentia. The subject is a spatio-temporal com­
pound which fralnes the boundaries of processes of becOlning. This
works by transforming negative into positive passions through the

logocentric set of standards, but is rather unhinged and therefore
relational.
This sort of turning of the tide of negativity is the transfonIlative
process of achieving freedOln of understanding through the aware­
ness of our lilnits, of our bondage. This results in the freedom to
affinn one's essence as joy, through encounters and Iningling with
other bodies, entities, beings, and forces. Ethics lneans faithfulness
to this potentia, or the desire to become. Deleuze defines the latter
with reference to Bergson's concept of "duration," thus proposing

tainable changes and transfonnation and enacts theln around hiln/
herself in a comlnunity or collectivity. Affinnative ethics rests on
the idea of sustainability as a principle of containlnent and toler­
able developlnent of a subject's resources/I understood environlnen­
tally, socially and psychically, as argued by Felix Guattari in his

tense of continuous "becOlning." Endurance has therefore a teln­

and self-perpetuation. But it also has a spatial side to do with trans­

tion of passions or forces. It evolves affectivity and joy, as in the

The point, however, is that extrelne pleasure or extrelne pain -
which Inay score the saIne on a Spinozist scale of ethology of

sion about duration in tilne. This is linked to lnemory: intense

ilnpact of painful events fixes them in a rigid, eternal present tense
out of which it is difficult to emerge. This is the eternal return of

They are also, however, paradoxically difficult to remelnber, insoȯ

Psychoanalysis had shown the way through the notion of the

sis
by stressing the structural function played by the negative,

This makes for two radically divergent conceptions of time and

tivity.26 This kind of ethics addresses the affective structure of pain

In a nomadic, Deleuzian-Nietzschean perspective, ethics is essen­

Contrary to the traditional lnorality that follows a rationalist and

LIMITS AND T HRESHOLD S
The dissolution of the hard-core self of liberal individualism is a

tingency. It also propels the subject, however, to act according to
this awareness. The result of the confrontation with the lilnit (the
limit-experience) is the transfonnation of the subject's relation to
knowledge and to itself as a knowing subject. The lilnit experience
accounts for the conversion of the subject into something else. This
is the ethical lnOInent.
The later Foucault argues, contrary, for instance, to Deleuze,
that the question of the lilnits of the philosophical subject, which
is operationalized through Bataille, was already raised by Kant's
critical thought. This is expressed in both Preface to Transgression
and in Foucault's genealogy of the human and social sciences in
