dOInain of ethics to knowledge and cognition in the sense of forces
that activate a subject's capacity to act upon itself and others (poten­
tia). This is self-styling or auto -poiesis as productive self·creation.
Ethics as praxis.
Ethics is about freedOIn from the weight of negativity, freedOIn
through the understanding of our bondage. A certain alnount of
pain, the knowledge about vulnerability and pain, is actually use­
ful. It forces one to think about the actual Inaterial conditions of
being interconnected and thus being in the world. It frees one frOIn
the stupidity of perfect health, and the full-blown sense of existen­
tial entitlelnent that cOInes with it.
What is ethics, then? Ethics is a thin barrier against the possi­
bility of extinction. It is a Inode of actualizing sustainable fonns of
transformation. This requires adequate asselnblages or interaction:


N Olnadic ethics
one has to pursue or actively create the kind of encounters that
are likely to favor an increase in active becOlnings and avoid those
that diminish one's potentia. It is an intensive ethics, based on the

tion, and coordination or resonance. It is a lnatter of unfolding-out
and enfolding-in the cOlnplex and lnulti-layered forces of bios-zoe as
a deeply inhulnan force.
In other words, potentia, in order to fulfill its inherent positivity,
lnust be "fonnatted" in the direction of sustain ability. Obviously,
this lneans that it is ilnpossible to set one standard that will suit
all; a differential approach becOlnes necessary. What bodies are cap­
able of doing or not is biologically, physically, psychically, historic­
ally, sexually, and emotionally specific: singular and hence partial.
Consequently, the thresholds of sustainable becomings also lnark

statement, not the assertion of defeat. It is the lyrical lament of

open fields of self-knowledge in the encounter of and configuration

Deleuze has an ahnost mathematical definition of the limit, as
that which one never really reaches. In his Abecedaire, Deleuze

however, usually shoots straight for the last one. That gesture pre­

T HE ETHICS OF DE-PERS ONALIZATION
Pain in our culture is associated to suffering by force of habit and
tradition and is given negative connotations accordingly. Supposing
we look a bit more critically into this associative link, however:
what does pain, or suffering, tell us? That our subjectivity consists
of affectivity, interrelationality, and forces. The core of the subject is
affect and the capacity for interrelations to affect and to be affected.
Let us agree to de-psychologize this discussion from this lnoment
on, not in order to deny the pain, but rather to find ways of working
through it.
This vision of ethics involves a radical repositioning or internal
transformation on the part of subjects who want to becOlne-lninor­
itarian in a productive and affinnative lnanner. It is clear that this
shift requires changes that are neither simple nor self-evident.
They mobilize the affectivity of the subjects involved and can be
seen as a process of transfonnation of negative into positive pas­
sions. Fear, anxiety, and nostalgia are clear exmnples of the nega­
tive emotions involved in the project of detaching ourselves from
fmniliar and cherished forms of identity. To achieve a post-identity
or non-unitary vision of the self requires the dis-identification from
established references. Such an enterprise involves a sense of loss of
cherished habits of thought and representation, and thus is not free
of pain. No process of consciousness-raising ever is.
The beneficial side-effects of this process are unquestionable and
in some way they cOlnpensate for the pain of loss. Thus, the felnin­
ist questioning and in smne cases rejection of gender roles triggers
a process of dis-identification with established fonns of lnasculin­
ity and felnininity, which has fueled the political quest for alterna­
tive ways of inhabiting gender and embodying sexuality.28 In race
discourse, the awareness of the persistence of racial discrimin­
ation and of white privilege has led, on the one hand, to the critical


Nornadic ethics
reappraisal of blackness29 and, on the other, to radical relocation of
whiteness.3°
In a Spinozist vein, these are transfonnative processes that not
only rework the consciousness of social injustice and discriinination
but also produce a nlore adequate cartography of our real-life con­
dition, free of delusions of grandeur. It is an enriching and positive
experience which, however, includes pain as an integral eleinent.
Migrants, exiles, refugees have firsthand experience of the extent
to which the process of dis-identification frOln fainiliar identities
is linked to the pain of loss and uprooting. Diasporic subjects of all
kinds express the saIne sense of wound. Multi-locality is the affirma­
tive translation of this negative sense of loss. Following Glissant,
the becoming-nomadic Inarks the process of positive transform­
ation of the pain of loss into the active production of Inultiple forms
of belonging and cOll1plex allegiancesY What is lost in the sense of
fixed origins is regained in an increased desire to belong, in a Inul­
tiple rhizOlnic Inanner which transcends the classic bilateralism of
binary identity fonnations.
The qualitative leap through pain, across the InOlunful land­
scapes of nostalgic yearning, is the gesture of active creation of
affinnative ways of belonging. It is a fundaInental reconfiguration
of our way of being in the world, which acknowledges the pain of
loss but Inoves further. This is the defining Inornent for the pro­
cess of becOlning-ethical: the move across and beyond pain, loss,
and negative passions. Taking suffering into account is the starting
point; the real aiin of the process, however, is the quest for ways
of overcOlning the stultifying effects of passivity, brought about by

possibility for ethical transfonnation. Clearly, this is an antithesis
of the Kantian Inoral imperative to avoid pain or to view pain as the
obstacle to Inoral behavior. Nomadic ethics is not about the avoid­
ance of pain; rather it is about transcending the resignation and pas­
sivity that ensue frOln being hurt, lost, and dispossessed. One has to
becOlne ethical, as opposed to applying moral rules and protocols as
a form of self-protection. Transformations express the affinnative
power of Life as the vitalisin of bios-zoe.
The sobering experience - the hUlnble and productive recogni­
tion of loss, liinitations, and shortcOlnings has to do with self-rep­
resentations. Established mental habits, iinages, and terminology

railroad us back towards established ways of thinking about our­

BECOMING ETHICAL: O N  S USTAINABILITY
What is, then, the subject of ethical affirmation? It is a slice of liv­

tia (rather than potestas) - neither by the will of God, nor the secret
encryption of the genetic code - and yet this subject is elIlbedded

This idea of the "faithfulness" of the subject is ilnportant and it

NOlnadic ethics
This "faithfulness to oneself/, consequently, is not to be understood

city of a self ("me, myself and I") that is a clearinghouse for nar­

one's multi-layered subjectivity, binding the cognitive to the emo­
tional, the intellectual to the affective and connecting the In all to

acity to affect and to be affected. Translated into a telnporal scale,
this is the faithfulness of duration, the expression of one's continu­
ing attachment to certain dynalnic spatio-telnporal coordinates.
In a philosophy of telnporally inscribed radical ilnlnanence, sub­
jects differ. But they differ along lnaterially embedded coordinates,
because they come in different mileage, temperatures, and beats.
One can and does change gears and move across these coordinates,

tainability. By latitudinal forces Deleuze rneans the affects a sub­
ject is capable of, following the degrees of intensity or potency: how

In other words, sustainable subjectivity reinscribes the singular­

into the lnatrix." On the contrary, to lnake sense of this intensive,
lnaterially embedded vision of the subject we need a sustainability

threshold or fraIne. The containlnent of the intensities or enfleshed

A nOlnadic Deleuzian ethics prioritizes relation, praxis, and COln­
plexity as the key components and it accordingly prOlnotes a triple
shift. Firstly, it continues to emphasize a radical ethics of transform­
ation in opposition to the moral protocols of Kantian universalisIn.
Secondly, it shifts the focus frOln a unitary and rationality-driven
consciousness to ontology of process, that is to say, a vision of sub­
jectivity that is propelled by affects and relations. Thirdly, it disen­
gages the elnergence of the subject from the logic of negation and
attaches subjectivity to affinnative Otherness - reciprocity as cre­
ation, not as the recognition of Salneness. This results in renewed


N on1.adic ethics
I95
eInphasis on affirn1.ation as the politics of life itself, as the genera­
tive intensive force of zoe.
In response to the charge of Inoral relativisn1., I have en1.phasized
the central role of sustainability in nOlnadic ethics. Sustainable eth­
ics allows us to contain the risks while pursuing the original pro­
ject of transforn1ation. This is a way to resist the don1inant ethos
of our conservative tilnes that idolizes the new as a consun1.erist
trend, while thundering against those who believe in social change.
Cultivating the art of living intensely in the pursuit of change is
a political act. In this regard, I have insisted on the iInportance of
endurance - in the double sense of learning to last in tiIne, but also to
put up and live with pain and suffering. Again, it is a question of dos­
age and of balance. Thresholds of sustainability need to be Inapped
out, so that a rate and speed of change can be negotiated and set that
will allow each subject to endure, to go on, to stop at the second-last
slnoke, shot, drink, and book. This iInplies a differential type of eth­
ics, which clashes with don1inant n1.orality but contains criteria for
the section of the ethical relation and a regard for the lin1.its. These
need to be set by experilnentation with the collectively shared inten­
sities of a cOlnInunity that longs for the activation of affinnative
forces and hence require careful negotiations. The eInbodied struc­
ture of the subject is a lin1.it in itself, though lin1its in Deleuze's phil­
osophy are just the threshold of sustainable changes.
The key ideals of this ethics of freedOln are, firstly, the focus on
self-detennination or self-styling through the very acts of resistance
or transgression. This is in contrast to the juridical conception of
freedOln as a set of universal rights or entitlelnents. Secondly, this
idea of freedOln eInphasizes critical analysis and constant question­
ing. This is linked to the notion of governInentality in the sense of a
general organization of knowledge and of disciplinary apparati that
produce Inodes of subjectivity.36 The lesson of Spinoza about the
structurally repressive function of the state in relation to the project
of realizing the conotus is also relevant. This tradition of thought,
to which Toni Negri also belongs, is wary of the institutions that
govern us. Thus vigilance is the price of freedOln; it is the task of
the critical thinkers, as analysts of power, to assess the conditions
that are conducive to social change, as opposed to the en1phasis on
unchangeable factors.

Thirdly, the issue of self-scrutiny cannot be separated frorn the

resistance can be seen as a web of elnancipatory practices. Localized
and concrete ethical gestures and political activities Inatter Inore

a fonn of ethical praglnatisln.

Much of Deleuze's work, especially the books co-authored with
Guattari, has been read as political philosophy. I Deleuze clearly

space of the political."2 However, not everyone accepts this image

that "not a single one of Deleuze's own texts is in any way directly
political; Deleuze 'in hilnself' is a highly elitist author, indifferent
toward politics."3
Alain Badiou outlines several reasons for doubting whether
Deleuze was first and foreinost a political thinker and even whether
there is such a thing as a Deleuzian politics.4 A first difficulty is
that Deleuze never identified the political as a specific object or
domain of thought, in the saIne way that, in What is Philosophy?,
he singled out art, science, and philosophy.5 A second, Inore subject­
ive difficulty is that Deleuze was never very interested in politics.
In his solo writings, he never claimed that politics determined his
philosophical activity. Unlike Inany of his contemporaries, such
as Althusser, Derrida, Lyotard, or Nancy, he never argued that the
prilnary purpose of philosophy was political. On the contrary, he

tended towards a Inore traditional view of philosophy as the cre­
ation of concepts and suggested in a I985 interview that he was
interested in "the relations between the arts, science and philoso­
phy" (N I23).
A third difficulty concerns the content of Deleuze's political

becOlning and pure events that is the ultilnate source of creativity

The thing is, I became more and more aware of the possibility of distin­
guishing between becoming and history. It was Nietzsche who said that
nothing ilnportant is ever free from a "nonhistorical cloud." This isn't to
oppose eternal and historical, or contemplation and action: Nietzsche is

For Deleuze, it is becon1ings that are the real object of philosophy.
Philosophy as it is defined in What is Philosophya creates concepts

of Deleuze as a theophantic thinker of unworldly creation, to the

for any deterrninate political activity whatsoever.? In COlTIlTIOn with

1993 that his next and last book would be called "The I1nportance

Only after this social and political upheaval, and a long period of

(WP 99). This conception of philosophy as utopian engagement with

activity envisaged and the precise sense in which this is political

MARXIST POLITICS OR ANTI-POLITICS ?
In answer to the first set of questions about the kind of politics
envisaged, we can begin by noting that, like nlany of their compa­
triots lnobilized by the events of 1968, Deleuze and Guattari were

conditions of Inaintaining society as a fair systeln of cooperation

however, they were less interested in the capture of state power

PAUL PATTON
than in the qualitative changes in individual and collective iden­
tities that occur alongside or beneath the public political dOlnain.
In their view, all politics is sil1Tultaneously a Inacropolitics that
involves social classes and the institutions of political governlnent

ibility, affect, and allegiance. However n1uch they borrowed frOln
Marx's analysis of history and capitalisn1, their own work focused
on the individual and collective fonns of desire that constitute the
Inicropolitical din1ension of social change. This focus on the polit­
ics of desire led theln to abandon key tenets of Marxist social and
political theory such as the concept of the party as a revolution­
ary vanguard and the philosophy of history that sustained Marxist
class politics. They proposed a non-teleological conception of his­

izing as well as the reterritorializing aspects of capitalisln. They
insisted that the in1petus for social change was provided by move­
Inents of deterritorialization and lines of flight rather than by class
contradictions. Their rejection of the organizational and tactical
forn1s of traditional Marxist politics is definitively expressed at the
end of Dialogues when Deleuze and Parnet abandon the goal of

becOlning of people throughout society: "why not think that a new

all kinds of Inutating living Inachines conduct wars, are cOlnbined
and trace out a plane of consistence which undennines the plane of

cept of revolutionary-beco111il1g sought to el1COlnpass the Inultitude
of ways in which individuals and groups deviate frOln the 111ajor­
itarian nonns that ultilnately detennine the rights and duties of
citizens.
The fact that Deleuze read Marx during the I960s alongside
Nietzsche perhaps explains SOlne of the anti-political thelnes that
occasionally 111anifest thelnselves in his work (N 5I). There is no dis­
cussion of Nietzsche's views on politics and the state in Nietzsche
and Philosophy, only C01111nents on his theory of culture and, at the

Deleuze's political philosophy
203
axiological priority of the active over the reactive and the affirrna­
tive over the negative, the argun1ent Inight run along the following
lines. Power is fundalnentally active and relational, appearing in the
interaction between different kinds and degrees of force. In the state
of nature, individual and collective bodies collide in the pursuit of
their activities. However, purely chaotic interaction is not a state of
social existence: at best, life under such conditions will be uncer­
tain, at worst it will be brutish and short. Hence, it can be argued,
the overriding aim of political governinent is the establishment
and Inaintenance of relatively stable fonns of interaction. Social
relations require the stabilization and fixation of certain fonns of
interaction, including the institution of fonns of government which
enable stable and predictable fonns of action upon the actions of
others. In these terms, government is a fonn of action upon indi­
vidual or social forces which seeks to limit or constrain their pos­
sibilities for action. FrOln the perspective of the forces governed,
the governinent of individual and collective bodies is essentially
reactive. Deleuze and Guattari's account of the state as a process of
capture operating upon the priinary flows of Inatter and activity in
the social field renders explicit this reactive character of the polit­
ical apparatus. The state, they argue, captures flows of population,
cominodities, or Inoney in order to extract fronl these flows a sur­
plus which then beconles a Ineans to Inaintain and enhance its own
power. It is an institution whose primary Inode of operation is one
of liinitation or constraint, a Inatter of separating active forces frOln
w hat they can do. In these tenns, the state is by definition always a
secondary formation, and the political sphere is always reactive by
nature. In this way, the arguinent sketched above would lead to a
fundamentally anti-political orientation.
Eleinents of such an orientation may be found in Difference
and Repetition, for eXalnple, in the form of Deleuze's defence of
the singular against the general, the individual against the herd,
and a resistance to forms of equality and equalization. This "anti­
political" theIne emerges frOln the conception of the social field
(like every other) as a field of free differences and the rejection of
representation: every tiine there is representation there is always
"an unrepresented singularity" who does not recognize himself or
herself in the representant. Hence the Inisfortune of speaking for
others (DR 52). Elsewhere in Difference and Repetition, Deleuze


204
PAUL PATTON
points out that for every philosophy that begins from a subjective
or ilnplicit clailn about what everybody is supposed to know there

The theory of capitalist society outlined in Anti- Oedipus estab­
lishes a fundarnental dualisin within capitalist society between the
deterritorializing tendency of capital and the necessary reterritori­
alization effected by the state and its agents. Moreover, Deleuze and

but in the opposite direction, pursuing ever further the Inovelnent of
decoding and deterritorialization (AO 239-40). Much of the analysis
of capitalism in Anti-Oedipus supports such a reading. For exainple,
the authors describe the capitalist axiomatic as a system of enslave­
ment in which all are subject to the constraint of its axiOlns. By
contrast with the form of slavery established by the Despotic state,
which at least retained an apparatus of anti-production distinct

capitalisin installs "an unrivalled slavery, an unprecedented sub­
jugation" in which "there are no longer even any masters, but only
slaves cOlnlnanding other slaves" (AO 254). Here, there is only one
class, and bourgeois and proletarian alike are slaves of the social
machine. In contrast, Deleuze and Guattari point to "the revolu­
tionary potential of decoded flows" and suggest that the opposition
to this machine which is relevant frOln the point of view of revolu­
tionary politics is not that between capitalist and worker but that

Other eleinents of Deleuze and Guattari's Inature political phil­

of the capitalist social inachine do not siinply repress a natural state
of free and undirected social existence. They are also constitutive

Romantic anarchists who believe in a reahn of social being beyond

Deleuze's political philosophy

axioms" (ATP 463). The reason is not siinply that the conditions

MICROPOLIT ICS, F ORMAL NORMAT IVITY, AND
D ETERRITORIALIZAT ION
Deleuze and Guattari's political philosophy does not confonn to the
disciplinary nonns of anglophone nonnative political philosophy or
German Critical Theory. For the Inost part, the concepts developed
in Anti-Oedipus and A Thousand Plateaus do not directly address
the Inacropolitical public dOlnain, lTIuch less the normative prin­
ciples on which this should be based. They consider the different
fonns of modern government only from the Marxist perspective of
their subordination to the axiOlns of capitalist production. FrOlTI
this point of view, authoritarian, socialist, and liberal delnocratic
states are considered equivalent to one another insofar as they func­
tion as models of realization of the global axiomatic of capital. They
allow that there are important differences aITIong the various mod­
ern fonns of state but provide little discussion of these differences.
They affirm the importance of changes to regiInes of public right
that come about through struggles for civil and political rights,
for equality of econOlnic condition and opportunity, as well as for
regional and national autonOlny. However, they offer no normative
theory of the basis of such rights, nor of the kinds and degrees of
equality or regional autonOlny that should prevail (ATP 470-7I).
They offer no justification for the establishlnent of basic civil and
political rights, for the kinds of differential rights that Inight apply
to cultural or national nlinorities, or for particular ways of distrib­
uting wealth and other goods produced by social cooperation.
Instead, they focus on the Inicropolitical sources of political
change such as the Ininoritarian becOlnings that provide the affect­
ive ilTIpetus for political lTIoVelnents. On their view, the sources of
political creativity rnust always be traced back to shifts in the for­
Inations of individual and group desire that in turn lead to changes


206
PAUL PATTON
in sensibility, allegiance, and belief. To the extent that such Inicro­
political lnovements bring about changes in the majoritarian stand­
ards themselves, along with new fonns of right or different status
for particular groups, they effectively bring about what Deleuze and
Guattari refer to as "new earths and new peoples" (WP 99, 101). At
the saIne time, the significance of such Ininoritarian becOlnings
for public political right depends on their being translated into new
fonns of right and different statuses for individuals and groups:
"Molecular escapes and Inovements would be nothing if they did
not return to the molar organizations to reshuffle their segments,
their binary distributions of sexes, classes and parties" (ATP 216-
17). In this manner, even though they offer neither descriptive nor
normative accounts of Inacropolitical institutions and procedures,
Deleuze and Guattari do provide a supplelnent to liberal democratic
conceptions of political order. They invent a language in which to
describe micropolitical lnovements and infrapolitical processes
that give rise to new forms of constitutional and legal order. They
outline a social ontology of asselnblages and processes that bears
indirectly on the forms of public right. They invent concepts such
as becoming-lIlinor, nOlIladisln, smooth space, and lines of flight
or deterritorialization that are not lneant as substitutes for exist­
ing concepts of freedOln, equality, or justice but that are intended
to assist the elnergence of another justice, new kinds of equality
and freedom, as well as new kinds of political differentiation and
constraint.I5
Although this political ontology does not include nonnative polit­
ical concepts of equality, freedOln, and justice, it does include a kind
of formal nonnativity. Moreover, there is a progression in Deleuze
and Guattari's work frOln a focus on this fonnal nonnativity in the
earlier work toward increasing engagement with explicitly political
nonnativity in their later work. By "formal nonnativity" I mean the
way in which Anti- Oedipus discusses political institutions only
frOln the perspective of a universal theory of society and history.
The specifically political organization of society plays no independ­
ent role in this theory. Rather, it is treated as continuous with the
co-ordination and control of flows of matter and desire in non-state
societies governed by the Territorial Inachine with its systerns of
alliance and filiation.I6 Deleuze and Guattari present the state as a
new lnechanisln of alliance rather than as the embodiInent of any


Deleuze's political philosophy
207
ideal treaty or contract on the part of its subjects (AO I95-96). They
argue that the state fonn appeared in hlunan history in the guise of
the different kinds of Despotic Inachine, each with its own Inech΅
anisms of overcoding the flows of desire, before becOlning subor­
dinate to the "civilized Inachine" that is global capitalisln. What
they call the Territorial, Despotic, and Civilized social Inachines
are treated only as different regilnes of co-ordination and control
of the local desiring-machines that constitute individual, falnilial,
and social life. There is no discussion of the nonns that regulate
Inodern political life, only the normativity inherent in the typology
of desiring Inachines as embodying either the paranoiac, reaction­
ary, and fascistic pole of desire or the schizoid and revolutionary
pole (AO 340). For this reason, their "schizoanalytic" theory and
practice of desire proposes neither a political program nor a project
for a future fonn of society.
A Thousand Plateaus broadens and generalizes Deleuze and
Guattari's social ontology so that it becomes a general theory of
assemblages and the manner in which these are expressed through­
out hlunan history. The last vestiges of Marxist teleology are
relnoved frOln their universal history such that social fonnations
are defined by processes or becOlnings and "all history does is to
translate a coexistence of becOlnings into a succession" (ATP 430).
The successive plateaus provide a series of new concepts and associ­
ated terminology with which to describe different kinds of assem­
blages. These include concepts designed to express social, linguistic,
and affective asselnblages, such as strata, content and expression,
territories, lines of flight, or deterritorialization; the terminology
employed to outline a micro- as opposed to Inacropolitics, along
with concepts such as body without organs, intensities, molar and
Inolecular segmentarities, and the different kinds of line of which
we are cOlnposed; the terminology elnployed to describe capitalism
as a nonterritorially based axiomatic of flows of materials, labor,
and infonTlation as opposed to a territorial system of overcoding;
and finally, they include a concept of the state as an apparatus of
capture that, in the forms of its present actualization, is increas­
ingly subordinated to the requirelnents of the capitalist axiOlnatic,
along with a concept of abstract Inachines of Inetamorphosis, or
nOlnadic war Inachines, that are the agents of social and political
transfonnation.


208
PAUL PATTON
This nlachinic theory of society is nonnative in a specific and
fonnal sense, nmnely that the different kinds of asselnblage amount
to a world in which systelnatic priority is accorded to minoritarian
beconlings over lnajoritarian being, to planes of consistency over
planes of organization, to nomadic machines of lnetalnorphosis over
apparatuses of capture, to slnooth rather than striated space, and so
on. Deleuze and Guattari's political ontology presents certain kinds
of movelnent as primary: becOlning-minor as a process of deviation
frOln a Inajoritarian standard, lines of flight or deterritorialization
rather than processes of reterritorialization or capture, and so on.
In this sense, their ontology of asselnblages is also an ethics or an
ethology. This ethics lnight be characterized in the language of one
or other of the plateaus as an ethics of becOlning, of flows or lines
of flight, or as an ethics and a politics of deterritorializationP It is
"political" only in the very broad sense that it enables us to concep·"
tualize and describe transfonnative forces and movelnents as well
as the fonns of "capture" or blockage to which these are subject.
In order to appreciate the cOlnplexity of this ontology and the
kind of description that it allows, consider Deleuze and Guattari's
concepts of deterritorialization and reterritorialization. In the con­
cluding statelnent of rules governing some of their most important
concepts at the end of A Thousand Plateaus, deterritorialization is
defined as the lnovement or process by which something escapes or
departs frOln a given territory, where a territory can be a system of
any kind: conceptual, linguistic, social, or affective (ATP 508). By
contrast, reterritorialization refers to the ways in which deterritori­
alized elements recombine and enter into new relations in the con­
stitution of a new assemblage or the lnodification of the old. Systelns
of any kind always include "vectors of deterritorialization," while
deterritorialization is always "inseparable from correlative reter­
ritorializations" (ATP 509). Deterritorialization can take either a
negative or a positive form. It is negative when the deterritorialized
element is subjected to reterritorialization that obstructs or liInits
its line of flight. It is positive when the line of flight prevails over
the forms of reterritorialization and manages to connect with other
deterritorialized elements in a lnanner that extends its trajectory
or even leads to reterritorialization in an entirely new asselnblage.
As well as distinguishing between negative and positive deterrito­
rialization, Deleuze and Guattari further distinguish between an


Deleuze's political philosophy
209
absolute and a relative fonn of each of these processes. Absolute
deterritorialization refers to the virtual realn1 of becOlning and pure
events, while relative deterritorialization concerns only n10velnents
within the actual realm of elnbodied, historical events and proc­
esses. In the tenns of their ontology of assenlblages, it is the virtual
order of becOlning that governs the fate of any actual asselnblage.
Finally, in accordance with their method of specification of con­
cepts by proliferating distinctions, they distinguish between the
connection and conjugation of deterritorialized elelnents in the
construction of a new asselnblage. The effective transformation of a
given elelnent of social or political life requires the recOlnbination
of deterritorialized elements in mutually supportive and product­
ive ways to fonn asselnblages of connection rather than conjuga­
tion. Absolute and relative de territorialization will both be positive
when they involve the construction of "revolutionary connections
in opposition to the conjugations of the axiomatic" (ATP 473). Under
these conditions, absolute deterritorialization "connects lines of
flight, raises them to the power of an abstract vital line or draws a
plane of consistency" (ATP 5 10).
Deleuze and Guattari's concepts are normative in the sense
that they provide a descriptive language within which to judge
the character of particular events and processes. They enable us to
pose questions such as: Is this negative or positive reterritorializa­
tion? Is this a genuine line of flight? Will it lead to a revolution­
ary new asselnblage in which there is an increase of freedOln, or
will it lead to a new form of capture or worse (D I43-44)? In this
sense, the judgments enabled by Deleuze and Guattari's ontology
of assemblages and processes are entirely practical and pragmatic.
They enable a forn1 of reflective judgment, although one that is
closer to Kant's aesthetic judglnent than to his determinative judg­
Inents of practical or theoretical reason. Philosophy, Deleuze and
Guattari suggest, "is not inspired by truth. Rather, it is categories
like Interesting, Relnarkable or IInportant that determine success
or failure" (WP 82).
DELEUZE'S T URN TOWARDS POLITICAL NORMATIVITY
Deleuze and Guattari's Inachinic social and political ontology has a
nonnative dilnension insofar as it presents a world of interconnected


2IO
PAUL PATTON
n1achinic asselnblages, the innennost tendency of which is toward
the "deterritorialization" of existing asselnblages and their "reter­
ritorialization" in new fonns. Nevertheless, their ontology relnains
fonnal in relation to actual societies and forrns of political organ­
ization. Disagreelnents with Marxisln aside, all of their political
theoretical innovations were carried out within a broadly Marxist
perspective that envisaged the elnergence of new and better forms
of social and political life. However, at no point did they address
the nonnative principles that infonn their critical perspective on
the present, much less the question how these might be articulated
with those principles that are supposed to govern political life in
late capitalist societies. Nowhere did they engage directly with the
political norms embedded in liberal delnocratic political institu­
tions and ways of life, such as the equal lnoral worth of individuals,
freedOln of conscience, the rule of law, fairness in the distribution
of material goods produced by social cooperation, and so on. The
principled differences between liberal delnocratic, totalitarian, and
fascist states were mentioned only in passing in the course of their
analysis of capitalisln and present-day politics as a process of ax i­
omatization of the social and econOlnic field.
Read in the context of Western Marxism during the 1960s and
I970s, Deleuze and Guattari's failure to engage directly with the
political values and normative concepts that are supposed to infonn
the basic institutions of Inodern liberal delnocracies is not surpris­
ing. Their political philosophy predates widespread understanding
and acceptance of the ways in which Marx's critique of capitalist
society is bound up with concepts of distributive justice, as it does
the efforts to identify the relevant principles of justice that occurred
under the ilnpact of so-called analytic Marxisln in the course of
the I980s. Since then, there have been nUlnerous attelnpts to COln­
bine Marxist social theory with the nonnative principles infonning
varieties of left-liberal political theory.Is While these developlnents
had little impact in France, there was a silnilar rediscovery of eth­
ical and political nonnativity in French political thought during
this period. This was expressed, for example, in a renewed inter­
est in human rights, subjectivity, justice, equality, and freedOln in
the work of contelnporaries such as Foucault and Derrida. Guattari
became involved in electoral politics during the latter part of the
I980s, standing as Green candidate in I992 regional elections.


Deleuze's political philosophy
2II
Deleuze's writings and COll11nents in interviews frorn the 1980s
Inark a significant shift in his thinking about such nonnative issues.
For eXaInple, he responds to the renewed interest in human rights
during this period by insisting on the irnportance of jurisprudence
as the means to create new rights. While he criticizes the nlanner in
which human rights are represented as "eternal values" and "new
forms of transcendence," he Inakes it clear that he is not opposed
to rights as such but only to the idea that there is a definitive and
ahistorical list of supposed universal rights. He argues that rights
are not the creation of codes or declarations but of jurisprudence,
where this irnplies working with the "singularities" of a particular
situation (N 153). He returns to the question of rights and jurispru­
dence in his Abecedaire interviews with Claire Parnet, recorded in
1988-89, where he affinns the ilnportance of jurisprudence under­
stood as the invention of new rights, along with his own fascin­
ation for the law.I9 In his 1990 interview with Negri, he reaffinns
the ilnportance of jurisprudence as a source of law with reference
to the question what rights should be established in relation to new
fonns of biotechnology (N 169). Deleuze's endorselnent of rights and
jurisprudence clearly cornmits him to the existence of a rule of law
and the kind of constitutional state that this ilnplies. In the case of
societies that seek to govern thelnselves in this manner, the con­
cept of a right iInplies that certain kinds of action on the part of
all citizens will be protected by law and, conversely, the enforce­
Inent of lilnits to the degree to which citizens can interfere with the
actions of others.
Deleuze's political writings frorn the 1980s onward provide evi­
dence not only of his cornlnitInent to the rule of law but also to deln­
ocracy. His 1979 "Open Letter to Negri's Judges" already adopted the
speaking position of a democrat cornlnitted to certain principles in
relation to due process and the rule of law (TRM [2007 edition] 169).
His concern with dernocracy becomes Inore pronounced in What is
Philosophya, where there is a series of highly critical reIn arks about
actually existing deinocracies. Far from disinissing the democratic
ideal, these cominents illiply that other actualizations of the con­
cept or "pure event" of delnocracy are possible. What is Philosophya
offers no Inore direct account of principles that are supposed to gov­
ern modern democratic societies than A Thousand Plateaus. In this
sense, it offers no theory of public right. Many of the elements of


212
PAUL PATTON
Deleuze and Guattari's prior cOlnlnitrnent to Marxisln relnain in
the diagnosis of the present outlined in What is Philosophy? For
eXaInple, the analysis of the isonl0rphic but heterogeneous char­
acter of all states with regard to the global capitalist aximnatic
is reproduced in identical tenns. FrOln this perspective, there are
political differences between different kinds of state but also com­
plicity with an increasingly global systeln of exploitation. They sug­
gest that even the Inost delnocratic states are cOlnprOlnised by their
role in the production of hlunan Inisery alongside great wealth (WP
107; N l73). They Inaintain their COlnlnitlnent to the revolutionary­
becOlning of people rather than the traditional Marxist concept of
revolution, even as they point out that the concept of revolution
is itself a philosophical concept par excellence, one that expresses
"absolute deterritorialization even to the point where this calls for
a new earth, a new people" (WP lOI).
What is Philosophy? argues for the inherently political vocation of
philosophy. Philosophy is defined as the creation of concepts where
these serve an overtly utopian function: "We lack resistance to the
present. The creation of concepts in itself calls for a future fonn, for a
new earth and people that do not yet exist" (WP 108). In the present,
the task of philosophy is aligned with the struggle against capital­
iSln. Deleuze and Guattari suggest that philosophical concepts are
critical of the present to the extent that they "connect up with what
is real here and now in the struggle against capitalism" (WP 100).
At this point, the outline of a new concept appears in their political
philosophy. What is Philosophy? contrasts the actual universality of
the Inarket with the virtual universality of a global democratic state
and describes philosophy as it is envisaged here as reterritorialized
on a new earth and a people to come quite unlike those found in
actually existing democracies. Deleuze and Guattari's neo-Marx­
ist support for becoming-revolutionary as the path towards a new
earth and a people to COlne is cOlnbined with a call for resistance
to existing fonns of democracy in the name of a "becoming-derllo­
cratic that is not to be confused with present constitutional states"
(WP II3). In contrast to the fonnal nonnativity of their earlier work,
the political normativity of Deleuze and Guattari's later philoso­
phy is defined by this relation between becOllling-revolutionary and
becOlning-democratic. On this basis, in full recognition of their dif­
ferences frOln liberal nonnative political philosophy, it nevertheless


Deleuze's political philosophy
213
becOlnes possible to COlnpare their later work with that of a left­
liberal political philosopher such as John Rawls.2o
IMMANENT UTOPIANISM AND BECOMING ­
DEMO CRAT IC
In Justice as Fairness: A Restatelnent, Rawls identifies four purposes
served by his kind of reconstructive liberal normative political phil­
osophy: First, it can help to resolve deeply disputed questions by
searching for COlnInon philosophical and Inoral ground between
the protagonists. Second, it can serve the task of orientation that
seeks to identify reasonable and rational ends, both individual and
collective, and to show "how those ends can cohere within a well­
articulated conception of a just and reasonable society."21 Third, it
can address the task of reconciliation by showing the limits of what
can be achieved within a democratic society characterized by the
existence of "profound and irreconcilable differences in citizen's
reasonable cOlnprehensive religious and philosophical conceptions
of the world."u Finally, it serves the "realistically utopian" task of
"probing the limits of practicable political possibility." It asks what a
just and democratic society would be like, given the "circumstances
of justice" that obtain in the actual historical world in which we
live, but also what it would be like "under reasonably favourable but
still possible historical conditions."23 Rawls notes that the limits of
the practicable are not silnply given by the actual, because we can
and do change existing social and political institutions. However,
he does not pursue any further the question of what determines
the lilnits of the practicable or how we Inight ascertain what these
lilnits are.24
Deleuze's conception of philosophy is concerned above all to
challenge the lilnits of our present social world. What is Philosophy?
presents a conception of the political vocation of philosophy with far
Inore radical alnbitions than those acknowledged in Rawls' realistic
utopianisln. Of the four functions of political philosophy identified
by Rawls, Deleuze's philosophy does not address those of resolution,
orientation, or reconciliation. It does address the utopian function,
although not by setting out nonnative principles against which
we Inight evaluate the justice or fairness of social institutions.
The sense in which Deleuze and Guattari's political philosophy is


214
PAUL PATTON
utopian must be understood in tenns of the connection between the
absolute deterritorialization pursued in philosophy and the relative
deterritorializations at work in its social lnilieu: "There is always a
way in which absolute deterritorialization takes over frOln a relative
deterritorialization in a given field" (WP 88). The utopian vocation of
philosophy can be achieved only when the concepts that it invents
engage with existing fonns of relative deterritorialization. This con­
ception of philosophy therefore implies an ilnmanent utopianisln
in the sense that it does not silnply posit an ideal future but rather
ainlS to connect with processes of relative deterritorialization that
are present in but stifled by the present milieu, extending these and
taking theln to extrelnes. To the extent that these processes or "lines
of flight" encompass resistant political forces along with the ideals
or opinions that motivate them, this ilnlnanent utopianisln cannot
avoid drawing on elements of present political normativity to sug­
gest ways in which the injustice or intolerability of existing institu­
tional fonns of social life might be removed. In this manner, because
the concept of democracy ties together a number of the values at the
heart of contemporary political thought, elements of that concept
may be used to counteractualize certain forms of resistance to the
present in public political culture. These elements in turn provide
the components of the concept of "becoming-democratic" which
serves the utopian task of political philosophy by probing the limits
of delnocratic processes in contemporary society.
Deleuze offers no detailed account of "becOllling-delllocratic."
However, it is possible to fill out the concept with elelllents of his
prior work with Guattari as well as occasional COlllments in inter­
views. For exalllple, in his interview with Negri, he suggests that
new rights in relation to the situations created by modern biology
should be proposed by "user-groups" rather than "supposedly well­
qualified wise men" (N 170). In effect, he invokes the principle that
decisions ought to be taken in consultation with those lllOSt affected
by thelll. This is one of the founding principles of lllodern demo­
cratic governance, and Deleuze is not the only theorist to reCOlll­
mend its extension and application to new contexts. This suggests
that the opening-up of decision-Illaking procedures throughout soci­
ety lllight constitute a vector of "becoming-democratic."
Minoritarian becomings provide another vector of "becoming­
democratic." These are defined as the variety of ways in which


Deleuze's political philosophy
2I5
individuals and groups fail to confonn to the majoritarian standard
(ATP I05-6). They have given rise to a succession of Ineasures to
extend the scope of the standard and thereby broaden the subject
of delnocracy: first, in purely quantitative tenns by extending the
vote to wornen and other Ininorities; second, in qualitative tenns
by changing the nature of political institutions and procedures to
enable these newly enfranchised Inelnbers to participate on equal
tenns. Efforts to change the nature of public institutions in ways
that both acknowledge and accomlnodate Inany kinds of difference
are ongoing, for exaInple in relation to sexual preference and phys­
ical and Inental abilities, as well as cultural and religious back­
grounds. Deleuze and Guattari's support for Ininoritarian be comings
affirms the iInportance of efforts to enlarge the character of the
Inajority. By their nature, processes of Ininoritarian becoming will
always exceed or escape frOln the confines of any given Inajority.
Nevertheless, they eInbody the potential to transfonn the affects,
beliefs, and political sensibilities of a population in ways that can
lead to the advent of a new people. To the extent that a people is con­
stituted as a political cOlnInunity, the transformations it undergoes
will affect its conceptions of what is fair and just. In turn, these will
affect the distribution of rights and duties as well as the presence of
minority citizens in the public institutions and political functions
of the society.
A third vector of "becoming-delnocratic" involves efforts to
achieve a In ore just distribution of Inaterial social goods. Deleuze
is often critical of the way that modern delnocratic states fail to
live up to this aspect of their egalitarian prOlnise (WP I06-7; N 1 72).
However, his suggestion that delnocratic states are Inorally and pol­
itically comprOlnised by their role in the perpetuation of this forn1
of injustice implicitly raises the nonnative question: What princi­
ples of distribution should apply in a just delnocratic society? Should
we advocate radically egalitarian principles that would treat any
undeserved inequality of condition as unjust, or should we be satis­
fied with Rawls' difference principle, according to which social and
economic inequalities are allowed but only when they are attached
to positions open to all and when they are "to the greatest benefit
of the least advantaged Inembers of society"PS Should the principles
of distributive justice apply globally or only within the borders of
particular delnocratic states? Deleuze does not provide us with the


216
PAUL PATTON
Ineans to answer these nonnative questions but they are inevitably
raised by his criticisIns of existing states of affairs.
C O NCLUSION
Deleuzian philosophy as it is presented in What is Philosophya is
clearly political in the sense that it has an inherently political voca­
tion. The creation of concepts serves the larger project of bringing
about new earths and new peoples. Deleuze and Guattari propose a
novel kind of utopian political thought that is neither Marxist nor
liberal. They rely upon a political ontology of assem.blages rather
than individual subjects of interest and right. Their goal is the trans­
fonnation of existing political nonns and institutions rather than the
reconstruction of the nonnative principles iInplicit in existing liberal
denlOcratic institutions into a coherent political theory. Despite the
substantive differences that separate their approach from that of lib­
eral nonnative political philosophy, there is at least a degree of con­
vergence between theln. Deleuze and Guattari's collaborative work
Inoves frOln a formal to a more substantive engagelnent with the
explicitly political concepts and nonns that make up the public polit­
ical culture of liberal democratic states. The concept of a "becOlning­
democratic" at the heart of their later political philosophy points to
the role of elements of existing concepts of delnocracy in historical
struggles to iInplernent or expand democratic government.

MANUEL DELANDA
Deleuze, mathematics,
realist ontology
REALISM IN HIS TORY
Unlike Inost of his contelnporaries, Deleuze was a realist philoso­
pher. But his realist stance was deeply innovative and constituted
a sharp break with the brand of realisln that dominated Western
thought for 2,500 years, the one created by Aristotle. As is well
known, the Greek philosopher's world was populated by three cat­
egories of entities: genus, species, and individual. Entities belonging
to the first two categories subsisted essentially, those belonging to
the third one subsisted only accidentally. I The genus could be, for
exalnple, AniInal, the species Human, and the individual this or
that particular person characterized by contingent properties: being
white, being Inusical, being just. A genus was linked to its various
species (Horse, HUlnan) by a series of logically necessary subdivi­
sions. The genus Animal, for eXaInple, could be subdivided into
two-footed and Inany-footed types; then subdivided into differences
in extrelnities: hooves, as in horses, or feet, as in humans. When we
reached a point at which any further distinctions were accidental,
like a foot Inissing a toe, we arrived at the level of the species, the
lowest ontological level at which we could speak of an essence or of
the very nature of a thing.
Possession of these essential traits is what guaranteed the mind­
independent identity of things in Aristotelian realisln. It follows that
any new brand of realism, if it is to be truly novel, Inust replace the
categories of genus and species with sOlnething that does not imply
an ontological cOl1lInitlnent to transcendent entities (essences). This
task is easier for species than for genera. The transcendent nature
of species can be eliIninated silnply by transfonning theln into his­
torical entities, like Darwin did. In evolutionary theory a biological
220


Deleuze, n1athelnatics, and realist ontology
22I
species is as singular, as unique, and as historically contingent as an
individual organisn1: a species is born when its gene pool is closed
to the flow of genetic Inaterials frOln other reproductive COlnmu­
nities - that is, it is born through reproductive isolation - and its
dies through extinction. In other words, species like organislns
are "subject to corruption and decay," as Aristotle would say.2 And
their defining properties are not logically necessary. Reproductive
isolation is a contingent achievelnent that varies by degree, so noth­
ing guarantees that the identity of a biological species will endure
forever. Similarly, driving a species to extinction is like killing an
individual organisln: we destroy a unique historical creation that
can never return again.
Thus, getting rid of essences at the level of species is silnply a
Inatter of introducing history (evolutionary history in this case) into
a realist ontology. But essences at the genus level are an entirely
different Inatter. What we need here is a Ineans to conceptual­
ize a "topological animal," an abstract animal that can becOlne a
hUInan or a horse through a series of eInbryological operations: fold­
ings, stretchings, invaginations, cellular Inigrations. This second
replacement is the one that involves mathelnatics. More specific­
ally, if species Inust be conceived as individual singularities, genera
must be replaced by a topological diagram structured by universal
singularities) This can only be achieved by going beyond deduct­
ive logic, the source of the concepts of the general and the specific,
and into mathelnatics: the differential calculus, group theory, non­
Inetric geOlnetry. To understand how mathelnatics Inust replace
logic in a non-transcendent form of realisIn we Inust first give a
siInple description of how differential equations are used in science,
because what we want to explore is not the ontology of Inathelnatics
itself (Platonist, Constructivist) but the ontological issues raised by
the use of Inathelnatics to Inodel the real world.
T HE ROLE OF MATHEMATICS
To create a Inathelnatical Inodel of an actual physical systeln one
Inust first specify the relevant ways in which the systeln is free to
change, that is, we must discover its "degrees of freedOln." Because
as the degrees of freedOln of a systeln change its overall state changes,
a Inodel of the systeln Inust capture the different possible states in


222
MANUEL DELANDA
which it can exist. As the lnathelnatician Henri Poincare showed
a century ago, this set of states lnay be represented as a space of
possibilities with as lnany dilnensions as the systeln has degrees of
freed01n. This space is referred to as "state space/' the space of all
possible states in which the systeln being lnodeled can exist. In this
space each point represents one possible state for a physical systeln,
the state it has at a given instant of tilne. As the states of a physical
systeln change with time, that is, as the system goes through a tem­
poral sequence of states, its representation in state space becomes
a continuous sequence of points: a curve or a trajectory.4 A set of
different trajectories captures several possible histories for the sys­
teln. Each point in this space, each possible state, can have different
probabilities of existing. A space in which all the points are equally
probable is a space without any structure, and it represents a phys­
ical system in which states change in a c01llpletely random way, the
trajectories wandering around state space without settling down.
By the eighteenth century, however, lnathematicians like Leonard
Euler knew that the space of possible solutions for differential equa­
tions did have structure. In particular, he showed that S01ne of the
points that constituted this space were special or relnarkable in that
the states they represented were llluch more likely to occur than
any other possible state. This ilnplies that these special or singu­
lar points are like the long-term tendencies of a systeln, the states
that would be realized if we just waited long enough. It does not
lnatter where they begin, all the possible histories will tend to end
at a singular point. Moreover, these singularities are universal in
the sense that they can be shared by entirely different physical sys­
telns: gravitational, lnechanical, optical, electrostatic. Indeed, the
unification of all branches of Classical Mechanics in the nineteenth
century was made possible by this universality. To return to our ori­
ginal exalnple, the space of all possible anilnals lnust be conceived
as structured by universal singularities detennining the overall
tendencies shaping evolutionary history. But replacing the genus
Animal by such a topological diagram faces a lnajor obstacle: unlike
the spaces of classical physics we do not know the structure of the
possibility spaces associated with animals. Nevertheless, many of
the concepts that we would need to understand the "topological
animal" can be developed starting with the simpler cases, the only
ones explicitly discussed by Deleuze.


Deleuze, Inathelnatics, and realist ontology
223
Deleuze is not, in fact, the only contenlporary philosopher that

towards the Inathelnatics actually used by scientists, and it will

cussion of Deleuze's views in cOlnparative terms. For Inany analyt­
ical philosophers abandoning the categories of the general and the
particular is a difficult step because Inany of them were trained to

late nineteenth century: first the differential calculus was reduced

on arithmetic was reduced to set theory. For those philosophers
who believed that this reduction was in fact achieved, mathelnatics
disappeared and deductive logic became the only fonnal discipline
that needed to be Inastered. It is not surprising, therefore, that real­
ist analytical philosophers tend to speak like Aristotle, defining
the identity of things by the necessary and sufficient conditions to
belong to a general category. In other words, defining identity by
the possession of an essence.
Moreover, those who accept the reduction of Inathematics to
logic take an axiomatic approach to scientific theories: the content
of a theory is modeled by a set of axiOlns, or self-evident truths,
and by all the theorems that Inay be derived from those axioms

phers argue that it is through the use of tools like the differential

it is these models, not any logical reconstruction of them, that
should be the subject of philosophy of science. Among the leaders
of this Inovement is Bas Van Fraassen, who has stated his position
this way:
The semantic view of theories makes language largely irrelevant to the
subject. Of course, to present a theory, we must present it in and by lan­
guage. That is a trivial point .. , In addition, both because of our own
history - the history of philosophy of science which became intensely
language-oriented during the first half of [the last] century - and because


224
MANUEL DELANDA
of its intrinsic importance, we cannot ignore the language of science. But
in a discussion of the structure of theories it can largely be ignored.s
The reason for leaving language and logic behind and bringing

study, that is, state space diagrallls - is that it is only by analyz­
ing actual models that we can explore the relation between theor­
ies and the laboratory phenOlllena they are designed to explain. In
other words, we want to know why these lllOdels actually work,
why they lllanage to capture the regularities in the behavior of

the logical structure of an axiomatic. Since it is their success in

ontology let's give a silllple description of how models are lllatched
to phenolllena. Let's assume that we have a laboratory where we
can lllanipulate real physical phenOlllena, that is, where we can
restrict their degrees of freedOlll (by screening out other factors)
and where we can place them in a given initial state and then let
thelll run spontaneously through a sequence of states. Let's also

each of those states. After several trials we generate data about the

it evolves frOlll different initial conditions. We can then plot these

trajectory.
We then run our lllathematical model, giving it the SaIlle values
for initial conditions as our laboratory runs, and generate a set of

If the lllathelllatical and experilllental trajectories display geomet­
rical silllilarity this will be evidence that the model actually works.
As one analytical philosopher puts it:
[WJe can say that a dynamical theory is approximately true just if the mod­
eling geometric structure approximates (in suitable respects) to the struc­
ture to be modeled: a basic case is where trajectories in the model closely
track trajectories encoding physically real behaviors (or, at least, track

Deleuze, n1athen1atics, and realist ontology

It is only when lTIathelnatical lnodels have the capacity to track the

MATHEMATICAL MODELS AND ONTOLO GICAL
COMMITMENTS
Starting with different ontological cOlnlnitn1ents, however, we

ponents of state space. The first candidates for ontological evalu­

Take, for instance, the possible fat man in the doorway; and again, the
possible bald man in the doorway. Are they the same possible man, or two
possible men? How do we decide? How many possible men there are in that

226
MANUEL DELANDA
What Quine is arguing here is that we do not possess the lTIeanS
to individuate possible entities, that is, to identify thelTI in the
lTIidst of all the possible variations. The traditional approach to

This branch of logic is concerned with the analysis of counterfac­

sibilities '" [But] many models in which the system laws are expressed as

The extra structure that state space brings to the determination of

Van Fraassen, for example, can still deny the need to be ontologic­

actualism lTIisses the fact that the population of possible trajector­

Deleuze, n1athen1atics, and realist ontology
227
knowing how it would behave in conditions that n1ay not in fact

ity spaces is what is needed.
The significance of Deleuze's realist approach is precisely that it
supplies us with such an assessn1ent. Deleuze is not an actualist but
he is not a realist about traditional n10dalities, either. He would not

therefore introduce a new fonn of physical n10dality, virtuality, to

patterns of becOlning in nature. Son1ething virtual is something

Fraassen in being cOlnlnitted only to actual entities but also with

fonnal analysis of this structure involves considering a different

When the study of the behavior of differential equations was
given a geOlnetric fonn, the abstract spaces used were differential

and Z values, presupposing a set of Cartesian coordinates and a tran­

a differential Inanifold is a field of rapidities and slownesses, the

a differential lnanifold, in fact, every point is not only a speed but a
velocity, since a direction n1ay be assigned to it. Because a velocity
can be represented by a vector, state spaces possess in addition to

ilnportance of this is that the distribution of universal singularities
that constitutes the structure of state space is not given by the tra­
jectories (or integral curves) but by the vector field itself. More pre­
cisely, while the nature of a singularity Inust be established through
the use of nearby trajectories whether a point singularity is a focus
or a node, for exarnple, Inust be detern1ined by observing how the


228
MANUEL DELANDA
integral curves in its vicinity approach it, spirally or on a straight
line - the existence and distTibution of the singularities does not

Already Leibniz had shown that the calculus . . .  expressed problems which
could not hitherto be solved or, indeed, even posed . . .  One thinks in par­
ticular of the role of the regular and the singular points which enter into
the complete determination of the species of a curve. No doubt the specifi­
cation of the singular points (for example, dips, nodes, focal points, centers)
is undertaken by means of the form of integral curves, which refers back

tion of the points already shows the necessary immanence of the problem

PROBLEMS AND T HE S TRUCT URE OF
PO S S IBILITY SPACES
For Deleuze the Inathelnatical distinction between a vector field and
its singularities, on the one hand, and the trajectories or curves, on
the other, becOlnes the philosophical distinction between the defin­
ing conditions of a problem and its many possible solutions. This
Inust be understood both epistemologically and ontologically. In the
first sense an epistelnological probleln is posed by giving a distribu­
tion of the significant and the insignificant, that is, by discovering
the ilnportant ways in which a phenOlnenon is free to change and
eliminating all the trivial ways. The problem can then be given a

of a non-metric space) and its structure determined via the vector
field. Finally, we can find nUlnerical solutions to the equations and
generate trajectories, each representing a series of states that we can
COlnpare with a series of actual states of a laboratory phenomenon.
Clearly, the crucial lnoment in this epistelnological sequence is the
first step: discovering what Inakes a difference and what does not
Inalze a difference in the behavior of a phenonlenon. The solutions


Deleuze, n1athelnatics, and realist ontology
229
we obtain at the end are only as good as the probleln they are sup··
posed to solve: if we included trivial degrees of freedOln in the ori­
ginal specification of the probleln its solutions Inay also be trivial
or Inisleading. Moreover, the concrete distribution of the significant
and the insignificant that defines the probleln retains its virtual
existence even as we solve it, since it guides the generation of all
individual solutions.
And silnilarly for the laboratory phenOlnena that are the target
of the Inathelnatical Inodel. A state space structured by a single
singularity defining an optilnum value (a IninilnUln of energy, for
eXainple) captures a real optimization problem to which a whole
variety of actual entities (soap bubbles, crystals, light rays) are con­
crete solutions. But the opthnization probleln subsists once it is
given a physical solution, ready to be posed again the next tilne a
flat piece of soap filln ml st wrap itself into a sphere, or a set of
Inolecules lnust conform itself to the geometrical shape of a crys­
tal. To return to our original example, the structured space of all
possible animals with which we lnust replace the genus Anhnal
must be thought as an objective ontological probleln, a probleln to
which every species and organisln are concrete adaptive solutions.
As Deleuze writes: "An organisln is nothing but the solution to a
probleln, as are each of its differentiated organs, such as the eye
which solves a light probleln" (DR 2II).
This implies that problelns are not only independent of their
solutions, they have a genetic relationship with theln: a problem
engenders its own solutions as its conditions becOlne progressively
better specified. Deleuze's discussion of this point uses a different
branch of Inathelnatics, group theory, and its application to the solu­
tions of algebraic, not differential, equations, so we first need to give
the historical background of this other field. There are two kinds
of solutions to equations, nUlnerical and analytical. A nUlnerical
solution is given by nUlnbers that, when used to replace an equa­
tion's unknowns, Inake the equation come out true. For eXainple, an
algebraic equation like X2 + 2X - 8 = 0 has as its numerical solution
x
= 2. An analytical or exact solution, on the other hand, does not
yield any specific value or set of values but rather the global pattern
of all values, a pattern expressed by another equation or fonnula.
Or in the terms used before, an analytical solution gives us the


230
MANUEL DELANDA
structure of the space of all possible solutions. The above exan1ple,
which n1ay be rewritten without nUlnerical constants as:
ax2 + bx + c = 0
has the analytic solution:

x
2a
By the sixteenth century lnathelnaticians knew the exact solutions
to algebraic equations where the unknown variable was raised up
to the fourth power (that is, those including x2, x3, and X4). But then
a crisis ensued. Equations raised to the fifth power refused to yield
to the previously successful lnethod. The breakthrough came two
centuries later when it was noticed that there was a pattern to the
solutions of the first four cases, a pattern that lnight hold the key to
understanding the recalcitrance of the fifth, known as the quintic.
The n1athematicians Niels Abel and Evariste Galois found a way to
approach the study of this pattern using resources that today we rec­
ognize as belonging to group theory.I2 The tenn "group" refers to a
set of lnathematical entities (numbers, operations on numbers) and a
rule of combination for those entities. The set lnust possess closure,
which lneans that when we use the rule to cOlnbine any two entities
in the set, the result is also an entity in the set. For our purposes here
the most important type of entities are transformations, and the rule
a consecutive application of those transformations. For example, the
set consisting of rotations by 90 degrees (that is a set containing rota­
tions by 90, 180, 270, and 360 degrees) fonns a group, since any two
consecutive rotations produce a rotation also in the group. A group, in
turn, can be used to determine the properties of a mathelnatical entity
that relnain invariant under transformations and that are therefore its
most significant properties. A cube, for exalnple, remains unaltered
(as far as its optical behavior is concerned) under the above group of
rotations, so the group captures an ilnportant aspect of its identity.
To understand how a group of transfonnations can be used to
establish the conditions of a probleln, that is, to generate a distri­
bution of the significant and the insignificant, let's give a concrete
example: the use of groups of transfonnations to study the invari­
ants of physical laws. For the laws of classical physics, the group
includes displacelnents in space and tilne, as well as rotations and


Deleuze, Inathen1atics, and realist ontology
other transforn1ations. Let's ilnagine a physical phenOlnenon that
can be reliably produced in a laboratory. If we displace it in space
by reproducing the phenOlnenon in another, far away laboratory
we will leave all its properties invariant. Silnilarly, if we silnply
change the tilne at which we begin an experilnent, we can expect
this tilne displacenlent to be irrelevant as far as the regularity of the
phenOlnenon is concerned. It is only the difference in tilne between
the first and final states of the experilnent that Inatters, not the
absolute tilne at which the first state occurs. Thus, via transfonn­
ations applied to the equations expressing laws we can discover
those types of change to which the law is indifferent, that is, the
type of changes that do not Inake a difference to it, allowing us to
correctly conclude that using absolute time or absolute position as
inputs to the equation expressing a law is irrelevant.
In a siInilar way, Galois used certain transfonnations (substitu­
tions or permutations of an equation's solutions) that, as a group,
revealed the invariances in the relations between solutions. More
specifically, when a pennutation of one solution by another left the
equation valid, the two solutions becalne indistinguishable as far as
their validity was concerned. The group of an equation is crucial to
its solvability, because it expresses the degree of indistinguishabil­
ity of the solutions.13 Or as Deleuze would put it, the group reveals
not what we know about the solutions, but the objectivity of what
we do not ],now about them, that is, the objectivity of the probieln
itself (DR r62). And he goes on to argue that, besides delnonstrat­
ing the autonOlny of probielns frOln solutions, the group-theoretic
approach shows that the solutions to the equation are produced as
the original group gives rise to subgroups that successively linlit
the substitutions that leave relations invariant. That is, the problem
gives birth to its solutions as its own conditions becOlne progres­
sively better defined. As he writes:
We cannot suppose that, from a technical point of view, differential cal­
culus is the only mathematical expression of problems as such . . .  More
recently other procedures have fulfilled this role better. Recall the circle in
which the theory of problems was caught: a problem is solvable only to the
extent that it is "true" but we always tend to define the truth of a problem
by its solvability . . .  The mathematician Abel was perhaps the first to break
this circle: he elaborated a whole method according to which solvability
must follow from the form of a problem. Instead of seeking to find out by


232
MANUEL DELANDA
trial and error whether a given equation is solvable in general we must
determine the conditions of the problem which progressively specify the
fields of solvability in such a way that the statement contains the seed of
the solution. This is a radical reversal of the problem-solution relation, a
more considerable revolution than the Copernican . . .  The same judgement
is confirmed in relation to the work of Galois: starting from a basic "field"
(R), successive adjunctions to this field (R', R", R"' . . .  ) allow a progressively
more precise distinction between the roots of an equation by the progres­
sive limitation of possible substitutions. There is thus a succession of "par­
tial resolvents" or an "embedding of groups," which make the solutions
follow from the very conditions of the problem. (DR 179-80)
PROBLEMS AND ACTUALIZATION OF T HE V IRTUAL
To link this genetic concept of problelns to a realist ontology we need
to cOlnbine the resources of group theory with those of dynmnical
systelns theory, as the geOlnetrical approach to the study of differen­
tial equations is known. In state space the relevant transformations
are Inathelnatical events, known as bifurcations, that can change
one distribution of universal singularities into another. These
rnathelnatical events result frOln operations applied to the vector
field of a state space: a slnall vector field is added to the Inain one to
perturb it, and when the perturbation reaches a critical threshold, a
bifurcation results.I4 In some cases it is only the nUlnber of singu­
larities that changes, but in other cases their type Inay also be trans­
fonned. So far we have considered only point singularities, defining
the tendency of a physical systeln to reach a steady-state. But there
are also line singularities, shaped into a loop, that define tendencies
to oscillate in a stable way. These are called "periodic attractors" or
"liInit cycles." More recently a third variety of universal singular­
ity has been found, the result of repeatedly stretching and folding
a closed loop. These are referred to as chaotic or strange attractors.
The transfonnations that convert one of these types of singularity
into another are also well known: the Hopf bifurcation changes a
point singularity into a periodic one, and the Feigenbaum bifurca­
tion changes a periodic singularity into a chaotic one. A sequence of
bifurcations has a silnilar group theoretic structure as the series of
pennutations used by Galois to generate the solutions to the quintic
equation: as the bifurcations transfonn one singularity (or set of
singularities) into another, all the solutions to the probleln posed


Deleuze, n1athen1atics, and realist ontology
233
by the differential equations progressively unfold: steady-state solu­
tions, periodic solutions, chaotic solutions.
These regular sequences can be found not only in Inathematical
Inodels but also in objective phenOlnena, such as the phenOlnena
studied by fluid lTlechanics. Moving fluids are presented with a prob­
leln as their speed increases, and they solve this problem by adopt­
ing a variety of regilnes of flow adapted to each range of speeds. At
a slow speed the solution to the problen1 is silnple: stick to steady­
state or unifonn flow. But after a critical threshold of speed that
solution becOlnes insufficient and the Inoving fluid Inust switch to
a convective or wavy flow. Finally, after crossing yet another critical
threshold, the faster speeds present the flow with a problem that it
cannot solve by Inoving rhythlnically and it is forced to become
turbulent, distributing its energy into a structure of vortices within
vortices. I5 As these different Inanners of Inoving unfold one after
another, they display all the solutions to the probiein of flow.
Moreover, the iSOlnorphism between the behavior of the matheIn­
atical lnodel and the fluid Inechanical phenOlnena shows that they
Inay be considered as di!feTent actual solutions to the same viTtual
pToblem. In other words, to explain the success of a lTlathematical
model all we need to do is to consider it an actualization of the saIne
virtual problem that the phenOlnenon being modeled solves. There
is no correspondence between Inodels and reality but a relation of
co-actualization. I6
The fact that Deleuze compared the achievelnents of Abel and
Galois to the revolutionary ilnpact of astronOlny's switch to helio­
centrism clearly shows that he thought these ideas represented a
radical departure frOln the past. But, as he adds elsewhere, the gains
frOln group theory can only be realized philosophically if we blend
its insights with those of non-metric geOlnetries to create a theory
of problelns:
Solvability lTIUst depend upon an internal characteristic: it must be deter­
mined by the conditions of the problem, engendered in and by the problem
along with the real solutions. Without this reversal, the famous Copernican
revolution amounts to nothing. Moreover, there is no revolution so long as
we remain tied to Euclidean geometry: we must move to a geometry of
sufficient reason, a Riemannian-like differential geometry which tends to
give rise to discontinuity on the basis of continuity, or to ground solutions
in the conditions of the problem. (DR r62)
