When I described the nature of state space above I ren1arked that,
unlike the points that COlnpose a n1etric space, defined by their rela­
tion to a set of global coordinates, a point of state space is indi­
viduated by the instantaneous rate of change of curvature at that
point. This entirely new way of conceptualizing space was the
achieven1ent of two great nineteenth-century n1athen1aticians,
Carl Friedrich Gauss and Bernhard Rielnann. Another giant of
nineteenth-century n1athen1atics, Felix Klein, gave us the way to
link this achieven1ent with the resources of group theory. Klein
realized that the different geon1etries known to hiln (Euclidean
geOlnetry, affine geOl11etry, projective geon1etry) could be associated
with groups of transforn1ations that left their basic features invari­
ant. Thus, the space of Euclidean geon1etry rel11ains invariant by a
group containing all displacen1ents, rotations, and n1irror-ilnagings,
that is, transfonnations that leave rigid features like lengths, areas,
and vohnnes unchanged. In projective geOlnetry, on the other hand,
lengths and other rigid features do not relnain unchanged - they
are distorted or changed in size depending on the type of projection
used but other n10re abstract features do ren1ain invariant. As one
historian of Inathen1atics puts it:
[I]t was evident from the work of Von Staudt that projective geometry is
logically prior to Euclidian geometry because it deals with qualitative and
descriptive properties that enter into the very formation of geometrical fig­
ures and does not use the measures of line segments and angles. This fact
suggested that Euclidian geometry might be SOlne specialization of project­
ive geometry. With the non-Euclidean [metric] geometries now at hand the
possibility arose that those . . .  might also be specializations of projective
geometry.17
When differential geOlnetry and topology were added to the grow­
ing list of non-n1etric geOlnetries Klein's followers specified their
transfonnation groups and realized that they were Inore abstract
than, and hence logically prior to, projective geOlnetry. A Inatheln­
atical entity possessing invariant properties under a larger group
of transfonnations is said to have Inore sYlnlnetry than one with
a sn1aller group. The achievelnent of Klein and his followers was
to realize that all the geOlnetries were related to one another by
a SYIY1lTletTy-bl'ealzing cascade: starting with the least I/lnetric" of
all geOlnetries, topology, we can break its sYlnlnetry (by reducing


Deleuze, nlathelnatics, and realist ontology
235
the nlllnber of transfonnations in its group) and generate differen­
tial geOlnetry. Silnilarly, differential geometry becOlnes projective
geOlnetry as it loses sYInInetry, projective becOlnes affine, and affine
beconles Euclidean. Although Inathematicians do not draw onto­
logical conclusions frOln this fact, it is not too difficult to discern
in this cascade the same genetic relation that Deleuze establishes
between problelns and solutions: through a series of broken sym­
Inetries topology gives rise to Euclidean geometry, or to phrase it
like Deleuze, continuous spaces give birth to discontinuous ones.
Moreover, the sequence of geornetries also illustrates another
Deleuzian concept: progressive differentiation. While in topology
all closed figures (a circle, an ellipse, a square, a triangle) are iden­
tical, in projective geOlnetry only those that are conic sections
(circles, ellipses, parabolas) are identical. In affine geOlnetry these
figures becOlne different from each other, but the SaIne figure in
different sizes is still the SaIne. And finally, in Euclidean geOln­
etry all figures of different sizes are distinct from one another. IS In
other words, the more continuous and flexible spaces have the least
degree of differentiation (the slnallest nUlnber of distinct figures),
while the most discontinuous and rigid have the largest nUlnber of
differentiated figures. It is this progressive differentiation that links
epistemological and ontological problelns: solutions in both math­
elnatics and laboratory phenomena are engendered as the probleln
itself progressively differentiates.
Although Deleuze never mentions the work of Felix Klein, it seelns
clear that Klein supplies precisely what Deleuze is asking for when
he says that there is no revolution in the theory of problelns unless
we link group-theoretic ideas and non-Inetric spaces. Moreover, the
progressive differentiation of the geometries that transfonns unseg­
Inented spaces into fully seglnented ones supplies us with a power­
ful image to contrast the Deleuzian and Aristotelian approaches to
realist ontology. For Aristotle the world is already pre-segInented
by general and specific categories that are eternal, that is, not sub­
ject to corruption and decay. For Deleuze, on the other hand, the
world of seglnented entities (rocks, plants, anilnals) emerges as solu­
tions to ontological problelns that are defined by conditions that
do not presuppose segInentation of any kind. The ontological prob­
lelns are defined by topological invariants: the number of climen­
sions of a space of possibilities, its connectivity, and its universal


236
MANUEL DELANDA
singularities. As these ontological problelns undergo a process of
actualization they becOlne progressively differentiated into a n1ulti ..
plicity of actual solutions. This differentiation proceeds in a fully
historical way, and lnay only reveal a portion of the possibility space
at a tilne. Thus, when reptiles dOlninated the world of vertebrate
animals, they exhibited a large degree of differentiation into species
that occupied most available ecological niches. MaInlnals, on the
other hand, were at that time undifferentiated nocturnal creatures
constituting a slnall component of ecosystelns. But with the lnass
extinction of dinosaurs, the elnpty ecological niches left behind pre­
sented lnammals with the opportunity to differentiate and explode
into the multiplicity of species that we can observe today.I9
To conclude this essay we lnay return to the world of matheln­
atics and ask ourselves whether we have not introduced transcend­
ent entities through the back door when we speak of "invariants,"
topological or otherwise? If the tenn "invariant" were used by itself,
it would certainly carry implications of an eternally unchanging
essence. But in its technical lneaning it is always used relative to a
transformation: rotations, displacelnents, projections, stretchings,
foldings. These transfonnations involve both the capacities to affect
of lnathelnatical operators, as well as the capacity to be affected of
the numbers, figures, or equations that these operators affect. The
application of an operator to its target, the addition of a small vector
field to the lnain one causing a bifurcation to occur, for exalnple,
is always an event. And events, unlike essences, are not necessary
but contingent. Unlike what happens in an axiomatic approach, in
a problematic conception of geometry
figures are considered only from the view point of the affections that befall
them: sections, ablations, adjunctions, projections. One does not go by spe­
cific differences from a genus to its species, or by deduction from a stable
essence to the properties deriving from it, but rather from a problem to the
accidents that condition it and resolve it. This involves all kinds of defor­
mations, transmutations, passages to the limit, operations in which each
figure designates an "event" much more than an essence; the square no
longer exists independently from a quadrature, the cube from a cubature,
the straight line from a rectification. Whereas the theorem belongs to the
rational order, the problem is affective and is inseparable from the meta­
morphoses, generations, and creations within science itself. (ATP 362; see
also DR 187-89)

I9 The relation between topological diagrams and the evolution of ani­

JOHN PROT EVI
1 1
"Life" was a Inajor theme for Deleuze, so much so that he would say
at one point: "Everything I've written is vitalistic, at least I hope it
is" (N 143). But before we get out the pitchforks at this uttering of a
forbidden word, we should reinember Deleuze's love of provocation,
and read the beginning of the passage to see his idiosyncratic notion
of vitalism: "There's a profound link between signs, life, and vital­
ism: the power of nonorganic life that can be found in a line that's
drawn, a line of writing, a line of lTlusic. It's organisins that die,
not life. Any work of art points a way through for life, finds a way
through the cracks" (N 143).
In this chapter we will skirt the relation of life and art, I how­
ever, and instead focus upon Deleuze's writings that are aimed at
life as it is understood in the biological register.2 We'll begin with
a guide to some key biophilosophical investigations in Deleuze's
single-authored masterpiece, Difference and Repetition: chapter 2
on organic syntheses and organic tiIne, and chapter 5 on einbryo­
genesis} Then, in the second part of the chapter, we will consider
several biophilosophical themes in Deleuze and Guattari's Anti­
Oedipus and A Thousand Plateaus, addressing "vitalism," "life,"
"nature," "content and expression," "evolution and involution,"
"milieus, codes, territories," "non-organic life," "body without
organs," and "organism."
DIFFERENCE A ND R EPETITION
Deleuze's overall aiIn in Difference and Repetition is to provide a
"philosophy of difference," in which individuals are seen as pro­
duced by the integration of a differential field, or the solution of a
239


240
TOHN PROTEVI
"probleln" (DR 2I I); a paradigln case would be lightning produced
fron1 a field of electrical potential differences between cloud and
ground (DR II9). The philosophy of difference counters what we
n1ight call identitarian philosophy in which individuals are seen
as produced by a prior individual. A paradigln case for identitarian
philosophy would be a parent giving birth to a child: there is always
a horizon of identity (the falnily lineage) within which differences
can be located. Following Gilbert Silnondon,4 Deleuze certainly
notes differences between physical and living individuation: phys­
ical individuation occurs all at once, at a boundary that advances;
biological individuation occurs via "successive waves of singular­
ities" triggering qualitative changes that affect the entire internal
lnilieu of an organisln (DR 255). But Deleuze will show that a phil­
osophy of difference (individuation frOln a field of difference) is not
restricted to physical events like the lightning case, but can also
account for living individuation, so that children are also integra­
tions of a differential field (of epigenetic and genetic factors). In fact,
the philosophy of difference lnaps form and content such that its
basic Inodel (individuation as integration of a differential field) is
itself divided into (or "differenciated," to use a technical Deleuzian
term we will explain shortly) its physical and biological lnodels
without cOlnprOlnising its universality. In this way, the physical and
biological models can apply to either register without reducing the
difference between the registers. Thus children, like all other indi­
viduals, are lightning flashes ("every phenOlnenon flashes [fulgure}
in a signal-sign systeln" [DR 222J), just as clouds, like all other dif­
ferential fields, are "eggs" ("the world is an egg" [DR 251}).
Organic time and organic syntheses
Deleuze provides two genetic accounts in Difference and Repetition,
static and dynalnic; in tenns we will explain later, the static Inoves
frOln virtual Idea to actual individual, while the dynalnic moves
from ilnlnediate object of intuition to Idea.s Chapter 2 of Difference
and Repetition is part of Deleuze's dynamic genesis lnoving from
intuition to Idea; in this section he will establish the form of organic
time as, literally, a "livingpresent."6 Deleuze drives down to the lnost
basic syntheses; he shows how beneath active syntheses (thought)
are passive syntheses (perception) and beneath passive perceptual


Deleuze and life
syntheses are passive organic syntheses (l11etabolisI11).7 The chal­
lenge is to describe passive syntheses in differential ten11s, so as to
avoid the "tracing" of eI11pirical identities back to transcendental
identities; avoiding such "tracing" is a basic principle of Deleuze's
thought. In other words, passive syntheses are genetic or constitu­
tive of the identities that arise within their series; there is no per­
ceiving subject prior to the series of perceptions nor is there a living
subject prior to the series of I11etabolic "contractions." Perceptual
and I11etabolic syntheses are not grounded but are grounding.
Deleuze will distinguish the organic and perceptual syntheses by
showing that organic syntheses "perfon11 a contraction" or induce
a habit in their own, I11aterial, register. For Hume and Bergson, as
Deleuze reads theI11, the psychological iI11agination l110ves fr0111 past
particulars to future generalities, so that from a series of particulars
we C0111e to expect another of the same kind. Deleuze will abstract
the process of "drawing a difference fr0111 repetition" as the essence
of contraction or habit and show that it occurs at the organic level
as well as on the level of the passive perceptual iI11agination (DR
73).8 Perceptual syntheses thus refer back to "organic syntheses,"
which are "a priI11ary sensibility that we are" (DR 73, eI11phasis in
original). Such syntheses of the elel11ents of "water, earth, light and
air" are not I11erely prior to the active synthesis that would recog­
nize or represent theI11, but are also "prior to their being sensed." So
each organiSIT1, not only in its receptivity and perception, but also
in its "viscera" (that is, its l11etabolisl11)' is a "sum of contractions,
of retentions and expectations" (DR 73). Here we see the fon11 of
organic ti111e, the level of what is literally the "living present" of
retention and expectation. Organic retention is the "cellular heri­
tage" of the organic history of life and organic expectation is the
"faith" that things will repeat in the ways to which we are accus­
tomed. So Deleuze has isolated a "priI11ary vital sensibility" in
which we have past and future synthesized in a "living present." At
this level, the future appears as need, as "the organic form of expect­
ation," and the retained past appears as "cellular heredity" (DR 73).
Now we I11USt distinguish two genres of contraction in Deleuze's
treat111ent: (r) contraction as activity in series as opposed to relax­
ation or dilation, and (2) contraction as fusion of succession of elel11-
ents. With the second form of contraction, we C0111e upon the notion
of a "contel11plative soul." Deleuze knows that the notion of an


242
JOHN PROTEVI
organic " contemplative soul" Inight strike his readers as a "Inys­
tical or barbarous hypothesis/' but he pushes on: passive organic
synthesis is our "habit of life," our expectation that life will con­
tinue. So we Inust attribute a "conteinplative soul" to the heart, the
Inuscles, the nerves, the cells, whose role is to contract habits. This
is just extending to "habit" its full generality: habit in the organic
syntheses that we are (DR 74).9 Organic syntheses operate in series,
and each series has a rhythm; organism.s are polyrhythinic: "the
duration of an organism's present, or of its various presents, will
vary according to the natural contractile range of its contemplative
souls" (DR 77). The rhythm of organic syntheses can be seen from
two perspectives, "need" and "satiety" (or "fatigue" in the sense of
being tired of sOlnething, fed up with something). Deleuze writes:
"need In arks the limits of the variable present. The present extends
between two eruptions of need, and coincides with the duration of a
conteinplation" (DR 77). "Fatigue/' then, is being fed up, being over­
full, when "the soul can no longer conteinplate what it contracts"
(DR 77). There are thousands of such rhythinic periods between need
and fatigue, periods that compose the organic being of hUlnans: frOln
the long periods of childhood, puberty, adulthood, and menopause
to Inonthly honnonal cycles to daily cycles (circadian rhythms) to
heartbeats, breathing cycles, all the way down to neural firing pat­
terns. Everything organic, each "contemplative soul," has a period
of repetition, everything is a habit, and each one of these repetitions
forms a living present that synthesizes the retention of the past and
the anticipation of the future as need.
Organic individuation
To appreciate fully Deleuze's treatlnent of individuation in chap­
ter 5 of Difference and Repetition, we Inust Inake a brief foray
into Deleuze's Inetaphysics, where we find a tripartite ontological
scheme, positing three interdependent registers: the virtual, the
intensive, and the actual. For Deleuze, in all reahns of being (I)
intensive Inorphogenetic processes follow the structures inherent
in (2) differential virtuai lnultiplicities to produce (3) localized and
individuated actual substances with extensive properties and dif­
ferenciated qualities that, in the biological realm, can be used in
classification schemes that distinguish species from each other and


Deleuze and life
243
distinguish the organs of an organisln frOln each other. Silnply put,
the actualization of the virtual, that is, the production of the actual
things of the world, proceeds by way of intensive processes.
In a fuller picture of Deleuze's ontology, we see that the virtual
field is cOlnposed of "Ideas" or "lnultiplicities/, which are consti­
tuted by the progressive deternlination of differential elelnents, dif­
ferential relations, and singularitiesj what are related are precisely
intensive processes, thought as linked rates of change (DR I82-9I).
Beneath the actual (any one state of a systeln), we find "ilnpersonal
individuations" or intensive lnorphogenetic processes that produce

ities" (that is, the key elelnents in virtual fields, marking systeln
thresholds that structure the intensive morphogenetic processes).
We thus have to distinguish the intense "ilnpersonal" field of indi­
viduation and its processes frOln the virtual "pre-individual" field
of differential relations and singularities that nlake up an Idea or
lnultiplicity. But it's even lnore cOlnplex than just three Inodes or
registers, for we have to distinguish "individuation" as the field of
individuation (called variously "the egg" or the "lnetastable field"L
frOln "dralnatization" as the process of individuation (embryogen­
esis or "spatio-telnporal dynalnisms"). Deleuze has thus a fourfold
"order of reasons: differentiation-individuation-dramatization-dif­
ferenciation (organic and specific)" (DR 25 I, translation lnodified).
Differentiation is the rnark of the virtual, the "pre-individual/'
while differenciation is the mark of the actual, the fully individu­
ated end product. So both "individuation" and "dramatization"
are intensive and impersonalj they are the field and the process of
individuation.
A silnple exalnple distinguishing field and process of individu­
ation can be found in the Ineteorological register, where the field
of individuation is cOlnposed of the cloud-ground systeln with its
electrical potential differences, while lightning is the process of

scale, the field of individuation of a weather systeln would be bands
of different temperature and pressure in air and water which exist

which are the spatio-telnporal dynarnislns, the process of individu­
ation of a singular event, sometilnes worthy of its own narne, as
with hurricanes. In the biological register, an exarnple of the field


244
TOHN PROTEVI
of individuation is the egg, while the process of individuation is

ation back to a transcendental identity qua "genetic progranl" we

A very iInportant point for Deleuze in his account of the bio­
logicai lnodel for ontogenesis is the priority of individuation to dif­
ferenciation. In other words, singular differences in the genesis of
individuals Inust precede the categories into which they are put;

"Individuation precedes differenciation in principle ' "  every dif­
ferenciation presupposes a prior intense field of individuation. It

tialist "what is?" Individuals are singular events before they are
InelIlbers of species or genera; a species is a construct, an abstrac­
tion froln a varying population of singulars. Deleuze is insistent
here: we have to beware the "tendency to believe individuation is

puts it very strongly: "any reduction of individuation to a lilnit or
cOInplication of differenciation cOlnpromises the whole of the phil",
osophy of difference. This would be to commit an error, this time
in the actual, analogous to that made in confusing the virtual with
the possible" (DR 247). The key point is that "individuation does

tion Inodified). In other words, Deleuze Inust distinguish between
any comparable difference between individuals - difference within

rence," the difference thought by Darwin, the "differenciation of
difference," that which does not track genus and species but pro­
duces it via natural selection as a stabilizing procedure. Making

In seeking a concrete exaInple of the precedence of individuation,
Deleuze now turns to eInbryos, where he Inust finesse what looks

(that is, it is not produced by differenciation as conditioning the

scherne), but is "lived by the embryo" in the process of individua­
tion-drarnatization (DR 249, emphasis in original). Thus the "experi­
ence" of the ernbryo (drarnatization as "spatio-ternporal dynarnism"
or rnorphogenetic process, the third element in the order of reasons)
points "backwards" as it were to the first two elements of the order
of reasons (differentiation and "individuation" as field), rather than
"forward" to the fourth element (differenciation). The experience of
the enlbryo points to differential relations or virtuality "prior to the
actualization of the species" and it points to flfirst movenlents" or
the "condition" of actualization, that is, to individuation as it "finds
its field of constitution in the egg" (DR 249). This rneans that the
lived generality of the ernbryo points "beyond species and genus" to
the individual (that is, to the field of individuation and that process

relative acceleration" of the elements of the individuation process,

tion or the detennination of the species." Thus a species is an "illu­
sion - inevitable and well founded to be sure - in relation to the play
of the individual and individuation" (DR 249-50).
At this point, Deleuze provides a fascinating critique of genetic
determinism. First, we are rerninded again of the primacy of indi­
viduation over differenciation, and that the flernbryo is the individ­

the farnous phrase "the world is an egg" (DR 251) we read that "the
nucleus and the genes designate only the differentiated matter in

vidual field to be actualized; but their actualization is detennined
only by the cytoplasm, with its gradients and its fields of individu­
ation" (DR 251). Again, the virtual is "pre-individual," while the
intensive is "impersonal." By showing how the genetic expression
in ontogenesis is detennined by cytoplasrnic conditions Deleuze is


246
TOHN PROTEVI
thus prefiguring a lnove in contelnporary biology, known collect­

and transcendent genetic program to a differential network of gen­

differential virtual structuring organic individuation matches the
Deleuzian principle of critique, the outlawing of the tracing relation
between transcendental/virtual and empirical/actual, a principle
that cOlnlnands a non-resemblance of actualized species and parts

also commands the non-resemblance of both virtual multiplicity

The twists and folds of elnbryogenesis do not resenlble either the

the adult organisln.
To conclude this all-too-brief sketch of organic individuation in
Difference and Repetition, we see that, for Deleuze, the "principal
difficulty" of elnbryology is posing the field of individuation for­

species. But this reverses the order in which individuation precedes
differenciation. So we rnust conceive individuating difference as
individual difference: no two eggs are identical (DR 252). Organic
individuation, field and process together, is a singular event preced­
ing differenciation. Once we've seen that, we've traced the order of
reasons frOln (I) virtual differentiation through (2) the impersonal
and intensive field of individuation to (3) spatio-telnporal dynamisms
as the process of individuation or dralnatization to (4) differencia­
tion as the fonnation of species and "parts," that is, qualitatively
different cell types and functions which can then be classified in
taxonOlnic schemes.

Difference and Repetition is different in both form and content from
A Thousand Plateaus. While Difference and Repetition has the clas­
sical fonn of a these d'etat - and Anti- Oedipus still has sOlnething
of the saIne linear argument - A Thousand Plateaus is written as a

Deleuze and life

reading "transversally" across its chapters or "plateaus" (ATP 3-25).
We will thus present our reading of Anti- Oedipus and A Thousand

Repetition focuses on how individuation determines the actual­

on the intermeshing of different rhythIns of intensive processes.
Difference and Repetition is thus sonlewhat "vertically" oriented
(from virtual to actual and back), whereas A Thousand Plateaus is
more "transversal" (the Ineshing or clashing of intensive processes).
These relative eIllphases should not be hardened into theses, how­
ever, especially as the tenns "plane of consistency" and "abstract
Inachine" in A Thousand Plateaus do seeln to have an ontological
status a reader of Difference and Repetition would see as "virtual."
With these preliIninary remarks, let us turn to our treatment of

Vitalism
We began this chapter with one of Deleuze's provocations, in which
he proclaiIned his writings "vitalistic" (epater les bourgeois is an
old French philosophical gesture), but Deleuze is not vitalistic in

claims hinlself a Inonist and materialist in many passages; Deleuze

phenOlnenon of physical, biological, psychic, social, or cosmic mat­

nlaterialist, not a Inechanist, and it is only as a reaction to Inech­

sense of matter in Inechanism, as chaotic or passive, that creates
the teInptation to classical vitalislll of the "entelechy" type. Seeing
matter as chaotic or passive creates the need for a hylomorphic res­

a form that organizes the rnatter. But Deleuze learned frOln Gilbert
Silnondon to mistrust hylOlllorphisln, as much for its social origins
in cOlnmand relations as for its Inetaphysical assumptions (ATP
408-ro). What we need to look for in Deleuze's notion of vitalislll is
the "life" that encompasses both organislns and "non-organic life."

This life concerns the capacity for novel elnergent properties in the

Life
For Deleuze and Guattari, "life" has a double sense, reflecting both
stratification and de stratification. It means both "organisms" as a
certain set of stratified beings and also the creativity of complex
systems, their capacity to produce new elnergent properties, new
behavior patterns, by de stratifying and deterritorializing. OrganisIns
are "a particularly complex systern of stratification" (ATP 336),
while life qua creativity is "a surplus value of destratification . . .
an aggregate of consistency that disrupts orders, forms, and sub­
stances" (ATP 336, italics in original). In the second, creative sense,
one example of which is speciation, the creation of novel "orders,
forn1s, and substances," then, life is not lilnited to the organism
fonn: "the organism is that which life sets against itself in order
to limit itself" (ATP 503). This notion of life as creativity gives rise
to "the prodigious idea of Nonorganic Life" (ATP 411, italics in ori,·
ginal), which we gloss as creative self-organization of material sys­
tems in registers other than the "organisInic." As we will shortly
see, by "organism" Deleuze and Guattari mean both homeostatic
or autopoietic conservation of a living entity, and organ patterning
useful to social machines.

By various terms built around the word "Inachine," Deleuze and

apparent in the beginning of Anti- Oedipus, where a schizophrenic's

schizophrenic] does not live nature as nature [i.e., as separate frOITI
man], but as a process of production" (AO 2). The schizophrenic's
being in contact with nature as "process of production" allows us to

The tern1 used for the "profound life" of nature's process in

2). Universal in scope, desiring-production is also ilnlnanent and
non-subjective - there is no subject that lies behind the production,

Desiring-production is immanent, autonomous, self-constituting,

The Inachinic naturalism of Anti- Oedipus should not be con­

the Inouth)" (AO 5). Although these machinic connections are for

desiring-lnachines are often patterned by the social lnachines into

Inent that would allow for novel connections, often Inade by artists
(AO 3I).

Content and expression
In one of their Inost bewildering slogans, Deleuze and Guattari

In strata (actualized systems, to use the tenns of Difference and
Repetition), expression takes part in a double articulation, and ultirn­

properties, while in asselnblages (intensive fields and processes of

Evolution and involution
Deleuze and Guattari have a strong and a weak sense of the cre­
ative transfonnation involved in the production of biological nov­
elty. The strong sense is novelty that does not produce substantial
filiation (i.e., does not produce an organisln with descendants); this
can be connected to the notions of "niche construction" and "life
cycle" in DST. 15 The weak sense is novelty that does produce sub­
stantial filiation (an organisln with descendants); this can be con­
nected to the notions of "serial endosymbiosis" in the work of Lynn
Margulis16 and "developmental plasticity" in the work of Mary Jane
West -Eberhard. 17


Deleuze and life
253
The strong sense, which excludes substantial filiation, is expressed

Thousand Plateaus:
Finally, becOlning is not an evolution, at least not an evolution by des­
cent and filiation . . .  It concerns alliance. If evolution includes any veritable
becomings, it is in the domain of symbioses that bring into play beings of
totally different scales and kingdoms, with no possible filiations. There is
a block of becoming that snaps up the wasp and the orchid, but from which
no wasp-orchid can ever descend. (ATP 238, emphasis in original)
We can connect this to the thoughts of "niche construction" and
"life cycle" in DST. Here, "niche construction" looks to the way
organislns actively shape the environlnent and thus the evolutionary
selection pressures for thelnselves and their offspring. Thus evolu­
tion should be seen as the change in organisln-environlnent systems,
that is, the organism in its constructed niche. It's the "bec01ning"
of the organisln-in-its-niche that needs to be thought as the unit of
evolution (e.g., the "wasp-orchid"). In generalizing and radicalizing
the thought of niche construction, DST thinkers propose the "life
cycle" as the widest possible extension of developlnental resources
that are reliably present (or better, re-created) across generations.
DST thinkers thus extend the notion of inheritance beyond the
genetic to the cytoplaslnic environment of the egg (an extension
many Inainstrealn biologists have C01ne to accept) and beyond to
intra-organismic and even (most controversially) to extra-somatic
factors, that is, to the relevant, constructed, features of the physical
(for eXalnple, tennite colonies), IS biological (inherited symbionts),
and social environments (for example, normal brain developlnent
in humans needs positive corporeal affect and language exposure in

of evolution encOlnpassing intranuclear, cytoplasmic, organic, and
extra-somatic elen1ents COlnes close to what Deleuze and Guattari
refer to above as "symbioses that bring into play beings of totally
different scales and kingdolns."
The weak sense of biological novelty is that which does result in

As we note above, the discussion of evolution in A Thousand

Territories thelnselves have exterior, interior, intennediary, and

Body without organs or BwO
To reach the plane of consistency - to be open to new orderings and

The organs are not its enernies. The enelny is the organisrn" (ATP

Adding to the potential confusion is a significant change in the
tenn "full BwO" frOln Anti- Oedipus to A Thousand Plateaus. In
Anti-Oedipus, the BwO is "full" when it is catatonic, a rnOlnent

A BwO is not a regression to a natural state, despite the ilnpres­

A BwO is not reached by regression, for a BwO is not the infant­

As an object of practice reached starting frOln the organism, the

OrganiS1TI
We will conclude this tour of biophilosophical then1es with the

The organisl11 is an el11ergent effect of organizing organs in a par­

transcendences" (ATP I59). The organiSITI is hence a construction, a

CONCLUSION
We have provided a brief overview of some biophilosophical themes
in Difference and Repetition, Anti- Oedipus, and A Thousand
Plateaus. Researchers are working on exploring the relations
between Deleuze's works and that of the biologists he reads (see
note 3); further work is needed there as well as in using Deleuze's
conceptual SChelTIe(s) to discuss contemporary biophilosophical
concepts such as developmental plasticity;23 autopoiesis/4 "process
structuralism";25 serial endosYlTIbiosis theory;26 and DeveloplTIental

Systelns Theory (see note IO). Such "extended synthesis" Inodels
provide for Inulti-level, interlocking, distributed systelns for cell,
organ, organisln, and life cycle developlnent and function in an evo­
lutionary perspective. Deleuze provides a philosophical context
for this endeavor, one that provides a vocabulary and ontological
scheme for interlocking processes of the fonnation, defonnation,
and transfonnation of self-organizing physical-biological-political
systelns.

T HE WRENCHING D UALITY OF AES THETICS
FrOln a certain point of view, it appears to be quite possible to char­

n1anner grounded in aesthetics. This possibility arises as a func­

arises in a discussion of the Platonic distinction between well­
founded copies that reselnble the Idea and copies called simulacra,
which exist as a perversion of or deviation from the Platonic notion
of the Idea (LS 256-58). What is significant in this account is the
claim that Platonisln founds the philosophical domain of represen­
tation realized through copies-icons that are understood to be the
product of an intrinsic relation to the model that serves as their
foundation (LS 257). We may venture to say, then, that Deleuze is
interested in this intrinsic relation and in the distinction between
representations or true copies and shnulacra, which are distorted or
false copies. As we will see, the distortion of figuration pushed by
the simulacra will be central to Deleuze's aesthetic thinking. It is,
he says, the critical edge of Inodernity, destroying both models and
their true copies (representations) in order to institute the creative
chaos (LS 264, 266).
The development of this theme and of the structure supporting
it appears to have continued throughout Deleuze's work, thus its
introduction sets the stage for Deleuze's notion of philosophy in
general. Referring to the wrenching duality, Deleuze states that aes­
thetics designates, first, a theory of sensibility, insofar as sensibility
is assulned to be the form of any possible experience. As the form
of any possible experience, aesthetics is clearly situated as an aspect

REAS ON'S IDEA
How an aesthetical judgInent comes about for Kant is a Inatter of
some importance as it provides Deleuze with the Idea organizing
his own logic of sensation, his account of the manner in which the
aesthetic Idea, the theory of sensibility with which we began, pro­
vides the form of any possible experience. How then does one arrive
at the aesthetical COlnInon sense necessary for free Imagination
and undetermined Understanding? The answer is that it requires
a shock, which COlnes frOln elsewhere, from the one faculty not
yet involved, which is Reason. Initially, Reason, the faculty of
transcendental Ideas, played no role in aesthetic sensation, but it
will be of the greatest iInportance because it situates the faculties
with respect to Nature's physical forces. The judgment of the sub­
liIne, unlike that of the beautiful, takes place in relation to form­
lessness, to boundlessness.12 And unlike the judgInent of beauty,
a judgInent that is compatible with the play of Imagination, the
judgInent of the sublime stops us cold. We encounter Nature in its
fonnless chaos, at its wildest and most irregular, in disorder and
desolation, where it is only size beyond measure, or power beyond
cOlnprehension.
Nevertheless, the apprehension of the Inost fundamental unit uti­
lized by mathelnatics is also aesthetic, as is the Inere apprehension


Deleuze's aesthetics of sensation
of any Inagnitude. Utilizing Inathematics, we Inay, in principle,

successive apprehension of one unit following another is always

F IGURATIVE NARRATION OR ASIGNIFYING F IGURES
In The Logic of Sensation, Deleuze begins by distinguishing differ­
ent sorts of artworks. Figurative work is illustrative and narrative
as well as signifying. Classical painting, especially single-point per­
spective painting, seems to be the target here. When works of art
"escape" the Classical Figurative work of art, they do so either by
veering into pure form through abstraction as so much modern art
does, or into what Deleuze calls the purely figural, achieved through
isolation, in which the painting is an isolated reality and the Figure
is isolated in the painting in a way that prevents any sort of narrative

2). Iconic inlages of this type are Inost often associated with Inedieval

So, it is perhaps not surprising when Deleuze Inaintains that the
work of Francis Bacon is not abstract like the artworks of abstract

either on the level of Understanding (narrative and illustrative) or on

Deleuze states, with respect to his own work, that Nature is
structured like Inathelnatical vector space, so it is crucial for us
to realize that in mathelnatics, the laws governing vector space
are identical to the Kantian categories of relation (DR 1994). These
laws of association, cOlnmutation, and distribution are carried

and accidents, cause and effect, and reciprocity between agent and
patient. When translated into logical tenns, the categories of rela­
tion represent the categorical relation (All S are P), the hypothetical
(If S then P), and the disjunctive relation (Either S or P).48 Of all the
categories that unite our representations under a single concept and
so yield knowledge, only the categories of relation are derived exclu­
sively frOln logic. As such, the categories of relation have no need of
sensible intuitions but ilnlnediately become cognitions.49 The lack
of the requirement for sensible intuitions allows Reason to extend
the categories of relation beyond experience, beyond sensible intui­
tions to supersensible Ideas. Reason thus produces the supersensi­
ble Ideas - Self (substance), World (the chain of causal events), God
(the capacity to join and disjoin) - which Reason is driven to extend
to infinity thereby creating supersensible Ideas, Ideas for which we

What supersensible Ideas can do is constitute what Deleuze

to qualitative changes. These are the intensities of ontological lneln­
ory.56 What belongs to nlelnory is heterogeneous but indivisible, but
what belongs to the intensities of external forces can be broken up

Deleuze describes, so powerfully, and which he thinks clear away

FRANCIS BACON, GENIUS
Like Kant's genius, this is where Deleuze's genius starts. Spirit,
the animating principle of the l11ind, puts the faculties into play.58
h11agination creates another Nature out of the l11aterial that actual
Nature gives it. We are freed from the C01111110nplace law of associ­
ation; we rernold 111 at t er; the defon11ations COl11mence. "The poet
ventures to realize, to sense rational Ideas of invisible beings, the
kingdo111 of the blessed, hell, eternity, creation . . .  he tries . . .  to go
beyond the 1i111its of experience and to present thel11 to Sense."59
This is how it comes about that the intensive body is traversed by
a wave tracing its thresholds, varying its al11plitudes in accordance
with thel11, acting on the body which SCrea111S and breathes, releas­
ing the hysteria of painting. This autoscopia is feeling oneself inside
a head, in a body, in the naked body, capturing forces and render­
ing thel11 visible (FB 44-55). The sensation, Deleuze claims, is like
Kant's, lively and full of spirit. Not entropy, but the clinamen, atoms
falling in the void, but still according to rules! "In the void, the vel­
ocity of the atom is equal to its l11ovel11ent in a unique direction in
a l11inimum of continuous time" (LS 269). Perhaps Deleuze thinks
that atoms can escape the second law of thermodyna111ics through
the catastrophe. But every Baconian triptych is unified by the law of
distribution, the unity of light and color distributed across the can­
vases, and Deleuze adl11its that in these paintings, til11e is a Inono­
chrOlnatic eternity (FB 85). Nothing more entropic in the universe.
How does one become capable of painting in this way? Bacon's
act of painting consists of randOl11 l11arks, scrubbing, sweeping, and
wiping the canvas, removing the figurative and narrative givens of
painting, doing away with perspective and representation. It is, says
Deleuze, as if a catastrophe has overcome the canvas. Catastrophe
theory belongs, at least in part, to the physics of structurally stable


282
DOROTHEA OLKOWSKI
systenls where patterns repeat thenlselves in generally regular
l11anners. But discontinuities appear, and catastrophe theory is an
attel11pt to provide a l11athel11atical fra111ework for describing these
processes.60 If we take up Kant's han110ny of the faculties, the l11an­
ner in which concepts of the Understanding provide concepts for
h11agination's intuitions, all under the influence of Reason, which
seeks to unify consciousness, then we can aSSUl11e that Reason is
a fixed-point attractor in an abstract space. Every til11e a concept
is applied to an intuition, it can be said to occur as a point in the
abstract state space of the faculties, ultil11ately producing an entire
surface of experience. Non11ally, shifts in intuition are minil11al.
But what if Reason del11ands, as we saw it does, that h11agination
attend to an infinitely large or powerful Intuition that exceeds its
finite capacity? The result, we said, is that h11agination is crushed
and must shift to an entirely new region of its faculties. It l11USt
shift in a discontinuous l11anner frOl11 Understanding to Reason.
The point where this discontinuity occurs is called a catastrophe
point.61 Recall, however, what we said above. When the artist under­
goes the encounter with the sublime, the feeling is one of joy over
being freed from the limits of h11agination and Understanding. The
freedOl11 is prerequisite to the original and exel11plary beautiful
works of art of genius. Imagination "emulates the play of Reason
in its quest after a maxil11um, to go beyond the limits of experience
and to present thel11 to Sense with a cOl11pleteness of which there
is no exanlple in Nature.//62 Following the catastrophic break with
Understanding, h11agination is free to approxiI11ate Reason's Ideas,
but this means, also, a destabilization. These presentations do not
"represent what lies in our concepts of the sublil11ity and l11ajesty of
creation/' rather Imagination spreads itself over numerous kindred
representations, arousing l110re thought than can be expressed in a
concept to furnish an aesthetical Idea.63 In this way, the kindred
representations enliven the l11ind and stil11ulate the h11agination,
but no single concept gives a rule for those representations.
Perhaps we can say that the paintings of Francis Bacon capture
the sensation of the sublil11e, the catastrophe. Perhaps we can also
say that sensation in Bacon's paintings is the effect of the action
of invisible forces on the body producing, not feelings nor repre­
sentations of violent feelings, but affects. And the l11ovel11ent of
such affects is l110vement in place, spasms, contraction, dilation


Deleuze's aesthetics of sensation
in contact with Nature's physical forces, what Deleuze calls here
its vital power, violent and catastrophic. In this sense, for Deleuze,
Bacon's paintings, regardless of the screalning, disintegrating heads,
express optilnisln and belief in life, where life Ineans the physical
forces acting on bodies. And perhaps this is analogous to Cezanne's
Nature, to the extent that Cezanne gives feeling to geOlnetry, so
that we feel the presence of the ilnage as lnuch as we see it (FE I I2).
FrOln Deleuze's point of view, this is surely the case.
All this adds support to the idea, which we proposed at the begin­
ning of this essay, that all of Deleuze's philosophy Inay be encoun­
tered fronl the point of view of aesthetics. It is a philosophy that
takes its orientation frOln the wrenching duality that Deleuze says
characterizes aesthetics. First, the theory of sensibility as the form
of possible experience that takes us out of figuration and brings us
to the reality of body, lines, and colors freed of organic representa­
tion, freed of the lnaterial reality of bodies (FE 55). And second, it is
a theory of art, not lnerely as a reflection on art, but as a reflection
on this new sort of real experience that unlocks areas of sensation
that are neither representation nor the "nless/' the confused sen­
sation of abstract expressionisln, nor the pure abstraction of min­
ilnalism (FE I02-3). What this aesthetic advocates is nothing other
than the hysteria of the senses. One begins with categories of the
Understanding, their representational and narrative organization,
then one clears theln away in what, for Deleuze, is the sublilne work
of genius: isolation, defonnation, dissipation, lnelting, but, ultim­
ately, eternal tilne (FE 63-64).

Deleuze and literature
In Cinema I and Cinema 2 Deleuze offered a relatively comprehen­
sive philosophical approach to film. Unfortunately, he produced no
cOlllparable work on literature, despite having shown a deep and
lasting interest in the art. Besides producing monographs on Marcel
Proust (1964; 2nd aug. edn. 1970; 3rd aug. edn. 1976), Leopold von
Sacher-Masoch (1967), and Franz Kafka (1975), he wrote long essays on
Pierre Klossowski (1965), Michel Tournier (1967), Elllile Zola (1967),
Canllelo Bene (1979), Herman Melville (1989), and Samuel Beckett
(1992), and in The Logic of Sense (1969) used Lewis Carroll's works as
a leitlllotif throughout the book. Indeed, allusions to writers are so
abundant that Dominique Drouet's index of Deleuze's literary ref΂
erences runs to 279 entries.! In a 1988 interview, Raymond Bellour
and Fran<;ois Ewald noted the absence of a literary counterpart to
the cinema books, even though literature "is everywhere present in
your work, running parallel, ahnost, to the philosophy," and asked
if literature were too close to philosophy for him to undertake such
a work. Deleuze answered that literature posed no special difficulty
for him, and that in fact he had "dreaIned about bringing together a
series of studies under the general title 'Essays Critical and Clinical,'
[but] it's just that I haven't had the chance to do the book I'd like
to have done about literature" (N 142-43). Deleuze's last book, of
course, bore the title Essays Critical and Clinical (1993), but its het­
erogeneous studies of writers and philosophers hardly lays out a
general and detailed philosophy of literature.
Nonetheless, Essays Critical and Clinical's brief opening essay,
"Literature and Life," does bring together thelnes introduced at
various points in Deleuze's career, and if the essay is not the Inicro­
Summa of a single poetics unifying all his literary studies, it still
286


Deleuze and literature
preserves traces of Deleuze's journey through literature while offer­
ing an exalnple of his genius for selectively gathering, synthesizing,
and transfonning elelnents of his earlier thought within intri­
guingly new configurations. The essay's few pages abound with
dicta about writing and literature. "Writing is a question of becOln­
ing" (ECC I); "Literature then appears as an enterprise of health"
(ECC 3); "Health, as literature, as writing, consists in inventing a
people who are Inissing" (ECC 4); "Literature is delirium" (ECC 54);
"The ultilnate ailn of literature is to set free, in the delirium, this
creation of health or this invention of a people, that is, a possibil­
ity of life" (ECC 4); literature "opens up a kind of foreign language
within language" (ECC 6); "a foreign language cannot be hollowed
out in one language without language as a whole in turn being top­
pled or pushed to a limit, to an outside or reverse side that consists
of Visions and Auditions that no longer belong to any language"
(ECC 5); "To write is also to becOlne sOlnething other than a writer"
(ECC 6). BecOlning, health, inventing a people, delirium, possibility
of life, foreign language within language, Visions and Auditions -
save for Visions and Auditions, all of these thelnes appear in works
preceding Essays Critical and Clinical, yet their synthesis in this
essay grants thern renewed intensity as cOlnponents of a decades­
long, open-ended thought-in-nlOvelnent about literature, whose
genealogy is worth tracing.
Deleuze's writings on literature fall into two principal groups,
pre- and post-Guattari (the two met in mid I970), the first focus­
ing on psychoanalytic themes and the interpretation of signs, the
second on sociopolitical dynamics and the deterritorialization of
language. Deleuze introduces several new concepts in his essays
on literature in the I980s and I990S, including fabulation, possi­
bilities of life, and Visions and Auditions, while cOlnbining them
with reconfigured versions of some Inotifs frOln his pre-Guattari
writings (critical and clinical, the event), but I see these late essays
as further elaborations on the fundamental concerns inaugurated in
his initial collaboration with Guattari.
EARLY DELEUZE
The two central texts in Deleuze's early thought about literature
are Proust and Signs (I964) and Masochisln: Coldness and Cruelty

RONALD BOGUE
(I967). It is telTIpting to seek a developlTIental ITIOVeITIent frOITI the
Proust book to the ITIonograph on Sacher-Masoch, but in fact the
basic outline of the latter study appeared in a I96I article, and

that the tenTIS "critical" and "clinical" (critique and clinique) first

ITIent. Thus, if there is a single ITIotif present in the earliest and the
latest of Deleuze's literary writings, it is that of the critical and the
clinical.
Like rnost of Deleuze's literary comlTIentaries, Masochism
advances a provocative thesis
in this instance, that sadisITI and
ITIasochislTI have little to do with one another, and that Sade and
Masoch were great clinicians whose works disclose the psychody­
namics of widely divergent universes. SadornasochislTI is a false
syndrome, Deleuze argues, a ITIistaken grouping of symptOlTIS that
obfuscates a clear understanding of sadism and rnasochism alike. To
rethink this false syndrome, Deleuze proposes a literary approach,
"since it is from literature that steIn the original definitions of sad­
ism and masochisITI." He suggests that lithe critical (in the liter­
ary sense) and the clinical (in the rnedical) sense . . .  enter into a
new relationship of mutual learning," because "symptOlTIatology is
always a question of art," and in this case, "the clinical specificities
of sadislTI and Inasochism are not separable frOlTI the literary values
peculiar to Sade and Masoch" (M I4).
Sade posits the existence of two natures: a secondary nature of
procreation and death, and a priITIary nature of pure negation, "an
original delirium, a prirllordial chaos solely cOlTIposed of furious
and lacerating molecules." Secondary nature alone exists in the
real world; priITIary nature, by contrast, is an Idea, delirious no
doubt, but "a deliriuln proper to reason" (M 27, translation modi­
fied). The sadist enacts such a priITIary nature through an apathetic,
rational deITIOnstration of this deliriuITI, in repetitive scenes of vio­
lence supervised by the father and directed against the procreative
ITIother. Masoch also posits two natures, but of an entirely differ­
ent sort: a secondary nature of sensuality and disorder inducing a


Deleuze and literature
constant war between n1en and W0111en, and a prilTIary nature of
cool suprasensuality and strict order. The presiding figure in this
prilTIary nature is the oral lTIother, "cold, ITIaternal and severe" (M
5 1). In the n1asochistic fantasy, the son fonTIs an alliance with the
oral n10ther, and it is the father she punishes when she whips the
son, at the SalTIe tilTIe that she purifies the son and 111akes possible
the parthenogenesis of a new ITIan, freed frOITI the sensuality and
disorder of secondary nature. If the sadist stands above the law in
open defiance, the ITIasochist ITIocks the law by parodically obey­
ing it, drawing up elaborate contracts with the dOlTIinatrix in order
to turn legal prohibitions of desire into sources of pleasure. Rather
than reiterate a rational deliriuITI of constant ITIotion and destruc­
tion, the masochist fashions motionless tableaux of suspended
desire. The fetish plays a special role for the lTIasochist, since it is
an object of Freudian disavowal, son1ething that enables a simul­
taneous acceptance and denial of reality (specifically, according to
Freud, the reality of the lTIother's lack of a penisL and thereby allows
the real to pass into fantasy. Hence, "disavowal, suspense, waiting,
fetishislTI and fantasy together make up the specific constellation of
lTIasochisIl1" (M 7 1). Delirious reason is not the lTIasochist's presid­
ing faculty, but perverse imagination, whose goal is not to impose a
violent Idea on the real, but "to neutralize the real and suspend the
ideal within the pure interiority of the fantasy itself" (M 72, trans­
lation lTIodified).
Both Sade and Masoch start with a personal obsession, but unlike
ordinary sadists or lTIasochists, they convert their obsessions into
lTIaterials for artistic creation. In their fiction, each fashions a double
of the world, a counter-real that distils the structures and practices

diagnosis of these illnesses of civilization. And both suggest new
possibilities beyond these illnesses, Sade, through ironic exagger­

pornographers, but "pornologists," whose ailTI is to confront "lan­
guage with its own lilTIits, with what is in a sense a 'nonlanguage'
(violence that does not speak, eroticislTI that relTIains unspoken)" (M
22). They create a double of the world, but they also "fonTI in lan­
guage a sort of double of language, capable of acting directly on the

RONALD BOGUE
In his 1988 interview with Bellour and Ewald, Deleuze links
Masoch and Proust as "civilization's doctors/' calling Masoch "a
great sYlnptOlnatologist" and labeling Proust's A la recherche du
temps perdu "a general selniology, a sYlnptOlnatology of different
worlds" (N 142-43). But in the initial 1964 edition of Proust and
Signs, Deleuze puts little stress on the notion of signs as sYlnptonls,
and nowhere does he describe Proust as a physician of civilization.
Deleuze's focus is on signs in a broader sense, albeit one that owes
nothing to Saussure. Signs in Proust's Recherche are like the lnoon,
with a bright and a perpetually dark side. They are hieroglyphs,
eniglnas that elicit interpretation. Each sign enfolds, or ilnplicates
(Latin plicare, to foldL sOlnething hidden, and requires unfolding, or
explication, to be grasped. The Recherche is the record of the nar­
rator's apprenticeship in signs, one that leads Marcel frOln worldly
signs, through the signs of love and signs of involuntary lnemory,
to the signs of art. Each of these four groups of signs has its own
temporality: "tirrIe wasted, time lost, time rediscovered, and tirne
regained" (PS 87). Worldly signs are vapid, elnpty indices of fashion,
status, and the ephelneral preoccupations of salons, coteries, and
cliques. The signs of love are sornewhat more substantial, yet decep­
tive and disappointing, revealed not through reciprocated love but
through jealousy and the lover's exclusion frOln the hidden worlds
enfolded in the loved one. Ultimately, these signs reveal a bifurca­
tion in sexuality, such that all sexuality is hOlnosexuality, even het­
erosexual lnen and women loving their respective lnale or female
counterparts within their lovers. The signs of involuntary Inelnory,
such as the well-known lnadeleine that evokes a distant COlnbray,
unlike worldly signs and the signs of love, do not represent wasted
or lost tilne, but instead a rediscovery of what Proust calls "a lnor­
sel of tirrIe, in the pure state/' or in Deleuze's words, "the local­
ized essence of tilne" (PS 61). The lnadeleine enfolds an essence
of COlnbray, a pure past that has never been actually present. In a
Proustian phrase Deleuze cites frequently in his writings, essences
are "real without being present, ideal without being abstract" (PS
61). Yet the signs of involuntary lnelnory are contingent, revealed
solely in haphazard circumstances, and only partially separable from
their lnaterial elnbodinlent. The signs of art alone reveal essences
directly and fully, and though they are cloaked in the materials of
paint, sounds, or words, they are "imlnaterial and no longer have


Deleuze and literature
anything opaque about thenl," for in art, Iisubstances are spiritual­
ized, Inedia denlaterialized" (PS 50).
Proustian essences, however, are not Platonic ideas. They do not
belong to sonle separatel ideal world, but are ilnnlanent within the
real. They are virtual rather than actual. An essencel as revealed in
an artwork, "is a difference, the absolute and ultinlate Difference"
(PS 41). It is a generativel self-differentiating difference that con­
stantly repeats itself. A virtual essence guides and infornls the indi­
viduation of the substance in which it is elnbodiedl this process of
actualization of the virtual being one of "expression/' as Deleuze
calls itl a Inaterial unfolding of enfolded difference in which
the actual is the expression of the virtual. Each essence is like a
Leibnizian Inonad, which expresses the entire world, but only frOln
its own perspective, revealing the world clearly in its proper region,
obscurely outside that area. The essence of an artwork likewise per­
spectivally expresses an entire worldl but that world itself, when
grasped fully rather than locally, resenlbles a Neoplatonic conlpli­
catiol "which envelops the Inany in the One and affinns the unity
of the Inultiplell (PS 45). In Proust, however, the One is self-differ­
entiating difference, not self-saIne identity. Essences, then, prove
to be not Inerely artistic but coslnic, and artistic creation is siInply
a particular instance of the world's ubiquitous actualization of the
virtual, the universal unfolding of incessantly repeated difference.
Marcel's apprenticeship in signs leads hiln to the revelation of
essences and to his vocation as a writer, yet if the Recherche is an
apprenticeship in the signs of art, Proust's thought about signs is
as lnuch philosophical as artistic - or at least, Deleuze lnakes use
of the Recherche as both an artwork and a work of philosophy. In
Proust and Sign's final chapter, "The I1nage of Thought," Deleuze
credits Proust with "setting up an ilnage of thought in opposition
to that of philosophy" (PS 94), one in which truth is not disclosed
through the good will of friends consciously deciding to seek it, but
through a violence to thought. What Proust shows is that lithe truth
is not revealed, it is betrayed; it is not conl111unicated, it is inter­
preted; it is not willed, it is involuntary" (PS 95). Those falniliar
with Deleuze's Difference and Repetition (1968) will recognize in
this cOlnnlentary on Proust a rough pT(icis of Deleuze's own argu­
nlent in Difference and Repetition's third chapter (also titled liThe
I1nage of Thought"), just as they will find expanded counterparts of


292
RONALD BOGUE
Proust and Signs's discussions of difference, repetition and expres­
sion in Difference and Repetition (as well as in Deleuze's lengthy
I968 study of Spinoza, Expressionisnl in Philosophy Spinoza).
In The Logic of Sense (I969), this intilnate relationship between
literature and philosophy continues, as does the close association of
literature and psychoanalysis found in Masochism. A "logical and
psychoanalytic novel" (LS xiv, translation Inodified), The Logic of
Sense isolates in Lewis Carroll's nonsense works "a series of para­
doxes which fonn the theory of sense" (LS xiii), a theory Deleuze
develops through meditations on diverse philosophical argu­
ments, including, among lnany others, those of the Stoics, Leibniz,
Nietzsche, Frege, Meinong, and Husserl. Deleuze grants the Stoics
pride of place because they lay the foundation for a theory of sense
via their concept of the lekton, or "expressible." For the Stoics, bod­
ies alone have true existence, but other entities, called "incorpore­
als," have a quasi-existence, insisting, or subsisting, if not genuinely
existing. Bodies are causes of thelnselves, incorporeals lnere surface
effects of bodies. The lekton is one such incorporeal. When foreign­
ers overhear an unknown language, they perceive the saIne sonic
bodies as the native speakers, but for those native speakers, SOlne­
thing is added to the sonic bodies - lekta, expressibles, incorporeal
surface effects of lneaning, or sense. Via this unusual differenti­
ation of bodies, causes, and being from incorporeals, effects, and
quasi-being, Deleuze formulates a broad theory of sense as an incor­
poreal surface at the interface of language and things, a transcen­
dental, lnetaphysical surface of events, with their own floating tilne
and paradoxical logic.
In the last third of The Logic of Sense, Deleuze turns from a
philosophical account of sense to a psychoanalytic explanation
of its psychosexual emergence along the surfaces of the body.
Prelilninary to this psychoanalytic analysis, however, is a crucial
chapter ("Thirteenth Series of the Schizophrenic and the Little
Girl") dedicated to Carroll's "Jabberwocky" and Antonin Artaud's
"translation" of the poem. Carroll's nonsense poeln has sense, just

Deleuze and literature
293
cTis]" (LS 88) without gralnlnar or syntax. What these sonic blocks

The literary texts of Carroll and Artaud, then, contribute to

DELEUZE AND GUATTARI
Anti- Oedipus (1972) Inarks an end to Deleuze's utilization of con­

Minor literature has three characteristics. First, it is not written in a

295
sense the absence of a national or con11nunity consciousness, and as

I1nplicit in this characterization of lninor literature is a concep­

Even a language's selnantic c01nponents are penneated by lines

that function together while

repugnant or diabolical way? We don't know, but we have ideas

The Trial, then, is not an oneiric fantasy or a religious allegory.

In the aesthetics of minor literature, then, Deleuze and Guattari

LATE DELEUZE

Deleuze and literature
301
Rather, after 1975 Deleuze speaks not of "lninor literature" but of

In all these processes, the ailn is to find a line of flight that leads

Besides engendering lines of flight through becOlnings, Deleuze

the cOlnponents of a collective assemblage" (K 84). This motif of a
virtual cOlnmunity is only briefly touched on in Kafka, but Deleuze

Literature, then, is both becOlning and fabulation, the prolonga­

introduced in Kafka and A Thousand Plateaus. Yet in "Literature

The concept of Visions and Auditions only elnerges explicitly in

and exchange," and for that reason "we really have to see philoso­
phy, art and science as sorts of melodic lines that are foreign to one

fied). Despite their mutual interference and intercession, however,

is Philosophya, Deleuze and Guattari emphasize the divide between

whereas artists create sensations (which they define as "percepts"
and "affects"). Even figures such as Kierkegaard and Nietzsche, who

Yet in Deleuze's thought this division between philosophy and
literature ultimately seerIlS porous and provisional. In a short text
on Spinoza written in 1989, two years before What is Philosophya,
Deleuze says that "style in philosophy strains toward three different
poles: concepts, or new ways of thinking; percepts, or new ways of
seeing and construing; and affects, or new ways of feeling. They're
the philosophical trinity, philosophy as opera: you need all three
to get things moving" (N 164-65). And in "Spinoza and the Three


Deleuze and literature
'Ethics'" frOlll Essays Critical and Clinical, published two years
after What is Philosophya, Deleuze says that Spinoza's Ethics is
actually three books: a "river-book" of "definitions, axiOlllS and pos­
tulates, delllonstrations and corollaries" that flows through Books
I-IV; a volcano book of interspersed scholia; and Book v, which is "an
aerial book of light" (ECC 151). And the three books' fonlls of con­
tent and expression are respectively "Notions or concepts," "Signs
or affects" and "Essences or percepts" (ECC 138). (In the 1989 text
on Spinoza, Deleuze says that in Book v Spinoza ceases "speaking
frOlll the viewpoint of concepts" and begins to "speak directly and
intuitively in pure percepts" [N 165].)
Deleuze claillls that the basic problelll faced by artists does not
"take a different fonll in literature than in the other arts" (N 143) and
that literature's proxilllity to philosophy poses no special difficul­
ties for hilll. Yet there is an illlportant difference between literature
and the other arts. When working as a philosopher, Deleuze is not a
painter or a COlllposer, but he is a writer, and llluch of what he says
about writing applies equally well to his own linguistic practice and
to that of the authors he adlllires. In A Thousand Plateaus, when
he and Guattari describe the book as "a little lllachine" with its
own "body without organs" (ATP 4), they offer Kleist and Kafka as
eXaIllples, yet they are clearly characterizing their own book when
they talk about the books of these authors. Deleuze bridles at the
assertion that philosophers, unlike creative writers, have no style,
countering that "great philosophers are also great stylists." Style in
philosophy involves the invention or redefinition of words, but also
syntax, "which is a sort of straining toward something that isn't
syntactic, nor even linguistic (sOlllething outside language)" (N 164).
Clearly, philosophers and literary authors who create Visions and
Auditions are both stylists who strain toward something outside
language. When asked whether A Thousand Plateaus was a work of
literature, Deleuze responded that it was "philosophy, nothing but
philosophy, in the traditional sense of the word" (TRM 176, transla­
tion modified), and it would be foolish not to take hilll at his word.
But it does seelll that philosophy and literature share a broader zone
of indiscernibility than those between philosophy and the other
arts - the zone of language and writing, a zone that grants literature
a special place in Deleuze's work and lllakes literature one of the
lllOSt powerful "intercessors" in his thought.

What
happens
when
psychoanalysis
encounters

Ultimately, the result is its transformation into schizoanalysis, of
course, thanks in large part to the collaboration with Guattari. But
Deleuze brings to the encounter a whole set of conceptual resources
derived from Spinoza, Leihniz, Kant, Nietzsche, Bergson, and Jung,
just as Guattari brings to the collaboration invaluable resources
derived frOln Marx, Hjelmslev, and Lacan.I Perhaps most import­
ant: Deleuze had developed a distinctive philosophical understand­
ing of the unconscious before addressing psychoanalysis itself in
works such as Logic of Sense and Anti-Oedipus. So it is critical to
eXalnine the sense of unconsciousness that elnerges from Deleuze's
readings of Nietzsche, Kant, Bergson, and Jung as necessary context
for explaining what happens to psychoanalysis when it becomes
schizoanalysis through Deleuze's collaboration with Guattari.2
BEFORE PSYCHOANALYSIS :  A PHILOS OPHICAL
UNCONS CIOUS
We start with Nietzsche for a number of reasons: first of all, because
Nietzsche is the Inost important of the three great Inaterialists
(including Freud) on whom Deleuze will draw in Anti- Oedipus, and
because it is he who provides the most capacious sense of uncon­
sciousness. For Nietzsche, hUlnan being expresses will to power, and
will to power is mostly unconscious; consciousness is strictly epi­
phenomenal. Moreover, what consciousness there is for Nietzsche
is transitory and unreliable: the psyche is a battleground for war­
ring forces or perspectives, and consciousness represents Inerely
the momentary victory of one partial perspective over others
or


308
EUGENE W. HOLLAND
indeed its disguise, as son1ething other than conquering force. Most
ilnportantly, though: Nietzsche provides in1portant correctives to
Kant, one of Deleuze's lnost favored and influential philosophical
precursors, despite his idealisln.
Where Nietzsche (arguably in response to Kant hilnself) fractured the
psyche, Kant sought to unify the psyche and hannonize its faculties,
and to systelnatize knowledge by bringing the intuitions of sens­
ibility into consistent correspondence with the concepts of under­
standing under the supervision of the regulative Ideas of Reason
(Self, World, and God). Even where Kant recognizes that sensible
experience can have no corresponding concept - as in the case of the
sublilne - he subjects intuition to Reason (and the Idea of infinity).
Following Nietzsche, Deleuze will dissect, disaggregate, and disor­
ganize the Kantian psyche, yet without dismissing SOlne of its parts
altogether. Using the sublime as a kind of wedge, Deleuze refutes
Kant's three regulative Ideas of reason as well as the Transcendental
Unity of Apperception that would add a unified subject ("I think") to
all experience: as a general rule rather than the exception, experience
defies subsumption by the understanding and becomes Problelnatic;
not only is God long since dead, but the Self is not self-identical
(it is composed of partial and competing larval selves, instead) and
the World is not cOlnpletely or even predOlninantly knowable (it is
instead composed of ephelneral islands of Being all but submerged
in chaos and indeterminacy)} The very forms of space and tilne that
our experience takes are not the product of conscious intention; the
syntheses of the imagination, lneanwhile, will continue to produce
experience, but no longer under the aegis of Reason and understand­
ing: consciousness is not the subject of but rather subject to the syn­
theses
which Deleuze will insist on calling "passive" syntheses
for this very reason. Even the third synthesis of recognition in Anti­
Oedipus, when conscious awareness finally supervenes ("So that's
what that was!"),4 occurs to the subject rather than being under its
conscious control (as Wittgenstein aptly illustrates with the compos­
ite figure of the duck-rabbit which we alternately "see as" as one or
the other, lnore or less involuntarily).5 Without the prelnise of a uni­
fied sovereign subject governed by Reason, lnuch of the productive
activity of the Kantian psyche turns out to be unconscious, and we
become conscious of it - if we ever do only ex post facto.


Deleuze and psychoanalysis
Bergson's sense of unconsciousness, like Nietzsche's, relates it Inore
directly to action than to experience and cognition, and situates it
in a broadly ethological rather than a narrower psychological con­
text.6 For Bergson, consciousness represents the interruption of an
otherwise autOlnatic or autonOlnic "sensorY-Inotor schelna" that
ordinarily relates a specific response-behavior to a perceptual trig­
ger in the environn1ent. Behavior that is cOlnpletely detennined by
instinct Deleuze is fond of citing the tick's "drop!" response to the
trigger-scent of the chelnicals in lnamlnalian sweat - leaves no rOOlTI
for conscious thought, or what Bergson calls intelligence. But actual
hlllnan behavior, not being cOlnpletely detennined instinctually,
alternates or is supplelnented with intelligent contelnplation, reflec­
tion, and recollection. Rather than trigger an ilnlnediate behavioral
response, a perceived ilnage lnay inspire reverie, or lnay recall iInages
frOln the past. Whereas in Freud's analysis of neurosis, repression of
a specific tralllnatic past defonns action in the present, for Bergson
conscious hlllnan action in the present requires repression of the past
as a whole, or at least repression of most of the past, except when spe­
cific elements of the past are brought to bear on a situation requiring
action in the present. So for Bergson, lnost of the past relnains uncon­
scious lnost of the tilne, except when hesitation to act in the pre­
sent calls smne portion of the past to consciousness in order to help
perform an action, or when cOlnpletely free frOln any delnands for
present action we contelnplate the past for its own sake. As Deleuze
insists in an early essay, the past in itself "is the unconscious, or lnore
precisely, as Bergson says, the virtual" (DI 29). So for Bergson and
Deleuze, a philosophical unconscious - the virtual past as a coexist­
ing whole - elnerges out and because of the gap in non-instinctually
detennined hUlnan being between perception and action.
And yet hUlnans are by no lneans purely contelnplative beings:
they act; and they act to smne degree in accord with instinct and to
a large degree in response to perceptions. So what bridges the gap
between perception and action in hUlnan being? For Bergson, the
bridge depends on the interplay of instinct and intelligence. And it is
significant in this regard that just before devoting an entire essay to
Bergson and just after publishing his first book, on Burne, Deleuze
wrote a short introductory essay on "Instincts and Institutions", for
the essay adopts the Bergsonian fralnework of instinct and intelli­
gence, albeit without lnentioning Bergson by nalne, and adopts the

EUGENE W. HOLLAND
focus on institutions characteristic of HUlneJ Instincts and institu­
tions are the two fonns n10bilized to solve the Problem of satisfying
what Deleuze prudently calls "tendencies." Instinctually, satis­
faction is direct and detenninate: the tick drops and lnust obtain
lnalnlnalian blood, or die. In institutions, satisfaction is indirect
and under-detennined: the various lneans or objects of satisfaction
never correspond intrinsically to the tendency. "The saIne sexual
needs will never explain the lnultiple possible forms of lnarriage . .  .
Tendencies are satisfied by lneans that do not depend on them . .  .
[and] no tendency exists which is not at the SaIne tilne constrained or
harassed, and thus transfonl1ed, sublilnated to such an extent that
neurosis is possible" (DI 20). Instincts and institutions fonn a con­
tinuUln: the more perfect and exclusive the lnatch between tendency
and object, the greater the role of an instinct comlnon to the entire
species; the lnore variable the objects of satisfaction, the greater the
role of intelligence rooted in historically and/or geographically spe­
cific tools and institutions (or of neurosis in a particular individ­
ual). Deleuze thus takes Bergson one step further, or provides greater
clarity: even when the sensory-motor gap is bridged by a reflective
detern1ination of what appears "useful/' any such sense of utility for
human being is defined socially and institutionally:
[H]uman utility is always something other than mere utility. The insti­
tution refers us back to a social activity that is constitutive of models of
which we are not conscious, and which are not explained either by tenden­
cies or by utility, since the latter, as human utility, presupposes tendencies
in the first place. (DI 20, translation modified)
So institutions provide socio-historically specific behavioral models
for matching a wide range of objects to tendencies, and these contin­
gent models (which Hurne would call habits) lnay be as unconscious
as those provided by instinct.
We end with Tung, not only because he developed a notion of uncon­
sciousness in direct contradistinction to Freud's, but also because he
derived that notion explicitly from Kant and Bergson. Tung's differ­
ences with Freud are well known; three are particularly important
to Deleuze. First of all, and Inost generally, whereas Freud's under­
standing of psychodynamics was based squarely on neurosis, and
therefore could accomlnodate psychosis only cursorily and with


Deleuze and psychoanalysis
3 I I
great difficulty, Tung's perspective centers on processes of dissoci­
ation, of which neurosis and psychosis are Inilder and severer ver­
sions. This is related to a second, more basic difference, regarding
the very definition of psychic energy or libido. Freud defined libido
exclusively as sexual energy, whereas Tung defined libido as psychic
energy in general, of which sexualized psychic energy is a subset
arising with the sexual instinct in puberty and preceded by other,
pre-sexual libidinal fonns. Tung can thus agree with Freud that
Inany neuroses arise from difficulties with sexuality; but whereas
for Freud psychosis, too, Inust arise as a reaction to sexual anx­
iety (usually a reaction against hOlnosexualityt for Tung psychoses
can involve regression to pre-sexual libidinal stages where Inagical
thinking dissociated frOln reality-testing by the ego prevails.
Finally, and Inost important, is the disagreement over instincts
and their relation to the unconscious. Despite retaining a theory of
instinct throughout his career, Freud fairly quickly came to distin­
guish categorically between the unconscious proper, conceived of as
the result of repression, and an older concept of the /lid" conceived
of as a reservoir of instinctual or biological urges. (Lacan will inain­
tain this distinction even more strenuously than Freud.) His view of
instinct, Ineanwhile, was always a dual or dialectical one, involving
Eros and Thanatos late in his career, self-preservation and sexual
reproduction earlier in his career. While Tung's theory of instinct
resembles Freud's earlier theory, it is not dialectical but evolution­
ary and developinental: instincts evolve in each hUlnan being frOln
a concern for preservation of the organism itself to a concern for the
perpetuation of the species. Moreover, lung's theory is not dualistic
but multiple: there are Inany instincts (not just two), and for Tung
they take the fonn of archetypes; it is here that he draws most dir­
ectly on Bergson and Kant.S
In relation to Bergson, instincts are understood as dispositions
to act in order to satisfy urges, yet these dispositions are always
socio-historically contingent and specific; they cOll1bine instinctual
intuition and institutional intelligence to varying degrees, and are
therefore always to SOlne extent unconscious. But for hUlnan beings,
instincts affect Inore than action itself, according to Tung:
Just as we have been compelled to postulate the concept of an instinct
determining or regulating conscious behavior, so, in order to account for
